<meta>This page continues the discussion of the nature of the Intellect and its relationship to the things it creates and contains, following the Neoplatonic logic established by Proclus.</meta>

THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 46

Therefore, according to its own essence, the intellect defines all things—both those which exist in another according to their cause, and those which exist through participation. For just as individual things are naturally disposed, so they participate in those things that are more excellent <note>Higher spiritual or metaphysical realities.</note> than themselves. But they do not participate in them as those higher things actually are; otherwise, they would be participated in by all things in the same way. Instead, different things participate in different ways. Therefore, participations exist according to the specific property and power of the participants.

Thus, those things that exist before the intellect are contained in the intellect intellectually. Furthermore, the intellect is also—in an intellectual manner—those things that come after it. For the intellect does not arise from its effects, nor does it possess those effects within itself as they are in their own lower state, but rather it contains their causes. Because the intellect is the cause of all things by its very being <term>ipso esse</term> <note>The concept that a divine being creates simply by existing, rather than through a process of labor.</note>, and because its being is intellectual, it follows that it contains the causes of all things intellectually. 

Thus, every intellect possesses all things intellectually: both what is before it and what is after it. Therefore, just as every intellect possesses <term>intelligible things</term> <note>original: "intelligibilia"; things that can be understood by the mind alone.</note> intellectually, so it also possesses <term>sensible things</term> <note>original: "sensibilia"; things perceived through the physical senses.</note> intellectually, etc.

### PROPOSITION 174.

**Every intellect, by the very act of understanding, produces those things that come after it; its act of making is found in its understanding, and its act of understanding is found in its making.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the object of thought and the intellect are the same, and if the being of each thing lies in the act of understanding <term>intellectione</term> that exists within itself, and if it makes what it makes by its very being, and produces according to the being that it is, then it produces its products by the very act of understanding. For being and understanding are both one. 

Indeed, the intellect and every being that is within it are the same. If, therefore, it makes by its very being, and its very being is understanding, it makes by its very act of understanding. The actual act of understanding is found within the process of understanding; this, in turn, is the same as being itself. Moreover, its being is found in its acting—for an agent that acts without moving <note>A reference to the "unmoved mover," a being that causes change without changing itself.</note> always possesses its being in its acting. Therefore, the act of understanding is also in the acting, etc.

### PROPOSITION 175.

**Every intellect is participated in primarily by those things which are intellectual both according to their essence and according to their action, etc.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For it must be that it is participated in either by these or by others which possess an intellectual essence but do not always engage in the act of understanding. But to be participated in by those others is impossible. For the action...

<summary>This page argues that the Divine Intellect contains all things (both higher causes and lower physical effects) in a mental or "intellectual" way, and that for such a being, the act of thinking and the act of creating are the same thing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 176 regarding the relationship between the eternal Intellect and the beings that participate in it, transitioning into Proposition 177 concerning the nature of Intellectual Forms.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI". Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Diadochus" or "Successor" because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens centuries after Plato.</note>

the intellect is immobile <note>In this context, "immobile" means unchanging and eternal, not subject to the flow of time or physical movement.</note>. Therefore, those things that participate in it must always participate in its intellectual activity. These participants are always intellectual themselves. For an activity that occurs only during a specific portion of time cannot be joined to the eternity of the intellect’s activity. Between two extremes, just as with essences, so too with the changes in actions: every action that is completed in a specific time is mediated by an action that possesses perfection through all time. For nowhere do progressions happen immediately; rather, they occur through related and similar steps, both in their substance and in the perfection of their actions. Therefore, every intellect is participated in first by those things that are able to understand through all time, and which do always understand—even if their understanding occurs within the flow of time, rather than being purely eternal <note>Proclus is distinguishing between the "Eternal" (which exists outside of time) and the "Everlasting" (which exists within time but never ends).</note>.

->COROLLARY<-

From this, it is clear that it is impossible for a soul—which understands at some times but not at others—to participate in the Intellect directly. <note>Because the Soul is "intermittent" (sometimes thinking, sometimes not), there must be a middle layer between it and the constantly-thinking Intellect.</note>

## PROPOSITION 177

All intellectual forms are both within one another and yet each exists distinctly by itself.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

If every intellect is indivisible, then the multiplicity within it is united through an intellectual lack of parts. All beings within it will be entirely one and indivisible; they unite with one another and all permeate through all. Yet, because they are all immaterial and incorporeal, they are not confused with one another; each keeps its own purity separately and remains what it is. The distinct participation of individual participants demonstrates this lack of confusion among intellectual forms. For if the things being participated in <term>participated in: the higher reality or "Form" that a lower being shares in or draws its character from</term> were not distinct and separate from one another, then the participants themselves would not participate in them distinctly. Instead, there would be an even greater, indistinct confusion in lower things, which exist in an inferior order. For where would distinction come from, if the things that constitute and perfect them were themselves indistinct and confused? Furthermore, the unity of the forms is attested to by the indivisible substance that contains them,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 176 (likely) and introduces Proposition 177 from Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*. The text focuses on the nature of "Intellectual Forms" and how higher divine intellects differ from lower ones.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 47

...and uniform essence. For those things which have their existence in the indivisible and the uniform, exist in that same thing indivisibly. For in what way could you divide that which is indivisible and one? They exist together, and they permeate through one another, whole through whole, without distance. For there is no distance separating them, nor does that which contains them act as a space where one thing is here and another is elsewhere. Rather, everything exists together in the indivisible and the one; therefore, they are also in one another. Thus, all **intellectual forms** <term>Intellectual forms: the eternal, divine archetypes or "blueprints" of reality existing in the Divine Mind</term> exist both within one another in a state of unity, and yet each remains distinct and separate.

## COROLLARY

If anyone requires examples to supplement these demonstrations, let him observe the **theorems** <term>Theorems: here meaning established intellectual truths or scientific principles</term> that exist within a single soul. Indeed, all of these exist in the same essence—an essence which lacks physical size—and they are united with one another. For the soul does not hold the things within it in a spatial or physical way, but rather indivisibly and without distance; they are simultaneously united and distinct. For the soul brings forth all its truths purely, each one individually, without dragging anything along from the rest. If these truths were not always distinguished according to their **disposition** <term>original: "habitum" — the latent state of knowledge in the soul before it is actively thought about</term>, then the active thinking of the soul would not be able to distinguish them either.

## PROPOSITION 177. <margin>2</margin>

**Every intellect, being a fullness of forms, is comprehensive of either more universal or more particular forms. The superior intellects possess more universally whatever those who come after them possess more particularly. Conversely, the inferior intellects possess more particularly whatever those before them possess more universally.**

### DEMONSTRATION

For the superior intellects employ greater powers, because they are more "one-like" <note>more "uniform" or closer to the absolute unity of the One</note> than the secondary ones. The inferior intellects, being more multiplied or fragmented, diminish the powers they possess. For those things closer to the **One** <note>The "One" is the ultimate, supreme principle in this philosophical system</note>, though they are more "contracted" in terms of quantity, surpass in power those things that follow; and those further from the One are the opposite. Therefore, because the superior intellects possess a greater power but a smaller number of forms, they produce more effects through fewer forms (in terms of quantity) because of the sheer strength of their power. Those who come after them produce fewer effects through a greater number of forms, following the deficiency of their power. If, therefore, those...

<summary>This page explains how divine ideas (forms) exist within the mind without being crowded or confused, using the human soul's knowledge as an analogy. it also establishes that higher divine minds are more powerful because they are simpler and more unified, whereas lower minds are more complex but less powerful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 177 regarding the relationship between higher and lower intellects and their respective forms.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<- <note>original: "Procli Diadochi". Proclus was known as the "Successor" (Diadochus) because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens centuries after Plato.</note> [page 13]

...produce more through fewer [principles], those forms that exist within them are more universal. And if these [lower intellects] produce fewer through more [principles], the forms within them are particular.

->COROLLARY<-
<note>A corollary is a logical consequence that follows naturally from a proven statement.</note>

From these things, it clearly follows that those things generated from higher causes according to a single form are produced separately by secondary causes according to many ideas. Conversely, those things produced from lower causes through many and distinct forms are produced by higher causes through fewer and more universal forms. Indeed, the universal and common qualities come to all participants from above; but the divided and specific qualities come from secondary causes. Hence, secondary intellects, through more particular distinctions of specific traits, in a certain way articulate and refine the formations of the first intellects, etc.

### PROPOSITION 178.

**Every intellectual <term>form|The "forms" or "ideas" are the divine archetypes or blueprints that exist in the divine Mind (Intellect) and shape all reality.</term> is the creator of eternal things.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if every form is everlasting and immobile, it is the cause of things that are immutable in their essence and of eternal substances, but not of those that are generated and corruptible <note>In Neoplatonism, "generated" refers to things in the physical world that come into being and "corruptible" refers to things that eventually decay and die.</note>. Therefore, everything created according to an intellectual form is intellectually eternal. For if a form produces all that follows it by its very existence, and its existence is always in the same state, then the things produced by it will also be in the same state and will be eternal. Therefore, neither those classes of things created by a formal cause at a specific time, nor corruptible things (insofar as they are corruptible), have a pre-existing intellectual form. For if they did, they would be incorruptible and unbegotten, having their substance from those forms, etc.

### PROPOSITION 179.

**Every intellectual <term>number|In this context, "number" refers to a distinct, ordered collection of spiritual beings or intellects.</term> is finite.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if there is another multitude after it, diminished in essence, and thus one is closer to the **One** <note>The "One" is the ultimate, infinite source of all existence in Proclus's system.</note> while the other is more distant...

->Closer<-

<summary>Proclus explains that higher intellectual forms are more universal and produce eternal, immutable effects, whereas secondary causes provide specific, divided traits. He also argues that the number of intellectual beings is finite and limited.</summary>

### THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 48

Indeed, that which is nearer to the **One** <note>The 'One' is the supreme principle in Neoplatonic philosophy, representing absolute simplicity and the source of all existence.</note> is smaller in quantity, while that which is further away is greater. Even the **intellectual number** <note>The total count or group of divine intellects or minds.</note> will be smaller than every multitude that follows after it. Therefore, it is not infinite. Thus, the multitude of **Minds** is limited. For that which is smaller than another is not infinite, since the infinite, insofar as it is infinite, is smaller than nothing.

#### PROPOSITION 180.

**Every intellect is a whole, as if consisting of parts; it is both united with others and distinguished from them.** But the **unparticipated intellect** <term>unparticipated intellect: a transcendent mind that exists independently and does not "share" itself with lower souls</term> is simply universal, containing all parts within itself universally. Each of the **particular intellects**, however, possesses the whole as a part, and thus contains all things particularly. For if it is all things individually, then "individually" is nothing other than "particularly."

#### DEMONSTRATION.

The universe, therefore, exists in each of these particularly, according to some one prevailing particular aspect, determined in all things.

#### PROPOSITION 181.

**Every participated intellect is either divine, as depending upon the Gods, or is merely intellectual.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if there is a divine and unparticipated intellect, that which is primarily akin to it is not something that differs in both respects—namely, by not being divine and by not being unparticipated. For things that are dissimilar in both respects are incapable of being joined to one another. It is clear, therefore, that the middle term is similar to the first intellect in one respect and dissimilar in another. Therefore, it is either unparticipated and not divine, or participated and divine. 

But everything unparticipated is divine, since it has obtained a rank analogous to the **One** within the multitude. Therefore, there will be some intellect that is simultaneously **divine and participated**. But there must also be an intellect that does not participate in the divine **unities** <note>original: "vnitates," referring to the Henads, the highest units of divine being below the One.</note> but is purely an intelligent being. For in every series, the first members are joined to their own unity, while those... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, continuing on the next page.</note>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the 'Diadochus' or 'Successor' because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

...can be closest to those things which are in the order placed above. However, those things which are far from the first unity <note>original: "principe unitate." In this philosophy, the "One" or Unity is the ultimate source of all existence.</note> cannot depend on them. There is, therefore, both a divine intellect and an intellect that is intellectual only; the former consists according to the divine property which it has from its own unity and from that which cannot be shared. The latter, however, exists according to the union which it has received from the shared unity.

### PROPOSITION 182.
Every divine intellect that is participated <term>participated: a technical Neoplatonic term meaning a higher reality shares its essence with a lower one while remaining distinct</term> is participated in by divine souls.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For if the act of participation makes the participant similar to the thing shared and creates a natural connection between them, it is clearly necessary that the soul which shares in a divine mind must be a divine soul. It must be directed toward a divine intellect and, through the medium of the intellect, participate in the very deity <note>original: "deitas"</note> that exists within that intellect. For deity attaches the soul to the intellect as its participant, and binds the divine to the divine.

### PROPOSITION 183.
Every intellect that is participated, yet exists only as "intellectual" <note>This refers to a mind that is rational but not inherently divine.</note>, is participated in by souls that are neither divine nor those which undergo the change between reason and the loss of reason <note>original: "mentis, et amentiae mutatione"</note>.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For divine souls are not of this kind, nor are they merely partakers of the mind. For souls participate in the Gods through the mind, as has been demonstrated previously. Furthermore, these souls are not susceptible to change. For every intellect is participated in by those things which are intellectual according to their essence and their action. Indeed, this is also clear from the preceding arguments.

Every soul is either divine, or is changeable from reason to the lack of reason, or is a mean between these, always remaining in the middle, yet being inferior to divine souls.

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 49<-

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if the divine intellect <note>original: "intellectus." In this context, it refers to the highest form of spiritual or intuitive understanding.</note> is shared by divine souls, and the intellectual mind is shared only by those which are neither divine nor subject to changing from a state of understanding to a state of folly <note>original: "amentia." Proclus is describing a loss of rational or spiritual focus.</note>. However, there are also those souls which do experience this change, being sometimes in a state of understanding and sometimes not. It is clear, then, that there are three classes of souls: first, the divine souls; second, those that follow the divine and always share in the mind; and third, those which change, moving sometimes toward the mind and sometimes toward folly.

->PROPOSITION 185.<-

All divine souls are Gods in a soul-like manner. <note>Proclus means that while they are not the primary "Gods" (who are above all being), they participate so fully in the divine nature that they function as gods within the realm of the Soul.</note> All souls that share in the intellectual mind are always followers of the Gods. However, all those souls that are subject to change are followers of the Gods only sometimes.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if some souls possess the divine light illuminating them from above, while others always exercise their understanding, and others only occasionally obtain this perfection: then the first group, within the vast number of souls, are ranked in a way that is proportionate to the Gods themselves. The second group always follows the Gods, because they always function according to their mind and depend upon the divine souls; they have the same relationship to those divine souls as the "intellectual" has to the "divine." <note>This "intellectual" versus "divine" distinction is a core part of Proclus's ladder of reality.</note> Those souls that only sometimes understand also follow the Gods only sometimes; they do not share in the mind always in the same way, nor can they always turn back <note>original: "conuerti." This refers to the soul's ability to "revert" or look back toward its spiritual source.</note> toward the divine souls. For that which only occasionally achieves intellect can in no way always follow the Gods.

->PROPOSITION 186.<-

Every soul is an incorporeal essence <note><term>essence: "essentia." This refers to the soul's fundamental nature or what it actually IS, independent of its physical body.</term></note> and is separable from the body.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if the soul knows itself, and everything that knows itself "turns back" toward itself, and that which turns toward itself is not a body (since no body is capable of turning back into itself), nor is it [joined] to a body— <note>The text cuts off here, but Proclus is arguing that physical matter cannot be self-aware because it cannot "fold" back on itself in a spiritual sense.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical arguments of Proclus regarding the nature of the soul, specifically focusing on its self-knowledge and its relationship to the body, transitioning into formal propositions about immortality and the essence of life.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

...inseparable from the body. For that which is inseparable from the body is not naturally inclined to turn back upon itself <note>The "return to self" (epistrophe) is a key Neoplatonic concept where a higher being, like the soul, is aware of its own nature and origin.</note>, for it would then be separated from the body by this very action. Therefore, every soul is neither a bodily essence, nor is it inseparable from the body. But that it indeed knows itself is clear. For if it knows the things which are above itself, it is naturally inclined to know itself also; much more so, by knowing itself through the causes which exist before it.

### PROPOSITION 187.
**Every soul is immortal and incorruptible.**

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For everything which can be dissolved and destroyed in any way is either corporeal and composite, or has obtained its existence in a subject <term>subject: in this philosophical context, a "subject" is an underlying thing (like a body) that supports qualities or forms (like color or health).</term>. Indeed, that which is dissolved, as it exists of many parts, is corrupted. But that which is born to exist in another thing, once separated from its subject, vanishes into non-being. But the soul is both incorporeal and exists outside of every subject, existing in itself and turning itself back toward itself; it is thus immortal and incorruptible <note>The original Latin "immortalis est sinu" likely contains a printing error or archaic phrasing; it emphasizes the soul's inherent immortality based on its self-subsisting nature.</note>.

### PROPOSITION 188.
**Every soul is both life and a living thing.**

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For that to which a soul comes necessarily lives, and that which is deprived of a soul is immediately left deprived of life. For it lives either through the soul, or through something else and not through the soul. But to live through something else alone is impossible. For every participated thing <note>A "participated thing" is a fundamental reality (like Beauty or Life) that shares its essence with lower things.</note> gives either itself or something of itself to that which participates in it. If it does neither, it will not be participated in at all. Now, the soul is participated in by that to which it is present, and that which participates in the soul is called "animated" <note>original: "animatum". In Latin, this literally means "endowed with an anima (soul)."</note>. If, therefore, it brings life to animated things, it is either life itself, or a living thing only, or both life and a living thing together. But if it were a living thing only, and no longer life itself, it would surely be composed of life and non-life; therefore it would not know itself, nor turn back upon itself. For life is knowledge, and the cognitive power, insofar as it is such, lives. If, therefore, there is something in the soul that is not living, this part does not have the power of knowing through itself. But if life alone...

<summary>This page presents Propositions 187 and 188 of Proclus' Elements of Theology, arguing that the soul is immortal because it is a self-subsisting entity and that it must be the essence of Life itself to animate the body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's relationship to life and its metaphysical status as a "mean" or bridge between the spiritual and physical realms.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 50

<note>original: "lum est"</note>...it will no longer participate in intellectual life. For that which participates in life is a living thing, and not life alone. For life alone is primary and unparticipated. But those things which come after it are simultaneously living and life. Yet the soul is not an unparticipated life. Therefore, the soul itself is at once both life and a living thing.

## PROPOSITION 189.
**Every soul is self-living.** <note>original: "seu iua," likely a variant of "se viva" or a Latinization of the Greek "autozōos," meaning an entity that possesses life inherently rather than receiving it from an external source.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
If the soul is capable of reverting to itself, and everything that reverts to itself is **self-subsistent** <term>self-subsistent: an entity that exists independently and maintains its own being</term>, then the soul is also self-subsistent and sustains itself. Furthermore, it is both life and a living thing, and its very existence is defined by its vitality. For to whatever things it is present, it communicates life by its very being; and if the recipient is a suitable participant, it immediately becomes animated and living. This happens not by the soul ratiocinating <note>calculating or reasoning through a process</note>, nor by its choosing, nor by its vivifying through deliberate judgment, but by its very being, which bestows life as a gift that can be shared. Therefore, for the soul, "to be" is the same as "to live." If, then, it possesses being from itself, and this being is identical to living, it possesses the act of living by its very essence; thus, it provides life to itself and has it from itself. If this is true, the soul is indeed self-living.

## PROPOSITION 190. <margin>V</margin>
**Every soul is a mean** <note>an intermediate or bridge</note> **between the indivisible things and those divisible things associated with bodies.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
If the soul is self-living and self-subsistent, and possesses an existence that is separable from bodies, it is exempt from and superior to all divisible things located around bodies. For those things are entirely inseparable from their subjects. They are distributed among physical masses, have departed from themselves and their own indivisibility, and are stretched out alongside bodies. Even if they exist within "lives" <note>referring to the lower forms of biological life</note>, they are not the masters of their own lives, but belong to the things that participate in them. Even if they exist in essence or in forms, they are not their own forms, but the forms of the things they have shaped. 

But if the soul is not merely these things, it is an essence that is self-subsistent, a life that is self-living, and a knowledge that knows itself, and is separable from bodies in all these respects. However, the soul also participates in life...

<summary>This page argues that the soul is "self-living," meaning it generates its own vitality rather than just receiving it, and establishes the soul as a middle ground between eternal spiritual realities and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>soul, self-living, self-subsistent, indivisible, divisible, vitality, Neoplatonism, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of a proof regarding the soul's intermediate nature (likely Proposition 190) before introducing Proposition 191 of Proclus's "Elements of Theology."</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Diadochus" or successor to Plato’s Academy in Athens; this text is a foundational work of Neoplatonic philosophy.</note>

If indeed this is the case, and the soul participates in **being**, yet also participates in **knowledge** derived from other causes, it is surely clear that it is inferior to those things that are indivisible. This is because its life and essence are filled from elsewhere—and if this is true of its life, it is clearly true of its essence. For prior to life, there is both *unparticipated life* and *unparticipated essence*. <note>In Proclus's system, "unparticipated" (imparticipabilis) refers to a pure, transcendent form that exists before it is "shared" or "participated in" by lower levels of reality.</note> It is also clear that the capacity to know is a primary characteristic. Insofar as it is a soul, every soul possesses life, but not every soul possesses knowledge simply by virtue of being a soul. Indeed, a certain soul may remain a soul while being ignorant of true beings. Therefore, a soul is not "a knowing thing" in its primary state, nor is its knowledge identical to its very existence. It therefore possesses a secondary essence, derived from those things which are "knowing things" primarily and by their very existence. Since the soul's existence is divided from its knowledge, the soul is not among the indivisible things. Yet it has already been demonstrated that it is not among those divisible things associated with bodies. Therefore, it is intermediate between both.

## PROPOSITION 191

**Every participable soul possesses an eternal essence, but an activity that occurs in time.** <note>A "participable" soul is one that is capable of being "shared" by a physical body, acting as its life-force.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION

For a soul must have either both [essence and activity] eternally, or both in time, or one eternally and the other in time. 

But it cannot have both eternally; for if it did, its essence would be indivisible and the nature of the soul would not differ at all from an intellectual substance—that is, from an immobile substance—whereas the soul is by its nature self-moving. <note>"Intellectual substance" refers to the realm of *Nous* (Divine Mind), which is entirely outside of time and change.</note> 

Nor can it have both in time; for if it did, it would be a "generated" thing only, neither eternal nor self-subsisting. <term>self-subsisting (per se substans): A being that creates and maintains its own existence rather than being caused by something else.</term> For nothing that is measured by time is self-subsisting according to its essence. However, it is established that the soul is self-subsisting. For that which reverts to itself in its activity also reverts to itself according to its essence, and proceeds from itself. <note>"Reversion" is the Neoplatonic idea that a higher soul can look inward and return to its divine source through its own power.</note>

It remains, therefore, that every soul is eternal in one respect, but a participant in time in another respect. Either it is eternal according to essence but a participant in time according to its activity, or vice-versa. But the latter is impossible. Therefore, every participable soul has been allotted an eternal essence, but an activity that functions according to time.

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the soul's intermediate status and presents Proposition 191, which argues that while the soul's core existence is eternal, its thoughts and actions unfold sequentially within time.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, soul, eternity, time, essence, activity, self-subsistence, participable soul, intellectual substance</keywords>

->THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 51<-

## PROPOSITION 192.
**Every <term>participable soul</term> <note>A 'participable' soul is one that can be shared or 'participated in' by a lower body, as opposed to an 'unparticipated' soul which remains purely in the divine realm.</note> is always among the <term>beings</term> <note>In this philosophy, 'beings' (ontos onta) are eternal, unchanging realities, distinct from 'becoming' things which change and decay.</note>, and is the first of things <term>generated</term> <note>Generated things are those that exist or act within the flow of time.</note>.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if a soul is eternal according to its <term>essence</term> <note>Its inner nature or 'what it is'.</note>, it is a <term>true being</term> <note>original: "enter ens"</note> according to its existence, and is an "always-being." For that which partakes in eternity also partakes in "always being." If, however, according to its **action** it exists in time, it is "generated." 

For everything that partakes in time is always "becoming" according to the "before" and "after" of time; it is not all at once what it is, but is entirely "generated." If, then, every soul is in some sense generated according to its action, it must be the **first** of generated things. For that which is entirely generated is further removed from the eternal things <note>Proclus argues that the Soul acts as a bridge: its nature is eternal, but its thoughts and movements happen one after another in time.</note>.

## PROPOSITION 193.
**Every soul exists <term>proximately</term> <note>Directly, without any intermediate layers in the cosmic hierarchy.</note> from the <term>Intellect</term> <note>The 'Intellect' (Greek: *Nous*) is the realm of pure, divine thought that sits above the Soul.</note>.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if a soul has an immutable and eternal essence, it <term>proceeds</term> <note>Neoplatonists used the term 'procession' to describe how lower levels of reality flow out from higher ones like light from a sun.</note> from an immobile essence. For whatever proceeds from a mobile <note>Moving or changing.</note> essence is itself entirely changed according to its essence. Therefore, the cause of every soul is immobile. 

If, truly, the soul is perfected proximately by the Intellect, it also <term>turns back</term> <note>The 'reversion' or 'return' where a thing seeks its source to find its purpose and fulfillment.</note> toward the Intellect and partakes in the knowledge which the Intellect bestows on those things that partake <note>The text ends here mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>Proclus explains that the Soul occupies a middle ground in the universe: it is eternal in its nature but acts within time, and it derives its existence directly from the Divine Intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Soul, Intellect, Eternity, Time, Essence, Procession, Reversion, Neoplatonism, Proclus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*, specifically concluding a discussion on the soul's relationship to intellectual forms and beginning Propositions 195 and 196. The text explores the soul as a "middle ground" in the cosmic hierarchy.</meta>

-># PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI"; Diadochus was a title given to the heads of the Platonic Academy in Athens, meaning "Successor" to Plato.</note>

...it produces; it will also give to the soul established by it the essential <term>principles (rationes): also known as "logoi," these are the formative blueprints or rational structures of things</term> of all things within itself. For everything that acts by its very being—which is its primary state—grants that same quality to its product in a secondary state. Therefore, the soul possesses the images of intellectual forms in a secondary manner, etc.

## PROPOSITION 195.
**Every soul is all things: it contains sensible things as their primary model, but it contains intelligible things as their image.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For because the soul exists as the middle ground between <term>indivisible things (impartibilium): entities like pure intellects that cannot be broken into parts</term> and the things that are <term>divided among bodies (partibilium): physical things that occupy space and can be broken down</term>, it produces and creates the latter, while it looks to the causes of the former which exist before it and from which it proceeds. Therefore, the soul possesses the models of those things for which it stands as a cause. But it possesses through participation—as offshoots of those primary beings—the things from which it was established. 

Thus, the soul has received all sensible things through their cause; it possesses the <term>immaterial (immateriales)</term> principles of material things, the incorporeal principles of corporeal things, and the non-spatial principles of things that are separated by distance. Conversely, it has received <term>intelligible things (intelligibilia): realities known by the mind rather than the senses</term> as an image; it receives the indivisible in a divided way, the unified in a manifold way, and the immobile in a self-moving way. Therefore, the soul is all existing things: it is those primary things by participation, and it is those things that come after it by acting as their model, etc.

## PROPOSITION 196.
**Every soul that can be participated in uses an eternal body as its first vehicle, possessing a substance that is neither created nor destroyed.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if every soul is eternal in its essence, and if it animates a body by its very being in a primary way, then it must animate that body forever. For the "Being" of every soul is unchangeable. If this is true, then that which is animated is *always* animated, and always shares in life. Furthermore, that which always lives must, by a much greater necessity, always exist. And that which always exists is eternal. Therefore, the body which is animated first, and which hangs primarily from each soul, is eternal. But indeed, every <term>participable soul (participabilis anima): a soul that is capable of being "shared" by or linked to a physical form</term> is shared by some body in a primary way...

<summary>Proclus argues that the soul serves as a bridge between the spiritual and physical worlds, containing the blueprints for the material world and reflections of the divine world. He also posits that every soul is permanently attached to an "eternal body" or celestial vehicle.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Soul, Intelligible, Sensible, Participation, Eternal Body, Elements of Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Proposition 196 regarding the relationship between the Soul and the eternal body it inhabits, moving into the internal composition of the Soul's nature (Proposition 197) and its relationship to time and cycles (Proposition 198).</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 52<-

since it is indeed both <term>participable</term> <note>original: "participabilis"; in Neoplatonic thought, a 'participable' entity is one that can be shared or 'participated in' by a lower level of reality, whereas an 'imparticipable' entity remains transcendent and unshared.</note>, but not imparticipable, and animates the participant by its very being. Therefore, every <term>participated soul</term> <note>A soul that is linked to a specific body.</note> uses a first body that is eternal, unbegotten, and incorruptible according to its essence.

### PROPOSITION 197.
**E**very soul is a vital and cognitive essence, and an essential and cognitive life, and a cognition, essence, and life; and all these things exist together within it—the essential, the vital, and the cognitive—and all are in all, yet each is also distinct.

### PROOF.
**F**or if the soul is the middle ground between the <term>indivisible things</term> <note>referring to the pure Intellects above the soul</note> and the forms that are divided around the body, it is neither as indivisible as all intellectual things, nor as divisible as the forms of bodies. Since, therefore, in bodily things, essences, lives, and cognitions are divided—for the body does not possess all these things simultaneously in the same way—in souls these things exist indivisibly, unitedly, incorporeally, and all together, due to their immateriality and indivisibility. Yet, because in intellectual things all exist through a pure union, in souls they are more distinct and partitioned. Therefore, they are all together and yet separate.

If they are indeed all together and indivisible in one, they permeate each other; and if they are separate, they are divided, yet again without confusion. Thus, each exists in itself, and all exist in all. For life and cognition are present in the essence. If they were not, the soul would not know itself at all, if its essence were deprived of life and cognition. Likewise, essence and cognition are present in the life. For a mindless life without cognition belongs to the flaws of material things, which can neither know themselves nor are they pure essences. And a cognition without essence and life is non-subsistent. For every cognition belongs to a living being and has obtained an essence for itself.

### PROPOSITION 198.
**E**verything that participates in time and is always in motion is measured by periods <note>original: "periodis"; refers to cyclical or recurring measurements of time, like the orbits of planets or life cycles.</note>.

### PROOF.
**B**ecause it is indeed measured by time, the motion participates in both movement and a limit, and it proceeds by number. But because it is always in motion, and this "always" is not eternal... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page defines the Soul as a middle existence that balances unity and diversity, containing its essence, life, and knowledge simultaneously. It also introduces the idea that things in constant motion within time must be governed by cyclical periods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 198 regarding the nature of temporal motion and begins Propositions 199 and 200 of Proclus's "Elements of Theology." This section explores how the "world-soul" (the animating principle of the universe) moves in cycles rather than in a straight line.</meta>

-># PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. He was known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) to Plato.</note>

...but temporal, it is necessary to employ periods <term>period: a "periodus" is a recurring cycle or circuit of time</term>. For motion is a certain change from some states into others. Beings, however, are limited in their numbers and their magnitudes. Since these are limited, motion cannot be infinite in a straight line; nor can that which moves forever proceed in a finite, linear way. Therefore, that which is in constant motion will return from the same points to the same points again, and thus it will create a cycle.

### PROPOSITION 199.

**Every world-soul uses cycles in its own life, and returns to its starting point.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if a soul is measured by time and operates transitively <note>original: *transitiue*; meaning it acts step-by-step or through a sequence of stages rather than all at once</note>, and this is its own proper motion; and if everything that moves and participates in time while being eternal in essence uses cycles, revolves periodically, and returns from the same points to the same points—then certainly every world-soul, having motion and acting according to time, will also have circuits of motions and returns. For every cycle belongs to eternal things and involves a return.

### PROPOSITION 200.

**Every cycle of a soul is measured by time. But while the cycles of other souls are measured by a specific duration of time, the cycle of the First Soul, being measured by time, is measured by the entirety of time.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if all motions have a "before" and an "after," then cycles must also have them; for that reason, they participate in time, and time is what measures all the cycles of souls. But if the cycles of all souls were the same and revolved around the same things, then time would surely be the same for all. However, if the "returns" <term>return: the point at which a soul completes its cycle and begins its journey again</term> of different souls are different, then the periodic time of those returns must also be different. 

It is clear that the soul which is first measured by time is measured by the entirety of time. For if time is the measure of all motion, that which is moved first will be a complete participant in time and will be measured by the whole of it. For if the whole of time were not the measure of that which first participates in it, time would not measure anything else in its totality. But since all other 
                                            souls <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the movement of the "world-soul" must be cyclical rather than linear because the universe is finite. It establishes that while individual souls have their own specific time-cycles, the "First Soul" is measured by the totality of time itself.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, World-Soul, Neoplatonism, temporal motion, cycles, return, eternity, First Soul, Elements of Theology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 200 regarding the relationship between souls and time, before transitioning into Proposition 201 and 202 concerning the nature and activities of divine souls.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 53<-

That particular souls are measured by more specific measures of universal time is clear from these facts. For if they are more particular than the soul which first participates in time, they will not yet adapt their cycles to universal time. Instead, many of their own "returns" <note>original: *reditus*; referring to the completion of a soul's circular journey or cycle of reincarnation</note> will be merely parts of the single period and return made by the soul that first participates in time. For the participation of a lesser power is more particular, while that of a greater power is more universal. Therefore, other souls are not naturally suited to receive the entire temporal measure through a single life, since they have been allotted a lower order, measured by primary time, than those which have been assigned an even lower rank.

### PROPOSITION 201.

All divine souls possess three types of activities: some as souls, others as those that have received a divine intellect, and others as depending upon the Gods. They exercise **providence** <note>In this context, providence refers to the divine care and management of the lower universe</note> over all things as Gods; they know all things through an **intellectual life**; and they move bodies through a **self-moving essence** <term>self-moving essence: the Neoplatonic idea that the soul is the primary source of its own motion, unlike physical objects which must be moved by something else</term>.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Because these souls naturally participate in "super-mundane" <note>existing beyond the physical world</note> realities and are not simply souls, but **divine souls**, they occupy a rank within the breadth of the soul-realm that is analogous to the Gods. Consequently, they do not act only in the manner of souls, but also divinely, having been allotted the deified summit of their essence. Furthermore, because they possess an intellectual substance, by which they are ranked beneath the purely intellectual essences, they act not only divinely but also intellectually—one activity according to the "One" <note>original: *unum*; the highest, most unified part of the soul that connects directly to the divine</note> that is within them, and another that is established according to the intellect. 

A third activity is present in them according to their own specific existence: they act as the mover of those things which are by nature moved by another, and as the life-giver to those things which possess a life that is "brought in" <note>original: *inductibilem*; meaning life that is not inherent but provided by the soul</note> from the outside. For this is the proper operation of every soul, while the other activities—such as understanding and providing—belong to it through participation.

### PROPOSITION 202.

All souls that are **attendants** of the Gods, and who always follow the Gods, are indeed inferior to divine souls, yet they remain superior to particular souls.

<summary>This page defines the triple nature of divine souls—acting as gods, intellects, and movers—and establishes their place in the cosmic hierarchy above ordinary, particular souls.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the hierarchy of souls from Proclus's Elements of Theology, specifically focusing on the relationship between divine, intermediate, and particular souls.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "Proclus Diadochus." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the head of the Neoplatonic Academy in Athens; "Successor" refers to his position as a successor to Plato.</note>

## PROOF.

Divine souls participate in both the mind and in <term>Deity</term><note>The state of being divine or the nature of God.</note>. Therefore, they are at once intellectual and divine, and they preside over other souls, just as the Gods preside over all beings. However, <term>particular souls</term><note>Individual souls, such as those belonging to humans, which are distinct from the universal or divine souls.</note> are deprived of a direct path to the intellect, being unable to participate directly in the divine essence. For they would not fall away from intellectual activity if they participated in the intellect by their very essence, as was demonstrated previously. Therefore, those souls which always follow the Gods are intermediate; they participate in a perfect mind and thereby exceed the particular souls, yet they are not joined to the <term>divine unities</term><note>Also known as "Henads," these are the highest principles of unity in Neoplatonic thought, existing just below the One.</note>, for the intellect that is participated in by them is not divine.

### PROPOSITION 203.

In the whole multitude of souls, the divine souls—being greater than the others in power—are concentrated in number. Those which always follow the Gods <note>The Latin text reads "follow themselves," but the context and the original Greek indicate "follow the Gods."</note> hold the middle rank in all things, both in power and in quantity. Particular souls are indeed inferior in power to the others, but they have proceeded into a much greater number.

## PROOF.

For the divine souls are more akin to <term>the One</term><note>The ultimate, absolute source of all reality in Neoplatonism.</note> because of their divine essence; the middle souls are akin to it through their participation in the mind. These last ones in the order of things are different in essence from both the middle and the first souls. In the realm of eternal things, those that are closer to the One are more unified in number than those which are further away, and their "multitude" is more restricted. Those further away are more multiplied. Furthermore, the powers of the superior beings are greater; they hold the same ratio to the second rank as the divine does to the intellectual, and as the intellectual does to the soulful. Thus, the total number of inferior beings is larger. For that which is further from the One is more of a "multitude," and that which is closer is less of a "multitude."

### PROPOSITION 204.

Every divine soul presides over many souls that always follow the Gods, and over even more of those souls which only occasionally take up this rank.

## PROO—
<note>Text cuts off at the start of the next proof.</note>

<summary>This page outlines Proclus's hierarchy of souls, explaining that divine souls are few but powerful, while human (particular) souls are numerous but weaker, based on their proximity to the ultimate "One."</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Divine Souls, Particular Souls, The One, Hierarchy of Being, Henads, Intellectual Essence</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of souls from the previous section, specifically focusing on how divine souls lead various classes of subordinate souls and the relationship between a soul and its 'vehicle' or subtle body.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 54<-

->**DEMONSTRATION.**<-

For if a soul is divine, it is necessary that all things receive a generative order from it, and it must hold a primary-productive <note>original Latin: *primificum*; refers to the soul's ability to first bring other souls into being</note> status among souls. For that which is divine presides over all beings in every respect; and not only over those things which follow it only at certain times. For if it were to preside only over those things that follow it occasionally, how would there be a point of contact between these and the divine soul? Especially since they are fundamentally different, and do not participate <note>In Neoplatonism, 'participation' is the process where a lower being shares in the qualities of a higher being without the higher being losing anything</note> in intellect directly, much less in the Gods. If, however, the divine soul always presides over those things that follow it, how has the series proceeded all the way down to those temporary followers? For if that were the case, the intellectual beings themselves would be the very last, and they would be sterile, neither born to bring anything else to perfection nor to lead anything back to its source. It is therefore necessary that from every divine soul there first depend those souls that follow it perpetually, which operate through intellect and are led back to intellect; these are more particular than the divine intellects themselves. Second, there are the particular souls, which through these intermediate souls participate in Mind and are capable of a divine life. For it is through those beings that always participate that the more excellent part reaches those things that participate only sometimes, bringing them to perfection. And again, for every one divine soul, there are more souls that follow it occasionally than those that follow it eternally. For the power of Unity, as it is released into a multitude, always increases in number. Its power may decrease as it descends, but it surpasses in sheer quantity. Since each soul among those that always follow the Gods also presides over many particular souls, it imitates the divine soul; it draws many souls into the primary-productive unity of the entire series. Every divine soul, therefore, presides over many souls of the kind that always follow the gods, and over even more of those that only occasionally attain this rank.

->**PROPOSITION CCV.**<-

**Every particular soul holds the same relation to the soul under which it is ordered according to its essence, as its <term>vehicle</term> <note>original Latin: *vehiculum*; in Neoplatonic thought, this is the 'astral body' or 'chariot'—a subtle, immortal envelope that carries the soul through the heavens</note> holds to the vehicle of that superior soul.**

->**DEMONSTRATION.**<-

For if the distribution of vehicles occurs according to the nature of each soul, it is necessary that the vehicle of every particular soul holds the same relation to the vehicle of the universal soul as the soul itself holds to that higher soul.

<summary>This page describes the cascading hierarchy of souls, where a single divine soul governs both eternal intellectual souls and a larger number of temporary particular souls. It also establishes that the 'vehicle' (the subtle body) of a soul reflects the status and rank of the soul it carries.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, divine soul, participation, vehicle of the soul, intellectual souls, hierarchy of being, emanation</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between souls and their bodies, specifically focusing on the cycles of reincarnation and the nature of the "astral vehicle" or celestial body.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...it to that. But the distribution is according to nature. For first, those things that participate are joined by their very nature to those things in which they participate. If, therefore, just as the divine <note>referring to a divine soul</note> relates to the divine body, so the particular soul relates to the particular body, then both exist by the very nature of the participant; and what was said from the beginning is true: that the vehicles also have the same relationship to one another as the souls do.

## PROPOSITION 206.

**Every particular soul, while descending into generation infinitely, is also able to ascend from generation toward Being.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the soul sometimes follows the Gods, and at other times falls away from that divine intensity—sharing in both intellect and the lack of it—it is clearly evident that it exists alternately in the realm of <term>generation</term><note>The realm of "generation" refers to the physical world of birth, death, and constant change, as opposed to the eternal realm of Being.</note> and among the Gods. Nor, on the other hand, will it remain in bodies for all time. For that which has no temporal beginning will never have an end, and that which has no end must necessarily have no beginning. It remains, therefore, for each soul to complete cycles: of ascents out of generation, and of descents into generation; and this process is unceasing throughout infinite time. Therefore, every particular soul is able to descend and ascend infinitely, and this will not cease; this experience will occur for all souls.

## PROPOSITION 207.

**The vehicle of every particular soul is produced by an immovable cause.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it hangs eternally from the soul that uses it, and exists as something natively immutable in its essence, then it has received its substance from an immovable cause. For everything produced by moving <note>or "changing"</note> causes is itself entirely changed in its essence. But every soul has an eternal body <note>original: "vehiculum," the subtle or "astral" body that carries the soul</note> which is the primary thing participating in that soul. Therefore, the particular soul—and thus the cause of its vehicle—is immovable, and because of this, it is supramundane <note>existing above or beyond the physical world</note>.

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus’s Neoplatonic theology, specifically focusing on the "vehicle" of the soul, a concept central to late antique philosophy regarding how a spiritual soul interacts with a physical body.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 55

### PROPOSITION 208.

Every particular soul's vehicle is immaterial, indivisible in its essence, and impassible. <note>original: "impaſsibile" – in this context, it means "incapable of suffering" or "unaffected by external change."</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.

If this vehicle proceeds from an immovable craftsmanship <note>The "immovable craftsmanship" refers to the Divine Demiurge or Creator, who creates without being changed by the act.</note> and is eternal, it must possess an immaterial and impassible substance. For those things that are naturally inclined to suffer according to their essence are subject to change; they are entirely material, and existing in various states at different times, they depend upon moving causes. Consequently, they undergo every kind of transformation, being moved by their own primary causes. But it is also clear that the vehicle must be indivisible. For everything that is divided is corrupted by that very division, as it departs from the whole and from its own continuity. If, therefore, the vehicle is immutable in its essence and impassible, it must also be indivisible.

### PROPOSITION 209.

Every particular soul’s vehicle descends by the addition of more material **tunics**. <note>The "tunics" (tunicarum) are metaphorical layers or "garments" of matter that the soul puts on as it descends from the heavens into the physical world.</note> However, it is restored to the soul itself by the removal of every material thing, and by returning to its own proper form in proportion to the soul that uses it.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For indeed, the soul descends while taking on <term>irrational lives</term> <note>These are lower forms of life or impulses, such as basic appetites or emotions, that are not guided by reason.</note>; but it ascends by casting off all the powers of <term>generation</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, "generation" refers to the cycle of physical birth and the changing world of matter.</note> which it wrapped around itself during its descent. Having become <unclear>† † † †</unclear>, it imitates the lives of the souls that use it and is moved together with them, wherever they move. It represents the thoughts of some through its own circular motions; it represents the falls of others through their impulses toward generation; and it represents the purifications of others by leading them back into the immaterial. Since it is given life by the very existence of the souls and is innate to them, it is changed in every way alongside their changes; it follows them in every direction, suffers with those that suffer, returns together with those that are purified, and is raised up together with those that are led back, seeking its own perfection. For everything is perfected when it attains its own proper wholeness.

->P 2<-

<summary>Proclus argues that the soul's "vehicle" (a subtle, spiritual body) is essentially eternal and indivisible, though it takes on material layers as the soul descends into the physical world. This vehicle acts as a mirror to the soul's state, descending or ascending alongside the soul's moral and intellectual progress.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Elements of Theology, vehicle of the soul, ochema, immateriality, tunics of matter, generation, purification, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the "Elements of Theology" by Proclus, specifically focusing on the nature of the soul's vehicle and the soul's descent into the physical world. It follows the previous propositions regarding the soul's relationship to the divine and the intellect.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI". Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Diadochus" or successor to Plato's Academy in Athens.</note>

### PROPOSITION 210.

**E**very innate <term>vehicle (Latin: *vehiculum*). In Neoplatonic philosophy, this is the "astral body" or "spirit-body" (ochema) that serves as the intermediary between the immaterial soul and the physical body.</term> of the soul always maintains the same shape and size; however, it appears larger or smaller, or even of a different shape, through the addition and removal of other bodies.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if it derives its essence from an immovable cause, it is clear that it also receives its shape and size from that cause as something fixed, and both are immutable and unchangeable. Yet at various times it appears differently—both larger and smaller. Therefore, it is through other bodies, added from material elements and then removed again, that it appears to be of this or that shape, or of this or that size. <note>Proclus argues that while our physical appearance changes as we consume food or age, the soul's "luminous body" remains constant in its divine geometry.</note>

### PROPOSITION 211.

**E**very particular soul descending into generation <note>The "realm of generation" is the physical world characterized by birth, change, and decay.</note> descends in its entirety. It is not the case that one part of it remains above while another part descends.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or if some part of the soul remained in the intelligible <note>The "intelligible" realm is the world of pure, divine forms and eternal truths.</note> realm, it would think either intransitively <note>Thinking all things simultaneously and eternally.</note> or transitively <note>Discursive, step-by-step reasoning.</note>. But if it thought intransitively, it would be Intellect itself rather than a part of the soul, and the soul would merely be a direct participant in Mind. If, however, it thought transitively, then a single essence would be composed of both an "always-thinking" part and a "sometimes-thinking" part. But this is impossible, for these two modes of being are distinct, as has been shown elsewhere. Furthermore, it would be absurd if the highest part of the soul, being always perfect, did not govern the other powers and make them perfect. Therefore, every soul descends in its entirety. <note>This is a famous point of disagreement: Plotinus believed a part of the human soul stayed in the divine realm, but Proclus argues we come down completely into the human experience.</note>

->THE END.<-

[Woodcut illustration of a face or mask with decorative elements]

<summary>This page presents the final two propositions of Proclus's work, arguing that the soul's spiritual "vehicle" is unchanging in its true form and that the human soul descends fully into the physical world rather than leaving a part behind in the heavens.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, soul's vehicle, astral body, descent of the soul, generation, intelligible realm, transitive thought, ochema</keywords>

# THE SAME PROCLUS’
# ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS.
### D E F I N I T I O N S.

CONTINUOUS things are those whose boundaries <term>termini</term> <note>In ancient physics, the "limit" or "boundary" is what defines where one thing ends and another begins.</note> are one and the same.
Contiguous things are those whose boundaries are together.
Successive things are those between which there is nothing of the same kind.
The primary time of a motion is that which is neither greater nor less than the motion itself.
The primary place is that which is neither larger nor smaller than the body located within it.
A thing at rest is that which, both as a whole and in its parts, is in the same place at an earlier and a later time.

### T H E O R E M  I.
**T**wo things lacking parts will not touch one another.

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
For if it were possible, let two indivisible things <term>A and B</term> <note>Proclus uses letters to represent abstract points or units, following the style of Greek geometry.</note> touch one another. However, contiguous things were defined as those whose boundaries are together and in the same place. Therefore, these two indivisible things would have boundaries. Thus, A and B would not be indivisible <note>An "indivisible" by definition has no parts, and therefore cannot have a distinct "edge" or "boundary" separate from its center.</note>.

### T H E O R E M  II.
**T**wo indivisible things will not create a continuum.

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.
For if it were possible, let there be two indivisible things A and B, and let them form a continuum from both. But all continuous things must first touch one another. Therefore, A and B would touch each other even though they are indivisible, which is impossible <note>This refers back to the proof in Theorem I, which established that indivisibles cannot touch.</note>.

->PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-
->ANOTHER WAY.<-

**I**f from A and B <term>indivisibles</term> <note>original: "impartibilibus"; things that have no parts and cannot be divided, such as mathematical points.</note>, a continuum is formed, either the whole of A touches the whole of B; or the whole of A touches a part of B; or a part of A touches a part of B. But if a whole touches a part, or a part touches a part, A and B would not be indivisibles <note>because a "part" implies they can be broken down further</note>. If, however, the whole touches the whole, they will not form a continuum, but will only be <term>superimposed</term> <note>original: "adaptabuntur"; meaning they coincide or occupy the same space perfectly</note>. If, therefore, A is not a continuum, then B is not a continuum with A while the whole touches the whole.

->THEOREM III.<-
**O**f indivisibles that are within a continuum, that which lies between both is itself a continuum.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
**L**et there be two indivisibles A and B. I say that that which is between A and B is a continuum. If it were not so, then the indivisible A would touch the indivisible B, which is impossible <note>In this geometric logic, two points cannot "touch" to create a line; there must be space between them</note>. Therefore, the medium between them is a continuum.

->THEOREM IIII.<-
**T**wo indivisibles are not <term>successive</term> <note>original: "deinceps"; meaning "next in order" or "consecutive" without any other item of the same kind in between</note> to one another.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
**F**or let there be two indivisibles A and B. I say that A will not be successive to B. Since it has been demonstrated that between two indivisibles the medium is a continuum, let the medium between them be CD, and let it be divided at point E. Therefore, E is an indivisible, and it is a medium between A and B. But "successive" things are those between which there is no intermediate of the same kind. Therefore, A and B are not successive to one another.

### THEOREM VI.

IF any magnitude consists of indivisibles, the motion that occurs over it will also consist of indivisibles.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

LET magnitude **A. B. C.** be composed of the indivisibles **A**, **B**, and **C**. I say that the motion performed over magnitude **A.B.C.** will also be composed of indivisibles.

For let the motion **D.E.F.** be taken over it, and let this be the motion. Let the moving object be **G**; let **G** be moved according to **D** over **A**, and according to **E** over **B**, and according to **F** over **C**. Now, this **D** is either indivisible or divisible. Let it be, if possible, divisible, and let it be divided into two parts. Then **G** is moved through the half before the whole; but it is moved over **A**, therefore **A** would also be divisible. But it was [previously defined as] indivisible. Therefore **D** is also indivisible. Similarly, we shall clearly show that **E** and **F** are also indivisible, etc.

### THEOREM VII.

IF motion consists of indivisibles, the time of that motion will also consist of indivisibles.

I say that **D**, **E**, and **F** are themselves indivisible. For if **D** (in which **A** is carried) is divisible, let it be divided. Therefore, in half the time, a part [of the motion] is moved, and not the whole of **A**. Therefore, the motion **A** would itself be divisible. ->### THEOREM 8.<-

Of those things which move with unequal speed, that which is faster moves through a greater distance in an equal amount of time.

->### PROOF.<-

For let there be two things moving with unequal speed: let **A** be the faster and **B** the slower. Let **A** move from point **C** to point **D** in the time interval **FG**. Since **B** is slower, it will not arrive from **C** to **D** in the same time interval **FG**. For the "faster" is defined as that which arrives at the end earlier, while the "slower" is that which arrives later.

Therefore, let it [the slower object **B**] move during the time interval **FG** in such a way that it reaches point **E**. Thus, in the same time, **A** has moved through the distance **CD**, and **B** has moved through **CE**. However, the distance **CD** is greater than **CE**. Therefore, the faster [object] moves through a greater distance in the same amount of time.

->### THEOREM 9.<-

If things of unequal speed are moved, certain times can be taken—specifically, a greater time for the slower object and a lesser time for the faster—in which the faster object will move through a greater distance, and the slower object through a lesser distance.
<note>This theorem explores the non-intuitive relationship where we compare a fast object over a short time against a slow object over a long time.</note>

->### PROOF.<-

Let **A** and **B** be of unequal speed, where **A** is the faster and **B** the slower. Since the faster moves further in the same time, let **A** move the distance **CD** in time **FG**, and let **B** move the distance **CO** [in that same time]. Since **A** moved the distance **CD** in the whole time **FG**, it follows that it will move the [shorter] distance **CO** in a time shorter than **FG**.

Therefore, let a shorter time be taken and designated as **FK**. Since **A** has moved through the distance **CE** <note>The text uses various letters for points on a line to represent magnitudes of distance.</note> in the time **FK**, and **B** has moved through the distance **CE** in the [longer] time **FG**—and since the distance **CO** is greater than **CE**, and the time **FG** is greater than **FK**—we have shown the conditions in which **A** moved the greater distance **CO** [in time **FG**] and **B** moved the lesser distance **CE** [in time **FG**]. <note>The proof demonstrates that by manipulating the intervals of observation, one can always find a greater time for the slower and a lesser for the faster that preserves the ratio of their speeds.</note> Which is what was required to be done.

->### THEO-<-

### PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 38

->**THEOREM X.**<-

**Of those things which move with unequal velocity, the faster one passes through an equal distance in less time.**

->**DEMONSTRATION**<-

**Let there be** two objects moved at unequal <term>velocity<note>original: "velocitate." In this context, it refers to the rate of speed.</note></term>; let A move faster than B. Let A move through the <term>distance<note>original: "spacium." While literally "space," in these proofs it specifically refers to the distance or interval traveled.</note></term> CD in time FG, while B moves through a smaller distance CE in that same time. Since in the whole time FG, A has moved through distance CD, it will move through the smaller distance CE in a shorter time. Let it move in time GK. Now, B was moving through distance CE in time FG. Since time FG is greater than time GK, it follows that A moves through the equal distance CE in less time, and B in more time.

->**ANOTHER WAY**<-

**Let A be** faster than B, and let B move through distance CE in time FG. Certainly, A moves through distance CE either in the same time, in more time, or in less. But if it were in the same time, the velocities would be equal. If it were in more time, it would be slower. But it was assumed to be faster. Therefore, A will move through distance CE in less time, <term>which was to be demonstrated<note>original: "quod oportuit demonstrare." The Latin equivalent of the Greek "hoper edei deixai" (Q.E.D.).</note></term>.

->**THEOREM XI.**<-

**Every unit of time is infinitely divisible, as is every magnitude and every motion.**

->**DEMONSTRATION**<-

**Let A** be faster than B, and let this slower B move through distance CD in time FG. Since it has been demonstrated that the faster object crosses an equal distance in less time, A will cross the same distance CD in a shorter time than FG; thus, this time FG will be divisible. Let it be divided at point H. Furthermore, since A crosses a greater distance in an equal time (which has likewise been demonstrated), and the slower object moves through a smaller distance, the distance CD will therefore be divided. Let it be divided at point K. Again, because B crosses distance CK in time FH, A will cross that same distance in even less time, as has been demonstrated. Therefore, it will divide the time FH. And in this way, time will always be shown to be divided by the faster object (by the previous demonstration), and the distance or <term>magnitude<note>original: "magnitudo." Refers to any continuous physical extension, like a line or a body.</note></term> will be divided by the slower object, according to Theorem 8.

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS

But indeed, if these things are divisible, then motion is also divided to infinity.
