## THEOREM XII
->In a finite time, there is no motion over an infinite distance.<-

### DEMONSTRATION
For if it were possible to move across an infinite space **FE** in a finite time **CD**, let the time **CD** be divided into two equal parts at point **k**. In the time **Ck**, the object **A** moves across either the whole space **FE** or a portion of it. But for it to move across the whole is impossible; for in the total time **CD**, only a certain portion of **FE** is moved through, not the whole, as has been shown. <note>Proclus is following Aristotle’s logic that if you cover an infinite distance in a finite time, you would have to cover a portion of that infinity in a fraction of that time, leading to a logical contradiction regarding the speed and the nature of infinity.</note> Let it move across the space **LM** in the time **CD**. Therefore, the motion is finite within a finite [time]. And it is clear that it is also impossible for an infinite distance to be moved through in the time **CD**. For it is impossible for the whole and the part to be moved through in the same amount of time.

### COROLLARY
From this it is certainly clear that just as the whole magnitude or space is related to its part, so also the motion of the whole—the time—is related to the time of the part, in those things which move with equal velocity <term>uniform speed</term>.

## THEOREM XIII
->No finite magnitude will be moved through in infinite time, etc.<-

### DEMONSTRATION
Let **A** be that which moves through the finite magnitude **BC**, and let the time of motion be the infinite time **DE**. Let the magnitude **BC** be divided in half. The object **A** moves through half of **BC**. This occurs either in an infinite or a finite time. Let us first suppose it moves in an infinite time. But everything that moves continuously moves through the whole in a greater time than it moves through the part. Therefore, the object **A** would move through the whole **BC** in a time greater than infinity—which is impossible. <note>In ancient logic, "greater than infinity" is a standard way to show a premise is absurd.</note> Therefore, it does not move in an infinite time, but in a finite one. Let a finite time **KL** be taken; then for the remaining half of **BC**, the object **A** moves again. But not in an infinite time; rather, in a finite time for the same reason. Let the finite time **LM** be established; therefore, in the combined time of **KL** and **LM**, the object **A** moves—

### THEOREM XIV.

Given a faster object in a **sesquialter ratio** <term>sesquialter ratio|A ratio of 3:2, where the first value is one and a half times the second.</term> to a slower one, to demonstrate that **indivisible lines** <note>The author is arguing against the idea that space is made of "atoms" or "points" that cannot be further divided.</note> do not exist.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Let **A** be faster than **B** in the stated ratio, and let three indivisible lines **C**, **D**, and **E** be taken, while two, **F** and **G**, are taken in an equal time. For the ratio of speed to speed is sesquialter. Let the time be **K**, **L**, and **M**. Since it has been demonstrated that if magnitude were composed of indivisible parts, then motion and time would also be composed of such parts. Let the parts of time **K**, **L**, and **M** themselves be taken. Therefore, **A** itself traverses **C**, **D**, and **E** during the times **K**, **L**, and **M**. But **B** also traverses **F** and **G** in those same times. Therefore, the time **K**, **L**, and **M** will be divided into the motion of **F** and **G**. It will be divided. Therefore, the supposedly indivisible **L** will be divisible, which is impossible. And since the faster object traverses **C**, **D**, and **E** in the whole time **K**, **L**, and **M**, it traverses half in half the time. Therefore, line **D** itself will be divided. But it was supposed to be indivisible, which is impossible.

### THEOREM XV.

The "Now" itself is the same in both past and future time. <note>In classical physics, the "Now" (the instant) is treated as the boundary point where the past ends and the future begins.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.

If it were possible [for them to be different], let there be two, such as **A** and **B**; however, these are not immediately successive to one another, as was previously demonstrated. But if each is separate, between both there will be time that is divisible to infinity, as has been shown. Therefore, let time **A**-**B** be divided at point **C**. Indeed, if **A** itself were the boundary of the whole past, and **B** itself the beginning of the whole future, there will not be past and future in the space between them. But time **A**-**B** is divided at point **C**. Thus, one part of it is past and the other is future, which is impossible. Therefore, the "Now" is the same, both in the past and in the future.

The "Now" itself lacks parts.

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher who applied rigorous Euclidean geometry styles to physical and metaphysical problems.</note>
## PROOF.

For if the same <term>Now</term> <note>Latin: *Nunc*; the Greek *nyn*. In this context, it refers to a mathematical point in time that has no duration.</note> exists in both the past and in the future, it lacks parts. For if it were divisible, the same things would happen: there would be some part of the past in the future, and some part of the future in the past. This is impossible. Therefore, the **Now** itself is indivisible.

## THEOREM XVII.

**Everything that moves, moves in time.**

### PROOF.
If it were possible, let something move in the *Now* itself; let one thing be faster and another slower. Let the slower one first move through space **A.B.** But the faster one, if it moves through the same space, will move through it in less [than the Now]. Therefore, the *Now* itself is divided. But it was demonstrated to be indivisible. Therefore, nothing moves in the *Now* itself. Everything that moves, therefore, moves in time. 

But [suppose] the faster thing moves in the *Now* itself. Either an indivisible space is moved, or a divisible one. But it has been shown that an indivisible line is not possible. Therefore, let the divisible space **A.B.** be moved, and let this be divided at point **C**. If the faster thing has moved through the whole space **A.B.** in the *Now* itself, it will move through space **A.C.** in less time. Therefore, the *Now* itself is divisible, which is impossible, etc.

## THEOREM XVIII.

**Everything at rest, rests in time.**

### PROOF.
For if it rests in the *Now* itself and not in time, it would also move in the *Now*. But this has been demonstrated to be impossible; therefore, it does not rest in the *Now*.

### ANOTHER PROOF.
If the *Now* is the same in the past and the future, as has been shown, and it is possible to be at rest in the past and to move in the future, then in the middle of them <note>i.e., at the exact moment of transition.</note> there is neither rest nor motion. But if there is, it would be at rest and in motion in the same *Now*, which is impossible.

THEO-

### ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 60

->THEOREM XIX.<-
**Everything that moves is divisible.** <note>original Latin: "partibile est." This theorem argues that any object in motion must have parts; an indivisible point cannot technically "be in motion" across a distance because it would have to be in two places at once or nowhere at all.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
Suppose something moves from point A to point B. It must be either in A alone, or in B alone, or in both, or in neither, or part of it is in A and part of it is in B. But if it is in A, it has not yet moved. If it is in B, it is no longer moving. If it were in both at once, it would be both not yet moving and finished moving. If it were in neither, there would be no motion from A to B, nor in the space between them. It is therefore necessary that part of it is in A and part is in B. Consequently, everything that moves is divisible.

->THEOREM XX.<-
**If the parts of any motion belong to the parts of a continuous whole, then the entire motion belongs to that entire whole.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
Let D-E be the motion of object A-B, and E-F be the motion of object B-C. I say that the entire motion D-F belongs to the whole object A-C. It is necessary that the motion D-F belongs either to A-C, or to the parts of A-C, or to some other thing entirely. Since it is a motion, it must belong to something that is moved. But the whole motion does not belong to the parts of A-C (for the parts of this motion belong to those parts, but not the whole motion). Nor does it belong to something else. If the total motion D-F belonged to some other thing, then its parts would also be motions of that other thing's parts. But we already established they were motions of the parts of A-C. It is impossible for one motion <term>one in number: a specific, individual instance of movement</term> to exist in multiple subjects. Therefore, the motion D-F belongs to A-C, which was what needed to be demonstrated.

->THEOREM XXI.<-
**Everything that has changed, at the moment it has first completed that change, exists in the state into which it has changed.** <note>This refers to the "instant" of completion—the moment an object finishes moving from one state to another.</note>

->DEMONSTRATION.<-
Suppose object A has changed from state B into state C. I say that object A is now in state C. For it must be either in B, or in C, or in some other state. That it is not in B is clear, for it [the text ends here]

### THEOREM XXII.
**Every thing that has changed, has changed first in an indivisible moment; etc.**
<note>Proclus is arguing that the exact moment a change is "complete" must be a single, indivisible point in time, rather than a duration.</note>

**DEMONSTRATION.**
Let A be changed in the first instance BC; I say that BC is indivisible. For if it were possible, let it be divided at point D. Truly, it has either changed in both [parts] BD and DC, or in one of them. But if it changed in both, it also changed in the first. Therefore, it did not change in the first instance BC, but in the earlier BD. But if it is said to have changed in both, it is also supposed to have changed in the whole. If, however, it changed in only one part, it will no longer be in the whole of BC, but in a part of it. Therefore, BC is not divisible; which is what was to be demonstrated.

### THEOREM XXIII.
**No change has a [discrete] beginning of the change.**
<note>This theorem explores the idea that because time is continuous, you cannot find a "first" part of a movement that isn't itself made of even smaller movements.</note>

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For if it were possible, let the beginning of change AB be the change AC. If AC is indivisible, then an indivisible thing would be adjacent to another indivisible thing <note>In ancient geometry, "indivisibles" (like points) cannot touch each other to form a line; there is always space between them.</note>. If, however, it is divisible, let it be divided into AD and DC. If, therefore, it has changed in both of these, it has also changed in the whole. But it was posited that it changed through the whole. But if it was changing in one part, it has changed in the other; it has no longer changed in the whole first part. But if it changed in both before it changed in AC, then it changed in AD itself. Therefore, a beginning of change cannot be grasped.

### THEOREM XXIV.
**If a change occurs according to some quantity, it will not be possible to identify a first part within it.**
<term>quantity: original "quantum." In this context, it refers to a measurable magnitude, like distance or weight.</term>

**DE-**

<summary>This page contains three geometric-style proofs (Theorems 22-24) arguing that the completion of a change happens in an indivisible instant, but the process of changing itself has no "first" starting part because time and magnitude are infinitely divisible.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, philosophy of motion, indivisibles, continuity of time, Aristotelian logic, physics</keywords>

# PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 61
->PROOF.<-

For if it were possible, let there be a change in magnitude **A B**. I say that it is not possible to take a "first part" in this **A B**. For if some part, perhaps **A C**, were taken as the "first" according to which it has changed: If indeed this **A C** is indivisible, then a thing without parts would be touching another thing without parts <note>original: "impartile contiguo impartili". This refers to the philosophical problem that continuous things cannot be made of "indivisible" points touching each other; they must overlap or be divisible.</note>. But if it is divisible, there will be something of this **A C** that is prior, into which the change occurred first, and something else prior to that, and so on into infinity. Therefore, there is no part of a magnitude that is the "first" part to have changed.

### THEOREM XXV.
If the first time of any change is taken in any part of time, that part will also contain a part of the change.

->PROOF.<-
For let the first time of change **A B** be taken as **H R**. Since all time is divisible into infinity, let it be divided at point **k**. Therefore, the change is either in both **H k** and **k R**, or in neither, or in one of the two. But if it were in neither, it would not be in the whole time either. If indeed it were only in one of them, then **H R** would no longer be the "first time" of the change <note>Because a smaller "first time" would exist within it.</note>. Therefore, the change exists in both **H k** and **k R**. This is what was to be demonstrated.

### THEOREM XXVI.
Everything that is moving has been moved <note>The original Latin "motum est" suggests a completed state—"has moved" or "has been moved"—distinguishing the process of moving from the state of having completed a movement.</note> previously.

->PROOF.<-
Let magnitude **A B** be moved in the first time **H R**, and let this first time be divided at point **k**. Therefore, some part of magnitude **A B** has been moved; and indeed it was moving in the whole time **H R**, but in time **H k** it has been moved. For the boundary of time **H k** is <term>The Now<note>original: "Nunc". In Aristotelian physics, the 'Now' is a dimensionless instant that connects the past and the future, like a point on a line.</note></term> itself. In this "Now," it is possible to *have been moved*, but by no means is it possible to *be moving*. Similarly, indeed, we shall demonstrate by dividing the time **H k** that "having been moved" exists before "moving." For <term>The Now</term> exists in all time. And thus, so does "having been moved."

THEO-

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
->THEOREM 27.<-

**E**verything that has already moved was moving before.
->DEMONSTRATION<-
**L**et something have changed from point A to point B. It has changed either in a period of time or in the "Now" <note>original Latin: "Nunc." In ancient physics, the 'Now' refers to an infinitesimal, indivisible instant, rather than a duration of time.</note> itself. But if it changed in the instant itself, it would be in both A and B at the same time during that same instant. For if it is in A at one instant and in B at another, there will be time between the two, because one indivisible thing cannot be contiguous with another indivisible thing <note>impartile: something without parts. Proclus argues that instants cannot "touch" each other to form time; there must be a duration between any two distinct moments.</note>; therefore, it changed from A to B in a span of time. However, all time is divisible. Thus, it could also be changed in half that time, and in half of that half, and so on to infinity. Therefore, everything that has moved was moving previously. This is what was necessary to demonstrate.

->THEOREM 28.<-
**I**f that which moves is infinite, it does not cross a limited magnitude in a limited time.
->DEMONSTRATION<-
**L**et the infinite thing that moves be A; let the limited magnitude which it crosses be B; and let the limited time be C. If, therefore, A itself moves through B, it is clear that B also moves through A <note>This is a relative motion argument: if a long rod passes a fixed point, it is mathematically the same as the point passing along the length of the rod.</note>. Since A is infinite and B is limited, [B] moves through an infinite space in a limited time. This is impossible. For when A moves through B, B also moves through A. But A moves through B in a limited time (therefore B also moves through A [in that same time]), which is impossible, as was demonstrated in Theorem 12.

->THEOREM 29.<-
**I**f that which moves is infinite, it does not cross an infinite magnitude in a limited time.
->DEMONSTRATION<-
**L**et the infinite thing that moves be A, the infinite magnitude be B, and the limited time be C. If, therefore, the infinite A moves through the infinite B, it also moves through its parts. Let a part of B be taken, called D. [A] will also move through D; and since time C is limited, the time for crossing D is also limited... <margin>limited</margin>

-># PHYSICAL ELEMENTS. 62<-

...it has been shown. Let **I** be this time. Therefore, an infinite thing **A** does not pass through a bounded magnitude **D** <note>original: "terminatam magnitudinem"</note> in a finite time, specifically **I**, which is impossible—as was shown in the preceding section. Therefore, an infinite thing is not moved through an infinite space in a bounded time, which was the point to be demonstrated.

### COROLLARY.
FROM these things it is clear that infinite motion does not exist, unless it occurs by the same thing happening many times <note>The author is likely referring to circular or repetitive motion, where the "infinity" is just the repetition of the same path.</note>. For either the infinite will be moved through the finite, or the infinite through the infinite—if indeed motion were to be infinite. However, all these things are impossible. Therefore, there is no infinite motion.

### THEOREM XXX.
EVERY thing that is moved through a place is, in its entirety, in its first place in the "Now" <term>The Now (Latin: *Nunc*): In classical physics, this refers to an indivisible instant of time, rather than a duration.</term>.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
FOR if it were not in the "Now" itself, but in a span of time, let it be in time **A.B.** in its first place; and let time **A.B.** be divided into **A.C.** and **C.B.** Therefore, **A.C.** is prior to **C.B.** But the thing which is moved, **A.B.**, is in the whole first place. Yet, existing in the same place during both a "before" and "after" period is the definition of being at rest. Therefore, that which is moved would be at rest, which is impossible. Thus, that which is moved is in its first place in the "Now" itself.

### THEOREM XXXI.
EVERY thing that is indivisible <term>Indivisible (Latin: *impartile*): A point or entity that has no physical parts or extension.</term> in magnitude is, by its very nature, immovable.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
IF it were possible, let an indivisible thing **A** be moved from **B** to **C**. Since everything that is moved is moved within time, during that time in which it is moved, it must either be entirely in **B** or entirely in **C**; or, one part of it is in **B** and another part is in **C**. But if it is entirely in **B**, it is not yet moving, but is at rest. If, however, one part of it is in **B** and another part is in **C**, then it must have parts. Therefore, that which is indivisible cannot be moved. Which was to be demonstrated.

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a major Greek Neoplatonist philosopher. This text is likely from a Latin translation of his "Elements of Physics," where he applies geometric logic to the natural world.</note>

## ANOTHER WAY.
<note>This section provides an alternative proof for a previous argument regarding the nature of motion and points.</note>
Let **A** be an <term>indivisible</term> <note>original: *impartile*. In ancient physics, this refers to a mathematical point or a fundamental unit that has no width, length, or depth, and thus cannot be split.</note>, and let it move through **B**. Since everything that moves, before its larger part moves, a part equal to or smaller than itself moves. Therefore, **A** would also move in a similar way. But if a larger or smaller part of it moves, it would necessarily have parts. If, however, an equal part moves, then **B** would be composed of <term>indivisibles</term>, which is impossible, as has already been shown. Therefore, an indivisible does not move.

## THEOREM XXXII.
That which is **indivisible** does not move a distance equal to itself.

## DEMONSTRATION.
If it is possible, let it move, and let the time of the motion be represented by **A. B.** Since all time is divisible, let **AB.** be divided into **AC.** and **CB.** Therefore, in the time **A C.** that which is moving—the indivisible—will move less <note>i.e., move a distance smaller than its own "size".</note>. But in the total time **AB.** it moved a distance equal to itself. However, that which is equal to an indivisible is itself also indivisible. Therefore, there would be something smaller than an indivisible <note>The logic here is that if a point moves a distance smaller than a point, that distance must be a "fraction" of a point, which contradicts the definition of an indivisible.</note>. Which is impossible. Therefore, an indivisible will not move a distance equal to itself.

->**THE END OF THE FIRST BOOK.**<-

# BY THE SAME PROCLUS
### ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS.
### BOOK II.

#### SUPPOSITIONS.

[Decorative initial O] **E**very physical body is movable in space.
Every local motion is either circular, straight, or a mixture of these.
Every physical body moves with one of these types of motion.
Every physical body is either **simple** <note>original: "simplex"; in ancient physics, a "simple" body is an element like fire or water that is not made of other substances.</note> or **composite**.
Every simple motion belongs to a simple body.
Every simple body moves with one motion according to its nature.

#### DEFINITIONS.

[Initial R] Fast things are said to have a mutual ratio to one another, the same as the ratio of the spaces through which they move.
**Heavy** <term>Heavy (Graue): In this context, "heavy" is defined by direction (toward the center) rather than just weight.</term> is that which moves toward the center.
**Light** <term>Light (Leue): That which moves away from the center (upward, toward the heavens).</term> is that which moves away from the center.
To be moved in a circle is said of that which is carried continuously from the same point back to the same point.
**Contrary motions** are those which occur from opposites toward opposites.
One thing is the contrary of one other thing.
**Time** is the number of the motion of the celestial bodies. <note>This reflects the Aristotelian idea that time is a way of measuring or counting the movement of the stars and planets.</note>
A **single motion** is that which is uniform in kind, belongs to a single subject, and occurs in continuous time.

#### THEOREM I.

[Initial Q] Those things which move in a circle according to their nature are **simple**.

<meta>This page continues the cosmological proofs of Proclus Diadochus, likely following an introduction to the properties of celestial and elemental bodies. The text focuses on the unique nature of circular motion.</meta>

# OF PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
## DEMONSTRATION
Let **A.B.** be that which moves in a circle according to its nature. I say that it is <term>simple</term> <note>In ancient physics, a "simple" body is an element not made of other parts, like pure fire or the "aether" of the heavens.</note>. Since circular motion is a simple motion, and every simple motion belongs to a simple body, the body **A.B.** is therefore simple. Thus, those things that move in a circle are simple.

->**THEOREM II.**<-

Those things which move in a circle according to nature are neither the same as those which move in a straight line, nor are they the same as those composed of such things.

### DEMONSTRATION
Let **A.B.** be that which moves in a circle according to nature. I say that it is not the same as those things which move in a straight line. For if it were the same as any of those, it would move either upward or downward according to its nature. But every simple body is moved by one simple motion according to its nature. Therefore, that which moves in a circle according to nature is not the same as any of those which move in a straight line. Nor is it the same as any composite <note>A body made of a mixture of different elements.</note> body. For it has been demonstrated that everything moving in a circle according to nature is entirely simple. But that which consists of things moving in a straight line is composite. Therefore, **A.B.**, which moves in a circle according to nature, is neither the same as those things moving in a straight line, nor those composed of them.

->**THEOREM III.**<-

Those things which move in a circle according to nature possess neither <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "gravitatem," here referring to the quality of "heaviness" that pulls an object toward the center of the earth.</note> nor <term>levity</term> <note>original: "levitatem," the quality of "lightness" that causes an object like fire to rise away from the center.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION
Let **A.B.** be moved in a circle according to nature. I say that it possesses neither gravity nor levity. For if **A.B.** is heavy or light, it moves either toward the center <note>The center of the universe, which in this period was considered to be the Earth.</note> or away from the center according to nature. For it is supposed that what moves toward the center is heavy, and what moves away from the center is light. But that which moves toward or away from the center is the same as one of those things moving in a straight line. Therefore, **A.B.** would be the same as something moving in a straight line while also moving in a circle according to nature, which is impossible.

->**THEOREM IV.**<-

To circular motion, nothing is contrary.

### DE- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the demonstration of Theorem IV on the following page.</note>

He concludes that because celestial motion is circular, it cannot be categorized as heavy or light, as those qualities require straight-line motion.</summary>

ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 64
->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For if it is possible, let there be a circular motion from A to B, and let there be a motion contrary to this, either one of those which occur along a straight line <note>The author is referring to rectilinear motion, like an object falling down or rising up</note>, or another circular one. If, therefore, upward motion is the contrary to circular motion, then downward motion and circular motion would be one and the same <note>original: "vnus erunt." This is a logical proof by contradiction: if A is the opposite of B, and C is also the opposite of B, then A and C must be the same thing</note>. If, however, downward motion were the contrary, then upward motion and circular motion would be the same as each other. For to any one motion, there is only one contrary motion toward opposite places.

But if the motion from point A is contrary to the motion starting from point B, there will be infinite spaces between these two contraries. For between points A and B, infinite circumferences <note>curved paths</note> can be described. 

But suppose there is a semicircle AB, and the motion from A to B is contrary to the motion from B to A. If that which moves from A to B along the semicircle stops at B, it will never be a <term>circular motion</term> <note>In ancient physics, circular motion is defined by its continuity and its return to the starting point</note>. For circular motion was defined as being from one point back to that same point continuously. If, however, the other semicircle is also moved continuously, A will not be the contrary of B. And if this is not the case, neither is the motion from A... <note>The text cuts off here mid-sentence</note>

### THEOREM VI.
**E**verything that moves in a circle is finite.

**DEMONSTRATION**
**F**or if it is possible, let there be some circle **A.B.** that is infinite from its center; and let **C.** be taken as the center of circle **A.B.**, and from the center let there be lines **C.A.** and **C.B.** Therefore, lines **C.A.** and **C.B.** are infinite. Consequently, the space that is between them—the angle—is infinite. For if it were finite, it would be possible to extend angles **A.C.** and **B.C.** to a single distance from **A.B.** But this is impossible, for the lines from the center are infinite. Therefore, the angle **A.C.B.** is also infinite. That which moves from **A**, therefore, will never traverse the space **A.B.** But indeed, that which moves in a circle returns to the same point; therefore, it is not infinite.

**ANOTHER WAY**
**L**et it be that **A.B.**, which moves in a circle, is infinite; and let a finite angle **C.D.** be taken within **A.B.** If, therefore, **A.B.** beginning from **A** comes back to the same point, and returns to that same point despite being infinite, it would pass through the finite angle **C.D.** in a finite <term>terminato; bounded or specific</term> time. For all parts of **A.B.** itself will pass through the angle **C.D.** However, this is impossible. For it was shown in the first book that an infinite thing does not pass through a finite thing in a finite time.

### THEOREM VII.
**T**he powers of bodies that are infinite in magnitude are themselves infinite.

**DEMONSTRATION**
**L**et there be an infinite body **A.B.**, and let its power <note>original: "potentia"; referring here to physical force or capacity, such as weight or gravity.</note> be finite, **C.**; and let it be gravity, and let a part **B.D.** be cut off from the infinite **A.B.**, and let the gravity of this body **B.D.** be **E.** Therefore, gravity **E** either measures <note>In the Euclidean sense of "dividing evenly into".</note> gravity **C** or it does not. First, let it measure it. As many times as gravity **E** measures **C**, let the body **B.D.** measure the infinite body **A.B.** the same number of times. It will follow, therefore, that as **E** relates to **C**, so **C.D.** <note>likely a typo in the original for B.D.</note> will relate to **A.B.**; and alternately, as **E** relates to **B.D.**, so **C** will relate to **A.B.** But the gravity **E** belonged to **B.D.**; therefore, **C** will also be the gravity of **A.B.** But **C** was already the gravity of the whole **A.B.** Therefore, the power of the infinite and the finite would be one and the same, and equal. Which is impossible. For let a part **B.I.** be taken of **B.Z.**...

# ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 65

Therefore, the segment **BI** <note>These letters refer to segments in a geometric diagram common in 17th-century physics texts.</note> either possesses weight or it does not. If it does not possess weight, then weight will not exist in the infinite whole, but only in a part of it. If, however, it does possess some weight, then **BI** will be heavier than **BZ**; therefore, the weight of **IB** is greater than that of **C**. But the weight of **C** was the weight of the infinite whole. Thus, the weight of the part would be greater than the weight of the whole and the infinite. <note>The author is using a "reductio ad absurdum" argument here: if the part is heavier than the whole, the initial premise must be false.</note> Furthermore, let weight **E** not measure the weight **C**. If, therefore, something is left over unmeasured, if it is taken many times over, it will eventually encompass the whole of **C**. For if taken twice, it measures only a portion, and likewise three times, four times, and so on to infinity. Therefore, let as many magnitudes of equal weight be taken from **BD** as the total of **E** exceeds the weight **C** when taken together; and let these be **BZ**. This **BZ**, therefore, has the same weight as **C**. But the weight **C** belonged to **AB**. Therefore, a part will have a greater weight than the whole and the infinite. The same argument and the same reasoning apply to lightness <note>original: "leuitate"</note> and every other <term>power</term> <note>original: "potentia"—refers to the inherent capacity of a body to act or move.</note>. Therefore, the powers of infinite bodies are not limited.

### THEOREM VIII.
The powers of bodies that are finite in size are not infinite.

### DEMONSTRATION.
IF it were possible, let the power **B** of a finite body **A** be infinite. Let a half-part of **A** be taken, which we shall call **C**; and let its power be **D**. It is certainly necessary that **C** has a smaller power than **B**. For a part possesses a smaller power than the whole. Let it be, therefore, that as **C** is to **A**, so **D** will measure **B**. Therefore, power **B** is also finite, and as **C** is to **A**, so **D** is to **B**. And conversely, as **C** is to **D**, so **A** is to **B**. Moreover, the power **D** belongs to the magnitude **C**; therefore, the power **B** will belong to the magnitude **A**. Therefore, the magnitude **A** has a finite power **B**. But it was [initially] assumed to be infinite, which is impossible. For it is impossible for a power of the same kind to be both finite and infinite in the same thing.

### THEOREM IX.
The powers of those things which are moved with unequal velocity respond unequally to the times of the motions.

LET those things which are moved unequally be **A** and **B**. Let **A** be moved more slowly through the distance **CI** toward **CE**. And since **B** is moved through the distance **CE** in the time **DR**, it is moved through the distance **CI** in a shorter time. For this has also been demonstrated. Let it be moved in [the time] **DZ**. Since

### THEOREM X.
**Infinite heaviness or lightness does not exist.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-
For if it were possible, let A be an infinite <term>heaviness</term><note>original: "grauitas"; in ancient physics, this is the internal quality that causes an object to move downward.</note>, and let the body possessing it move through space B. Since everything that moves does so in a certain amount of time (as was demonstrated in the first theorem), A will move through space B in time C. Now, let D, which has a finite power, move through space B, and let the time of its motion be E. Time E must be greater than time C, because a greater power moves through the same space in a shorter time. 

Since that which has infinite power moves in time C, and that which has finite power moves in time E, and given that the powers of things moving at unequal speeds correspond to the times of their motions: it follows that the ratio of the infinite heaviness to the finite heaviness is the same as the ratio of the finite time E to time C. This is impossible. For the infinite has no ratio to the finite; and it certainly cannot have the specific ratio it would have if it were finite. The same reasoning also applies to <term>lightness</term><note>original: "læuitas"; the quality that causes objects like fire or air to move upward.</note>. Therefore, infinite heaviness does not exist, nor does lightness.

### THEOREM XI.
**Nothing infinite can be acted upon by something finite.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-
Suppose A is infinite and B is finite, and let A be <term>acted upon</term><note>original: "pati"; in this context, it means to be moved or changed by an external force.</note> by B in time C. Let D, which is smaller than B, be taken as acting during the same time C, possessing a power similar to B. Therefore, D will perform less action while acting in the same time C; for that which is smaller causes less motion than that which is larger. Let E, then, be the smaller amount acted upon by D. Let it be that as D is to B, so E is to some other amount Z. Since the ratio of power D to power...

power B (for these powers are active) so is E to Z. And alternately, just as power D is to magnitude E, so is power B to magnitude Z. But power D moved magnitude E in time C; therefore power B will also move [magnitude Z] in the same time. But it was supposed that the infinite, which was A, in time C, moved power B. Therefore, in the same time, the same power moves both a greater and a lesser thing—the finite and the infinite—which is impossible. For a greater thing is affected by the same power in a greater time, a lesser in a lesser, and an equal in an equal time. Therefore, the infinite cannot be affected <note>The Latin *pati* (to suffer/be affected) refers to the "patient"—the object receiving the action of a force.</note> by the finite.

### THEOREM XII.
**No finite thing can be affected by the infinite.**

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For if it were possible, let some finite thing **B Z** be affected by an infinite power **A** in time **C**; and let a finite power **D** be taken, and let it be of the same kind <note>original: "eiuſdem ſpeciei"</note> as **A**. This power [D] will surely act less than **B Z** in time **C**. Let it act upon **Z**, which is less than **Z B**. And let it be that as **Z** is to **B**, so power **D** is to power **E**. And since as **Z** is to **B Z**, so **D** is to **E**, then alternately, as **Z** is to **D**, so **B Z** is to **E**. But **Z** is affected by power **D** in time **C**; therefore **B Z** will be affected by power **E** in the same time. Therefore power **E** is the motive power <term>motive power: the force required to produce motion</term> of **B Z** itself in time **C**. But the power of **B Z** was **A**, which was infinite, and power **D** was finite, acting as a mover in the same time. Therefore, in an equal time, an infinite power and a finite power move the same thing, which is impossible.

### THEOREM XIII.
**No infinite thing can be affected by the infinite.**

**DEMONSTRATION.**
For if it were possible, let **A** be some infinite agent <note>The "agent" is the thing performing the action or exerting the force.</note>, and the thing being affected <note>original: "patiẽs" (patiens)</note> be the infinite **B**. Let **C D** be the time in which **A** acts and **B** is affected. And since **A** acted upon the whole of **B** in the whole time **C D**, it will act upon a part of it in a shorter time. Therefore, let a part of **B** be **E**, and let **D** be the time in which **A** acts upon it. And let it be that as time **D** relates to time **C D**, so **E** relates to **Z**, which is a part of **B**. For since the times are finite <note>original: "terminata"</note>, and **E** is also finite, it is possible to assume a proportion such that as **D** is to time **C D**, so **E T** is to another finite part of **B**, which is...

infinite. Let Z, therefore, be taken. It will be, then, just as time D is to time C D, so is E to Z. And conversely, just as time D is to E, so is time C D to Z. But time D relates to E in such a way that E, in time D, is acted upon by A. Therefore, C D will relate to Z in such a way that Z, in time C D, is acted upon by the same <note>meaning the same "power" or "force" A mentioned in the previous part of the proof</note>. But it was also assumed that the whole B, being infinite, is acted upon in time C D. Thus, both the part and the whole—both the infinite and the finite—are moved by the same power in the same amount of time. Which is impossible.

### THEOREM XIV.
**Simple bodies are limited according to their form.**

->**PROOF.**<-

Let the simple body be magnitude A. Since a **simple body** <note>In ancient physics, "simple bodies" are the primary elements like fire or earth, which have a single, natural direction of movement</note> is moved by a **simple motion**, therefore A is moved by a simple motion. If this motion is circular, it possesses one nature and one form <note>original: *speciem*; referring here to the specific "kind" or "type" of the element</note>. If, however, it is among those things that move in a straight line: if it moves only away from the center, it will be **fire**. If it moves only toward the center, it will be **earth**. If it is light in relation to one thing and heavy in relation to another, it will be one of the middle elements <note>This refers to Air and Water, which sit between the absolute lightness of Fire and the absolute heaviness of Earth</note>. Therefore, the forms of simple bodies are limited.

### THEOREM XV.
**No sensible body is infinite.**

->**PROOF.**<-

For if it were possible: Let **sensible body** <note>A "sensible" body is a physical object that can be perceived by the senses, as opposed to a mathematical or conceptual object</note> A be infinite. Since every physical body is either **simple** or **composite**, let it first be simple. And since the motion of every simple body is also simple, the motion of A itself will be simple. And since there are only two simple motions—straight and circular—then A will be moved either in a circle or in a straight line. 

But if it is moved in a circle, it is not infinite, as has already been demonstrated. If, on the other hand, it moves in a straight line: if it moves downward, it will have infinite weight; if it moves upward, it will have infinite lightness. For this too has been demonstrated: that the moving powers of infinite bodies are themselves infinite. But it is impossible for weight or lightness to be infinite, as has also been demonstrated. Therefore, the infinite body A does not move in a straight line. Moreover, it has been demonstrated that it does not move in a circle either. Therefore, it is not among those things moved by simple motion; nor, therefore, is it simple...

<summary>Proclus argues that physical elements are limited by their natural types of motion (upward, downward, or circular) and concludes that no physically perceptible body can be infinite in size.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, physics, elements, motion, infinity, fire, earth, simple bodies, Aristotle's influence, magnitude</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues a series of logical proofs (syllogisms) demonstrating that a physical body cannot be infinite, likely following the Aristotelian tradition of natural philosophy.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 67<-

...is complex. For every <term>simple body</term><note>In Aristotelian physics, a "simple body" refers to one of the four elements (earth, water, air, fire) which have a single, natural direction of motion.</note> moved with a **simple motion** according to its nature. Let **A**, therefore, be a **composite body**. But if it is composite, it is made either of finite parts or of infinite parts. If it is from finite parts—both in number and in size—then it is itself finite. If, however, it is from infinite parts, they are either infinite in number, or in size, or both. But there are not an infinite number of forms for simple bodies, as has been shown. It remains, therefore, that they would be infinite in size. But if it is one of those things that move in a circle, it has been demonstrated that it must be limited. If indeed it is one of those which move in a straight line, these too have been demonstrated to be finite. Therefore, body **A** is in no way infinite; it is neither simple nor composite.

->**OTHERWISE.**<-

Let **A** be an infinite and <term>perceptible body</term><note>A body that can be experienced through the senses, as opposed to a mathematical abstraction.</note>. Since it is infinite, it possesses infinite power. For this has been shown. But if it has infinite power, it will have either an **active** power or a **passive** power. If it has active power, it will act either upon a finite thing or an infinite thing; and if passive, it is acted upon either by a finite thing or an infinite thing. It has been shown, however, that the infinite cannot act upon an infinite thing, nor upon a limited thing, nor can it be acted upon by them. Therefore, the natural body **A** is not infinite. For every natural body has power, either active, or passive, or both.

->**OTHERWISE.**<-

Let body **A** be infinite. If, therefore, **A** is a physical body, it will be <term>mobile according to place</term><note>This refers to "local motion" or change of position, one of the primary ways of studying physical objects in this era.</note>. But everything mobile according to place either moves from one place to another or moves within the same place. If, therefore, **A** moves in the same place, it will move around a center; and since it must have a center, it will not be infinite. If, however, it changes location, it will not be everywhere at once, but in some specific part of the universal space. But the "infinite" is that which is extended everywhere and in every direction. Therefore, **A** itself will not be infinite.

->**OTHERWISE.**<-

If **A** belongs to those bodies that move in a straight line, it moves either by force or according to nature. But if it moves according to nature, it passes from a foreign place into its own proper place. Therefore, it is not everywhere.

->PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the last great head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. He was called "Diadochus" (The Successor) because he held the chair of Plato.</note>

For if a thing is moved by force, that which applies the force will be more powerful than the thing itself. But nothing is more powerful than the infinite. For the infinite also possesses an infinite moving power.

### THEOREM 16.
**Time is continuous and eternal.**

#### PROOF.
For if it were not continuous, nor eternal, it would have some beginning. Let time, therefore, be represented by the line AB, and let its beginning be A. If this A is itself time, it is divisible, and thus we would not yet have the beginning of time; instead, there would be another beginning before that beginning. But if the <term>Now</term><note>original: "nunc". In Neoplatonic philosophy, the 'Now' is the indivisible instant that links the past to the future.</note> itself exists, it must be indivisible and the boundary of another time. For the <term>Now</term> is not only a beginning, but also an end. Therefore, time existed before point A. Likewise, if B is the end of time—if B were time itself—it would be divided infinitely and into many other boundaries within itself. But if B is the <term>Now</term> itself, it will also be a beginning. For the <term>Now</term> is not only a boundary, but also an end.

#### ANOTHER PROOF.
Let time be represented by A.B. If it were not eternal, it would have a beginning and an end. But if this were the case, it would at one point be "being" <note>existing</note> and at another point "non-being" <note>not existing</note>. That which is of such a nature exists within time and then does not; therefore, time would exist within time.

### THEOREM 17.
**Circular motion is eternal.**

#### PROOF.
Let there be a circular motion of a circle A.B. I say that this is eternal. For since time is eternal, it is necessary that motion must always exist. And since time is continuous (for the <term>Now</term> is the same in both the past and the future), it is necessary that some single motion must also be continuous; for time is the <term>measure of motion</term><note>original: "numerus motus". This follows Aristotle's definition of time as the "number" or "calculation" of motion in respect to before and after.</note>. But all other types of motion are not eternal, for they occur from opposites to opposites <note>Linear motion, for example, has a starting point and an ending point which are opposites.</note>. Only circular motion, therefore, is eternal, for to it nothing...

<summary>Proclus provides logical proofs for the eternity and continuity of time, arguing that the "Now" acts as both a beginning and an end, and concludes that circular motion is the only motion that can be eternal.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus Diadochus, Neoplatonism, philosophy of time, circular motion, infinity, the Now, Aristotle’s physics</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 63

...nothing is contrary <note>original: *nihil est contrarium*. This likely concludes a previous discussion regarding circular motion, which Aristotelian physics argued had no opposite, unlike linear motion.</note>. As has been shown.
However, we shall demonstrate in the following way that all motions which occur between **opposites** <term>contraries: things that are as far apart as possible, like two ends of a line</term> are limited and not eternal.
For let there be a motion **A.B.** between two opposites, **A.** and **B.** This motion is therefore bounded by **A.** and **B.** themselves, and is not infinite. Furthermore, the motion that belongs to **A.** is not continuous with that which belongs to **B.** Instead, when that which is moved returns, it will come to a stop at **B.** For if the motion from **A.** and from **B.** were to become one and continuous, that which is moved from **D.** <note>The letter D likely refers to a starting point or intermediate point in the geometric diagram being described.</note> would be moved into the same place. It would therefore move in vain, since it would already be in **A.** But nature does nothing in vain; therefore, it is not a single motion. If, then, motions that occur between opposites are not eternal, it is likewise impossible to be moved along a straight line into infinity. For the opposites themselves are the boundaries; nor do they create a single motion when they return.

->ANOTHER WAY.<-

That the motion starting from **A.** is not continuous with that starting from **B.** For if they were continuous with one another, there would be a motion contrary to another contrary motion, occurring at the same time and regarding the same thing. For that which is in **B.** will have arrived there and will move out from there at the same time—and both of these as actualities—which is impossible. Therefore, the "now" <term>instants or moments of time</term> in which it arrived in **B.** is different from the "now" in which it departs from **B.** Between different "nows" there is a period of time; therefore, the object is at rest, and is moved by neither at that "now."

### THEOREM XVIII. ###

That which causes eternal motion is itself eternal.

->PROOF.<-

Let **A.** be that which causes an eternal motion; I say that **A.** itself is also eternal. Because if it were not, it would by no means cause motion at the time when it did not exist. However, if this were not causing motion, then the motion that existed previously would also cease. But it was supposed to be eternal. Furthermore, if nothing else were causing the motion, that which is moved eternally would become immobile. But if something else were causing the motion, the motion would not be continuous, which is impossible. Therefore, that which causes an eternal motion is itself eternal.

### THEOREM XIX. ###

The immobile precedes both movers and things moved. <note>Aristotle's famous concept of the "Unmoved Mover"—the primary cause of all motion in the universe that does not itself need to be moved by anything else.</note>

->PRO-<- <note>The text cuts off here at the start of the "DEMONSTRATIO" or Proof for Theorem 19.</note>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstrations of Proclus regarding the nature of motion and the first mover, following previous proofs about the finiteness of physical bodies.</meta>

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI". Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) to Plato’s academy.</note>

## PROOF

Let something that is moved be called **A**, moved by **B**, and this **B** moved by **C**. I declare that this series will eventually come to a halt, and that it is not the case that every mover is itself moved by something else. 

For if this were possible, the motion would either occur in a circle or proceed to infinity. But if there were an infinite number of both movers and things being moved, there would be an infinite multitude and magnitude. For everything that is moved is divisible <note>In Aristotelian and Neoplatonic physics, anything that moves must have parts or "magnitude" to occupy space and undergo change.</note>, and it moves by way of touching. Therefore, that which is composed of a count of infinite parts would be infinite in size. However, it has been demonstrated that it is impossible for a physical body—whether composite or simple—to be infinite. 

On the other hand, if the motion occurs in a circle, something that is only moved at certain times would be the cause of eternal motion, assuming everything in the circle mutually moves and is moved. This is impossible. For that which produces eternal motion must itself be eternal. Therefore, among things that are moved, the motion is neither circular nor infinite. Consequently, there exists a mover that is itself immobile and eternal.

### COROLLARY

From this, it is certainly clear that not all things are in motion: for there is indeed something immobile. Nor do all things remain at rest, for there are some things that move. Neither is it the case that some things always rest while others always move; for there are some things that rest at certain times and move at others—such as things that move from one opposite state to another. Finally, it is not the case that all things sometimes rest and sometimes move. For there is that which moves eternally, and that which is eternally immobile.

## THEOREM XX.

**Everything that is moved is moved by something.**

## PROOF

Let **A** be that which is moved; I say that **A** is moved by something. For it is moved either according to its nature or contrary to its nature. If it moves according to its nature, then nature itself is the mover. But if it moves contrary to its nature, then the thing that exerts the force is the mover. For every motion that occurs contrary to nature is forced <term>violentus: a technical term for motion caused by an external agent against an object's natural inclination, like a stone being thrown upward</term>.

## THEOREM XXI.

**The primary thing that causes circular motion is indivisible.** <term>impartile: lacking parts or physical magnitude; a "point-like" or spiritual existence that cannot be broken down</term>.

<summary>Proclus argues that motion cannot proceed infinitely or in a simple loop, concluding there must be an eternal, unmoved mover. He also establishes that all motion is either natural or forced and that the primary cause of circular motion must be indivisible.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, motion, Unmoved Mover, infinite regress, circular motion, natural motion, forced motion, Neoplatonism, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
# ELEMENTS OF PHYSICS. 69

## ☙ PROOF.

Let **A** be the first mover of motion. It is necessary that such a thing exists, because everything that is moved is moved by something else <note>This is a core principle of Aristotelian physics: nothing moves itself without a prior cause.</note>. Clearly, if **A** is the first mover, it will be immobile <note>original: *immobile*; This refers to the "Unmoved Mover," a being that causes motion in others without being moved itself.</note>. For that which is immobile precedes everything that moves. And since it causes eternal motion, it possesses an infinite power of moving; for a finite power also has finite actions, since action is derived from power. Therefore, if the action is infinite, the power is also infinite.

It is necessary, then, that the first mover of circular motion <note>In ancient and medieval science, circular motion—like that of the stars—was considered the most perfect and eternal form of movement.</note> is either a physical body or something incorporeal <note>meaning without a physical body, like a soul or a divine intellect</note>. If it were a body, it would be either finite or infinite. An infinite body does not exist; and even if it did, it could not move a finite body, as has been previously demonstrated. If, on the other hand, it is a finite body, it cannot possess infinite power, for the powers of things that are finite in magnitude are themselves finite, as has also been shown. Therefore, that which causes the first circular motion is not a physical body. It is, therefore, incorporeal and of infinite power, which is what needed to be demonstrated.

->**THE END.**<-

His other writings on all the Dialogues of Plato, and works of another kind, have perished.

However, there still exist in this genre of Platonic Philosophy the most elegant commentaries on the *Phaedrus* by Hermias, who was the father of Ammonius; likewise, certain excerpts from the commentaries of the far-learned Olympiodorus on the *Phaedo* and *Philebus*, and his complete work on the *Gorgias*. If all these were to be seen publicly, they would excite the most ardent love for divine wisdom in those hearts that devote themselves to philosophy—not for the sake of clever arguing, but for this one purpose: that they may truly become wise.

If these works should at some time be brought to light by the effort of some man—a true man indeed—then, however laborious yet glorious the task, it will finally appear how great a part of wisdom lies buried in darkness while we follow and love only this common wisdom used in the schools <note>A reference to "Scholasticism," the Aristotelian-focused curriculum dominant in Renaissance universities</note>. To this cause, as far as life and leisure allow, we do not lack the

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of Proclus's "Elements of Theology." There is no previous page for continuity as this contains the title and the first two fundamental propositions.</meta>

# PROCLUS LYCIUS THE SUCCESSOR
# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY
### 211 PROPOSITIONS
### DEMONSTRATED IN THE MANNER OF MATHEMATICS

->CONCERNING THE ONE<-

#### PROPOSITION I
**Every multitude in some way participates in unity.**
<note>Proclus uses "the One" or "unity" to describe the ultimate source of all existence. To "participate" means to derive its being or character from that higher source.</note>

**DEMONSTRATION**
For if it participated in unity in no way at all, neither the whole multitude would be one, nor would each of the individual parts from which the multitude is composed be one. Instead, each of those parts would itself be a multitude, and this would continue to infinity; and each of these infinites would in turn be an infinite multitude. 

For if a thing participates in the One in no part whatsoever, neither the whole itself nor the individual parts within it [would be one]; the result would be entirely and utterly infinite. For whatever individual part you might take from the many, it must be either one or not-one. But if it is not-one, it must be either many or nothing. 

Now, if each part is nothing, then that which is composed of them is also nothing. But if each part is many, it is composed of infinites-times-infinites. These things, however, are impossible. For no existing thing is composed of infinites-times-infinites, as there is nothing greater than the infinite—yet a whole composed of all parts is necessarily greater than any single part. Nor can anything be composed of nothing. Therefore, every <term>multitude</term> <note>original Latin: "multitudo"; refers to any collection of more than one thing</note> in some way participates in unity.

#### PROPOSITION II
**Everything that participates in unity is both one and not-one.**

**DEMONSTRATION**
For if it is not both, it must be the "One" itself. It "participates" in unity because it is something other than the One. Thus, it undergoes unity through participation and sustains the process of becoming one. 

However, if there were nothing besides the One, and the One existed alone, it would not "participate" in unity, but would be the One itself. If, however, there is... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This opening page introduces Proclus's logical framework for Neoplatonism, establishing that all pluralities must possess a degree of unity to exist, otherwise they would dissolve into an impossible "infinite infinity."</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, Multitude, Unity, Participation, Metaphysics, Logic</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the opening of a Latin translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology" (Elementatio Theologica). The text focuses on the metaphysical relationship between "The One" and multiplicity, a cornerstone of Neoplatonic thought.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 1

...there is something besides that which is not one, a participant in the One, and it is not the One itself: it is a "one being," and a participant in the One. This, therefore, is not "not-one," nor is it the One itself <note>Original: "ipsunum." This refers to the Absolute One or the Primal Unity in Neoplatonic philosophy</note>; yet it is one, being both "being" and a "participant in the One" simultaneously. Because of this, it is not the One existing by itself, but is both one and not-one: existing as something other than the One, by which it is indeed more than "not-one," but by what it undergoes <note>The author uses "patitur" (suffers/undergoes) to describe the process of receiving a higher quality</note>, it is one.
Therefore, everything that participates in the One is both one and not-one.

### PROPOSITION III.
Everything that becomes one, becomes one through the participation of the One, and insofar as it has undergone the participation of the One, it is one.

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if those things which are not one become one; namely, by coming together and communicating with one another, they become one and sustain the presence of the One, though they are not the One itself. They participate in the One, therefore, insofar as they allow themselves to become one. For if they were already one, they would not *become* one. That which already is, does not become that which it already is. But if they become one from "not-one" and from a state of deprivation, they will first possess the One by some unity being generated within them.

### PROPOSITION IIII.
Everything unified is different from the One itself.

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it is unified, it will be a participant of the One, insofar as it is called "unified." But a participant of the One is both one and not-one. However, the <term>One itself</term> <note>The "Ipsunum" or "Auto-hen"—the ultimate source of all unity that is not composed of parts</note> is in no way both one and not-one. For if the One itself were also both one and not-one, then the "one" within it would again have both these qualities, and this would continue into infinity; since there would be no "One itself" in which the process could stop. But everything would be both one and not-one. Therefore, the unified thing is something other than the One. For if the unified and the One were the same, there would be an infinite multitude, and similarly for each of those things from which the unified thing is composed.

### PROPOSITION V.
All multitude is secondary to the One.

DE- <note>Catchword for the next page, likely beginning the Demonstration of Proposition V</note>

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental Neoplatonic logic regarding "The One," arguing that anything that possesses unity is distinct from Unity itself and must "participate" in it to exist. It establishes that the Absolute One must be a simple, non-composite source to avoid an infinite logical regress.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, Participation, Unity, Multiplicity, Metaphysics, Infinite Regress</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page contains a foundational metaphysical argument from Proclus's *Elements of Theology* (specifically Proposition 5), translated from a Latin edition. Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" (Diadochus) of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</meta>

# A PROOF BY PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
<note>Proclus "the Successor" was one of the last major Greek philosophers; this text explores how "The One" relates to the "Many" (multitude).</note>

For if a multitude were prior to <term>the One</term> <note>original: "unum"; the ultimate, indivisible source of all reality in Neoplatonic thought</note>, it would not participate in the One. This is because that multitude would exist before the One itself comes to be. Indeed, it cannot be a participant in something that does not yet exist. Furthermore, that which <term>participates</term> <note>the philosophical concept where a lower thing shares in the nature of a higher principle</note> in the One is both one and not-one; but if the multitude were primary, the One would not yet have a foundation. However, it is impossible for there to be any multitude that does not in some way participate in the One. Therefore, a multitude cannot exist before the One.

But if it were simultaneous with the One by nature and of the same rank (for nothing prevents this in terms of time), then the One by itself could not be many, nor would the multitude be one, so that they would be simultaneously distinct by nature. This would be the case if neither were prior or posterior to the other. In that case, the multitude would not be "one" in itself; and every single thing within that multitude would also not be one, and this would continue to infinity <note>Proclus argues that without a unifying principle, everything would break down into smaller and smaller parts forever, which he considers a logical impossibility</note>; which is impossible.

Therefore, the multitude participates in the One by its very nature, and it is impossible to take any part of it that is not one. For if it were not one, it would be an infinity composed of infinities, as has been demonstrated. Thus, it participates in the One in every way. 

If the One—which is the <term>One-itself</term> <note>original: "ipsum unum"; the absolute Unity that is not "one something" but "Oneness" itself</note>—in no way participates in multitude, then the multitude will be entirely posterior to the One; it participates in the One, but is not participated in by the One. 

But if the One were to also participate in multitude—existing as a substance that is one, but being "not-one" through its participation—then the One-itself would be multiplied, just as a multitude is made one through the One. Therefore, both the One would communicate with the multitude, and the multitude with the One. 

However, if things that come together and communicate with each other in some way are gathered by another, that third thing is prior to them. If they gather themselves, they cannot be opposites. For opposites do not naturally move toward one another. But if the One and the multitude are distinguished as opposites, and the multitude (as a multitude) is not the One, and the One (as the One) is not a multitude, then one cannot become the other; for then they would be one and two at the same time. <note>In traditional logic, a thing cannot be its own opposite simultaneously in the same way.</note> Indeed, the rational and the irrational exist together in a genus as distinct opposites. 

But if there is something prior to them that gathers them together, that thing itself must be either one or not-one. If it is not-one, it is either many or nothing. It cannot be many, lest a multitude exist before the One. Nor can it be nothing, for how could nothingness gather anything? Therefore, it is the One alone. Nor is this "One" also many, lest we fall into an infinite regress. 

The One-itself therefore exists, and every multitude originates from the One-itself.

PRO- <note>This is a "catchword," a printer's tool repeating the first syllable of the next page.</note>

<summary>This page presents Proclus’s logical argument that "The One" (Unity) must be the primary principle of existence, as any "multitude" requires a unifying principle to exist or be understood.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, Multitude, Participation, Metaphysics, Unity, Infinite Regress</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of the "One" and the "Many," a central theme in Proclus's Neoplatonic philosophy. It transitions from the composition of groups (multitudes) to the relationship between a cause (producer) and its effect (product).</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 2<-

### PROPOSITION 6.
**Every multitude is composed either of things that have been unified or of unities.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For it is clear that each individual part of a group—a <term>multitude</term> <note>In this context, a "multitude" refers to any collection of more than one thing.</note>—is not merely a multitude itself; and likewise, it is clear that each part of that further multitude is not simply a multitude. If it is not just a multitude, it must be either a **unified thing** or a collection of **unities**. Indeed, if it participates in the "One," then these parts are **unified**. But those things from which a unified thing is first composed are **unities**. For if there is such a thing as "the One itself," there must also be that which first participates in it and is "first unified." This first unified thing is composed of unities. For if it were composed of things that were already unified, those in turn would be composed of something else, and so on to infinity. However, there must be a "first unified thing" that is composed of unities. And so we have found exactly what we proposed at the beginning.

->CONCERNING PRODUCERS AND PRODUCTS.<-
### PROPOSITION 7.
**Every producer of another is superior in nature to that which is produced.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For the **producer** <note>The "producer" is the cause or source from which something else originates.</note> is either superior, inferior, or equal to the product. Let us first suppose it is equal. Therefore, the thing produced by it either has the power to produce something else itself, or it remains entirely sterile. But if it is sterile, it is by that very fact inferior to its producer; it is unequal to its source, which is fruitful and possesses the power of acting, while the product is ineffective. 

However, if the product is also a producer of other things, it either produces something equal to itself (and this would happen in all cases, and all beings would be equal to one another, and nothing would be superior to anything else, since a producer would always constitute its product as an equal) or it produces something unequal. But if the latter is true, it will no longer be equal to its own producer. For it is the nature of equal powers to produce equal results. Yet these results would be unequal to one another, since the producer is equal to what came before it, but unequal to what comes after it.

B Not <note>original: "Non" — This is a "catchword," a common feature in old printing where the first word of the following page is printed at the bottom right to help the binder or reader.</note>

<summary>This page argues that all collections must eventually be composed of basic unities rather than an infinite regress of groups, and establishes the metaphysical rule that a cause is always more powerful and "superior" than the effect it produces.</summary>

<keywords>Multitude, Unities, Unified, Producer, Product, Causality, Superiority, Neoplatonism, Proclus, Oneness</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus regarding the relationship between causes (producers) and effects (products), moving into his famous definition of the "First Good."</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the last great head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. He is called "The Successor" (Diadochos) because he was seen as the spiritual heir to Plato.</note>

Therefore, it is not right for the product to be equal to the producer. On the contrary, the producer will never be inferior. For if the producer bestows essence upon the product, it also grants power according to that essence. If, however, the producer is the source of all power for that which comes after it, it would also be able to make itself of the same quality as that product. If this were true, it would even make itself more powerful. For lack of power does not prevent it—since the creative power is present—nor does a lack of will prevent it. For all things seek the good according to their nature. Therefore, if it can make another thing more perfect, it would have perfected itself before perfecting that which follows after it. Thus, the product is neither equal to the producer nor superior to it. In every way, therefore, the producer is superior to the nature of the product.
<note>This concludes Proposition 7, establishing a hierarchy where the source is always greater than what it creates.</note>

## ON THE FIRST GOOD, WHICH IS ALSO CALLED THE GOOD ITSELF.

### PROPOSITION 8.

For all things that participate in the good in any way, the First Good precedes them, and it is nothing other than the Good. <margin>O. <note>This marginal "O" likely serves as a reference marker for the reader or printer.</note></margin>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if all beings desire the good, it is clear that the First Good is above beings. For if it were the same as one of those beings, then either that being and the Good itself are the same, or they are different. If they are the same, this being would no longer be a desirer of the good, since it would itself exist as the Good itself. For that which desires something is in need of what it desires, and is something different and foreign from the object desired. 

Or, they are indeed one thing and another <note>meaning they are distinct</note>. In this case, this being will <term>participate</term><note>Participation is a Platonic concept where a physical or lower thing "shares in" the qualities of a higher, perfect Form or Principle.</note> in the good, while the Good itself will be the thing participated in by it. In that case, there is a certain "good" found within one of the participants, which only that specific participant desires. But that is not the "Good" in an absolute sense, which all beings desire. For that <note>the First Good</note> is the common object of desire for all beings. However, that which is generated within a specific thing belongs only to that participant. Therefore, the First Good is nothing other than the Good. For if you add anything else to it, by that addition you will diminish the Good itself, making it "a certain kind of good" instead of that Good which is simply and purely the Good.

<summary>This page concludes the argument that a cause is always superior to its effect and begins a proof that the ultimate "Good" must be a pure principle above all individual beings.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Causality, The Good, Participation, Essence, Perfection, First Principle</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus's Neoplatonic philosophy, moving from the nature of the "One" or "Good" toward the concept of self-sufficiency. The previous page likely established that the Good is the primary principle of all things.</meta>

### THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 3

For that which is added, since it is not the Good itself but something less than it, diminishes the Good itself by its own very essence. <note>Proclus argues that adding anything to a perfect unity actually makes it less perfect because it introduces multiplicity.</note>

->**CONCERNING THE SELF-SUFFICIENT.**<-

### PROPOSITION IX.

**Everything that is self-sufficient, whether in its essence or in its activity, is superior to that which is not self-sufficient and which depends on another cause for its perfection.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if all beings naturally desire the Good, and one provides the good for itself while another stands in need of another, the former truly possesses the cause of its good within itself, while the latter has it existing separately. Therefore, the closer a thing is to that which provides what is desirable, the more superior it will be compared to that which requires a separate cause and receives the <term>perfection</term> <note>In this context, "perfection" refers to the completion or full realization of a thing's nature.</note> of its existence or activity from elsewhere. 

Furthermore, the self-sufficient is both similar to the Good itself and yet a "diminished" version of it. It is "diminished" because it <term>participates</term> <note>A technical term in Neoplatonism where a lower reality shares in the qualities of a higher one without exhausting that higher source.</note> in the Good and is not the Primary Good itself. Yet it is related to it in some way, insofar as it is able to possess the good through its own power. However, that which participates in the Good through another is further removed from the Primary Good, which is nothing other than the Good itself, etc.

### PROPOSITION X.

**Everything self-sufficient is inferior to the Absolute Good.** <note>original: "simpliciter bono" — literally "the good simply," meaning the Good in its purest, unmixed form.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For what else is the self-sufficient than that which possesses the good by and in itself? Such a thing is already full of the good and is a participant in it. But it is not the <term>Absolute Good</term> itself. For that which is participated in—and which provides the fullness—is superior to the participant, as has been demonstrated. 

If, therefore, a self-sufficient thing has filled itself with good, that source from which it filled itself must be superior to the self-sufficient and "beyond self-sufficiency." Nor is the Absolute Good in need of anything. For it does not desire anything else; if it did, it would be deficient in good according to that desire,

B 2 <note>This is a printer's signature mark used for bookbinding, indicating the second leaf of the second quire.</note>

<summary>This page defines the "self-sufficient" as a being that contains its own cause of goodness, placing it higher than dependent beings but lower than the Absolute Good itself. It establishes a hierarchy of reality based on how close a being is to the source of all perfection.</summary>

<keywords>self-sufficiency, The Good, Proclus, Neoplatonism, participation, perfection, hierarchy of being, causality</keywords>
