<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus regarding the nature of the "Good" and the "One," specifically concluding Proposition 10 and beginning Proposition 11 on the nature of causality.</meta>

-># PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" (Diadochus) as the head of Plato’s Academy in Athens. This text is from his *Elements of Theology*, a foundational work of Neoplatonism.</note>

...ness, nor sufficient; for it would be full of the good, and not the good itself primarily. <note original: "nem, nec sufficiens; esset enim plenum bono, & non ipsum bonum primo">This is the conclusion of the previous argument stating that the ultimate principle cannot just *have* goodness, it must *be* Goodness itself.</note>

## CONCERNING CAUSE.

### PROPOSITION 11.

**All beings proceed from one first cause.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

Either there is no cause for any of the things that exist, or causes move in a circle (where things cause each other), or—since all things are finite—there is an infinite ascent where one thing is the cause of another and the "pre-subsistence" of essence never comes to a halt.

But if there were no cause for any of the beings, there would be no order of secondary and primary things; of those that bring to perfection and those being perfected; of those that adorn and those being adorned; of those that give birth and those being born; or of those that act and those that are acted upon. Furthermore, there would be no <term>science</term> <note>The Latin 'scientia' refers to "knowledge through causes." To the ancients, you do not truly know a thing until you understand what produced it.</note> of any beings. For the work of science is the knowledge of causes; we say we know something only when we recognize the causes of beings.

If, however, causes move in a circle, the same things will be both prior and posterior, and both more powerful and weaker. For every producing thing is superior to the nature of the thing produced. It makes no difference whether one connects a cause to its effect through many or few intermediate steps—it is still placed under that cause. For the cause will be superior to all the intermediates it produces, and the more intermediates there are, the more it acts as a cause.

If, instead, there is an infinite addition of causes, where one always comes from another, then once again there will be no science of anything. For there is no knowledge of infinite things. If the causes are unknown, there can be no science of the things that follow them either.

Therefore, if there must be a cause for beings, and causes are distinct from the things they cause, and there is no infinite ascent, then there must be a **first cause** of beings. From this first cause, as if from a root, each thing proceeds: some things existing close to it, and others further away. For it has already been demonstrated that there must be a single principle, because every <term>multitude</term> <note>In this philosophy, 'multitude' refers to the diversity of the physical and spiritual world, which must logically be preceded by a single unity (The One).</note> exists as a secondary thing arising from the One.

->2 u<-
<note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the book in the correct order.</note>

->CONCERNING<-
<note>This is a "catchword," the first word of the following page, used to ensure the pages were printed and bound in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>In Proposition 11, Proclus uses a logical "trilemma" to prove that all existence must stem from a single First Cause, arguing that without such a starting point, knowledge and the natural order of the universe would be impossible.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, First Cause, Causality, Hierarchy of Being, The One, Logic, Philosophy of Science, Elements of Theology</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," specifically moving from the general nature of causes to the identification of the First Cause as the "Good."</meta>

->THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 4<-

->ON THE GOOD.<-

### PROPOSITION 12.

**The principle and primary cause of all beings is the Good itself.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

IF all things proceed from a single cause, we must call that cause either the Good itself, or something superior to the Good. But if that cause is superior to the Good, does anything pass from it into beings and into the nature of beings, or does nothing pass? 

Indeed, if nothing passes, it is an absurdity; for we would no longer be maintaining it in the rank of a cause <note>Proclus argues that a cause, by definition, must impart some characteristic to its effects.</note>, since it is necessary that something from the cause be present in everything caused by it—especially from the primary cause, upon which all things depend, and through which each individual being exists. 

But if beings also have a <term>participation</term> <note>Participation is a Platonic concept where lower things "take part in" or reflect the qualities of a higher, perfect Form.</note> in that primary cause, just as they do in the Good, then there will be something superior to goodness penetrating into beings from that first cause. For, being superior to and above the Good, it would not bestow something inferior upon the secondary things than the gifts which the cause following it <note>i.e., the Good</note> bestows. 

Yet what could possibly be superior to goodness itself? We use the word "superior" <note>original: *praestantius*</note> to describe that which participates more fully in the Good. Therefore, if that which is not good cannot be called "superior" to the Good, it is entirely necessary that the Good is the first cause. 

Furthermore, if all beings desire <note>original: *appetunt*; the Greek philosophical idea that all things have a natural "longing" or "striving" toward their source and perfection.</note> the Good, how can there be anything prior to this cause? For if that prior cause is also desired, how is it not the Good in the highest degree? But if it is not desired, how is it that beings do not desire the very cause from which they proceed? Therefore, if it is the Good itself upon which all beings depend, then the principle and most primary cause of all is the Good itself.

### PROPOSITION 13.

**Every good thing has the power to unify those that participate in it, and every union is a good; and the Good itself is identical with the One itself.**

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "Diadochi". This title means "The Successor," referring to Proclus's role as the head of Plato's Academy in Athens during the 5th century.</note>

## DEMONSTRATION.
<note>This section concludes the proof for Proposition 13: "All that is good is capable of unifying its recipients, and all unification is a good; and the Good is the same as the One."</note>

**S**ince the <term>Good</term><note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, the "Good" is the ultimate source of all value and existence.</note> is the preservative of all beings, and for that reason is desired by all, it follows that the One itself is the preservative and container of the essence of each individual thing. (For by the One all things are preserved) and dispersal removes individual things from their essence. The Good itself makes those things one to which it is present, and it holds them together through union. But if the <term>One</term><note>The "One" is the first principle of all things, representing absolute simplicity and unity.</note> is that which gathers and contains, it will perfect each being through its presence. Therefore, for all things, to be unified is good. If indeed union is a good in itself, and the Good is a unifying force, then the "simply good" and the "simply one" are the same thing, unifying and making beings good at the same time. Whence also, those things which fall away from the Good in some way are also deprived of participation in the One; and those things which become devoid of the One, being filled with separation, are likewise deprived of the Good.

## COROLLARY.
**E**videntially, then, goodness is union and union is goodness; the Good is the One, and the One is primarily the Good.

## CONCERNING THE IMMOVABLE AND SELF-MOVING PRINCIPLE, OR CAUSE.
### PROPOSITION XIV.
**E**very being is either <term>immovable</term><note>Something that does not change or move in any way, usually referring to divine or purely intellectual realities.</note> or moved; and if it is moved, it is moved either by itself or by another.

## DEMONSTRATION.
**A**nd if it is moved by itself, it is <term>self-moving</term><note>original: "semobile". In Greek philosophy, this is the "autokineton," a being that possesses the source of its own motion, often identified with the Soul.</note>; but if it is moved by another, it is (so to speak) <term>moved-by-another</term><note>original: "alimobile". This refers to physical objects or bodies that cannot move unless pushed or pulled by something else.</note>. Everything, therefore, is either immovable, self-moving, or moved-by-another. For it is necessary, since there are things moved-by-another, that there is also that which is immovable, and between these, the self-moving. For if every thing moved-by-another is moved by something else that is itself in motion, then the motions must either go in a circle or proceed to infinity. But they go neither in a circle nor to infinity, since all beings are limited by a <term>principle</term><note>original: "principio". Here meaning a starting point or first cause.</note>, and the mover is superior to the thing it moves. There will be, therefore, something immovable which is the first mover. Truly, if this is so, it is also necessary for there to be a self-moving thing. For if all things were at a standstill, what would be the first thing to be moved? It would not be the immovable itself, for it is not by nature suited to be moved. Nor would it be the thing moved-by-another, for that requires another to move it. It remains, therefore, that the self-moving is that which first...

->**CONCERNING INCORPOREAL ESSENCE, AND WHAT IT IS**<-

### PROPOSITION XV.
**Everything that reverts back into itself is incorporeal.**
<note>original: "in se conuertitur." In this context, "reversion" describes a being’s ability to turn its attention, power, or consciousness back toward its own source or essence—a key trait of the soul and mind.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For no physical body is by its nature capable of reverting into itself. If that which turns toward something is joined to that toward which it turns, then it is clearly necessary that all parts of a body would be joined to all other parts of it in the case of something that turns into itself. For this is what it means to "revert into itself": when both things—that which is being turned and that toward which it is turned—become one. 

However, this is impossible in a physical body, and generally in all divisible things <note>A "divisible" thing is anything made of parts that occupy different points in space.</note>. For a divisible thing cannot be joined as a whole to itself as a whole, because its parts are separated and located in different places. Therefore, no body is naturally suited to revert into itself in such a way that the whole is turned toward the whole. If, therefore, anything is capable of reverting into itself, it is incorporeal and indivisible.

### PROPOSITION XVI.
**Everything that reverts back into itself has an essence separate from every body.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were inseparable from any body, it would not possess any activity that is separable from the body. It is impossible, for if...

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was one of the last great Greek philosophers and the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. "Diadochus" means "Successor," signifying his role as the inheritor of Plato’s intellectual lineage.</note>

...for if an essence were inseparable from bodies, it would be impossible for its essential activity to be separable. In such a case, the activity would be superior to the essence; for if the essence requires a body while the activity is self-sufficient, then the activity would belong to itself and not to the body. 

Therefore, if a thing is inseparable according to its essence, it is likewise—or even more so—inseparable according to its activity. But if this is so, it does not **revert to itself**. <term>Revert to itself</term> <note>original: "in se convertitur." This is the Neoplatonic concept of *epistrophē*, where a higher being—like a soul or intellect—"turns back" to contemplate its own nature and its divine source rather than being distracted by the physical world.</note> For that which reverts to itself, being something distinct from the body, possesses an activity separate from the body; this activity is performed neither through the body nor with the body, since both the activity and the object of that activity have no need of the body. Therefore, that which reverts to itself is entirely separable from bodies.

**Everything that primarily moves itself is capable of reverting to itself.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it moves itself, its motive activity is also directed toward itself, and the mover and the moved are one and the same. For either it moves by means of one part and is moved in another part, or the whole moves and is moved, or the whole...

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Proposition 18 from the preceding page, which argues that a cause must possess a quality more perfectly than the thing that receives it.</meta>

-># THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 6<-

the creator of anything is more excellent than the nature of the thing created. Therefore, that which pre-exists in the giver is more sublime than the gift itself. Indeed, the giver is what that gift is, but not in the same way; for the giver is that quality primarily, while the gift is that quality secondarily. 

For it is necessary that either both are the same and share one definition, or that they have nothing in common, or that one is primary and the other secondary. However, if the definition were the same, one would no longer be the cause and the other the effect; the quality would not exist in itself in one instance and as a gift in the other, nor would one be the maker and the other the made. 

If they have nothing in common, the one would by no means exist simply because the other exists, as it would communicate nothing to the other's being. It remains, therefore, that the quality bestowed is primary in the giver and secondary in the recipient; in these cases, the giver is one thing in its own being, while in the other, the quality is granted as a gift.

## PROPOSITION XIX.
Everything that primarily exists in any nature of beings is present to all things ordered according to 그 nature, in one definition and in the same way. <note>Proclus is arguing for "essential" properties. If a quality defines a specific category of being (like "rationality" for humans), then every member of that category must possess it in the same way by definition.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it were not present to all in the same way, but to some and not to others, it would be clear that it did not exist primarily in that nature. Instead, it would exist primarily in some things, and secondarily in others which only participate in it at certain times. For that which exists at one time but not at another does not exist primarily nor through itself; rather, it is accidental <note>adventitium: literally "coming from outside"; a property that is not part of a thing's core essence.</note> and comes from elsewhere to those things in which it is found, etc.

## PROPOSITION XX.
The essence of the Soul is superior to all bodies; the Intellectual nature <note>term: Intellectual (Intellectualis) — In Neoplatonic thought, this refers to the 'Nous' or Divine Mind, a level of reality higher than the individual soul.</note> is superior to all souls; and the One itself is superior to all intellectual substances.

<meta>This page concludes Proposition 20 and begins Proposition 21 of Proclus's "Elements of Theology." It continues the argument regarding the hierarchy of existence, moving from the physical body to the soul, then to the Intellect, and finally to the One.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...[arises] from itself, and lives through the soul, and while the soul is indeed present, it is in a certain way <term>self-moving</term><note>Latin: "semobile." In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to a being that has the source of its motion within itself, unlike physical objects which must be moved by something else.</note>, but when the soul is absent, it is moved by another. It is as if the body has this dependent nature, while the soul has been allotted a self-moving essence. For to whatever the soul is added, it imparts this self-motion. That which is imparted by the soul’s very being must exist in the soul much earlier. Therefore, the soul is above bodies, being self-moving by its very essence, while bodies are made self-moving only through participation in the soul.

Again, the soul, being moved by itself, holds the second rank after the <term>immobile nature</term><note>Meaning a reality that is perfectly stable and does not change or "move" in its thinking or being.</note>, which exists as an immobile act. This is because in the order of things, the self-moving precedes all things that are moved by others, but the immobile precedes all things that move others. If, therefore, the soul is moved by itself and moves other things, there must be something before it that moves others without being moved itself.

This is the <term>Mind</term><note>Latin: "Mens," translating the Greek "Nous," often rendered as "Intellect." In this system, Mind is a level of reality higher than the soul because it grasps all truth instantly and unchangingly.</note>, which moves while remaining immobile, always acting in the same way. For the soul, through the Mind, participates in "always understanding," just as the body, through the soul, participates in moving itself. For if "always understanding" belonged to the soul primarily, it would belong to all souls, just as the capacity for self-motion does. Therefore, this quality does not belong to the soul primarily. It is necessary, then, that there be a "primary intellectual being" before the soul.

Thus, before souls, there is the Mind. But truly, even before the Mind, there is **the One**. For although the Mind is immobile, it is not "the One" itself. The Mind understands itself and acts upon itself <note>Because "thinking" involves a distinction between the thinker and the thought, the Mind contains a subtle form of plurality and cannot be the ultimate, simple source.</note>. However, all things—in whatever way they exist—participate in the One, but not all things participate in the Mind. For only those things that possess a portion of Mind must necessarily participate in knowledge, because intellectual knowledge is the principle and first cause of knowing. Therefore, the One is superior to the Mind. Nor is there anything higher than the One. For the One and the **Good itself** are the same. And the Good itself is the principle of all things, as has been demonstrated.

### THAT THE MIND IS NOT THE FIRST CAUSE.

**E**very order, beginning from a unity, proceeds into a certain multitude that shares the nature of that unity; and every multitude of an order is reduced back into a single unity.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or unity, having the character of a principle, produces its own proper multitude. Accordingly, one chain and one order...

It then introduces the principle that all complex systems or "multitudes" must originate from and return to a single unifying principle.</summary>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of Neoplatonic metaphysics, specifically focusing on the relationship between Unity (The One) and Multiplicity within various "orders" of reality (Nature, Soul, Mind).</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 7<-

Every order, in its entirety, has its descent from unity into multiplicity. For there would be no order, nor a <term>chain</term> <note>original: "catena" — in Neoplatonic thought, this refers to the "Golden Chain" or series of beings descending from a single source</note>, if unity were sterile by itself. Indeed, multiplicity is led back again into a single common cause of all coordinated things. For that which is the same in every multiplicity did not proceed from just one of those things within the multiplicity. For that which comes from only one out of many is not common to all, but is the specific property of that one alone. Therefore, since within every order there is a certain communion, coherence, and identity—because of which some things are called "coordinated" and others are said to belong to a different order—it is clear that the identity of every order proceeds from a single principle. Thus, there is one unity before the multiplicity in every order, providing a single logic and series to the things ordered within it, both toward each other and toward the whole. Let one thing be the cause of another among those under the same chain; however, for it to be a single chain, it is necessary for the cause to exist before all things, and for all things to be produced by it as coordinated—not merely as each thing is a distinct entity, but as an existing part of this specific order.

->COROLLARY.<-

From these points it is certainly clear that both unity and multiplicity exist within the nature of the body; a single nature has many coherent parts, and many natures constitute the single nature of the universe. It is also present in the order of souls: to begin from one first soul, to descend into a multiplicity of souls, and to reduce that multiplicity back to one. And in the intellectual essence, there is an intellectual unity; a multiplicity of minds proceeds from one mind and is turned back toward it. And for the One <note>The "One" here refers to the ultimate principle of reality, the source of all existence</note> that is before all things, there is a multiplicity of unities, and for those unities, a return into the One. Therefore, after the One which is first, there are unities; and after the first Mind, there are minds; and after the first Soul, there are souls; and after the universal Nature, there are natures.

### PROPOSITION 22.

**Everything that exists primarily and principally, according to each individual order, is one, and neither two, nor more than two. Instead, everything is uniquely begotten.** <note>original: "unigenitum" — in this philosophical context, it refers to a single, unique manifestation that heads its own series or order</note>

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic principle that every group of similar things (an "order") must originate from a single unit and eventually return to it. It establishes a hierarchy where a single Nature, Soul, and Mind act as the sources for the many natures, souls, and minds that exist in the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Unity, Multiplicity, The One, Hierarchy of Being, Neoplatonism, Chain of Being, Universal Nature, First Mind, First Soul, Unigenitum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of the "One" and the hierarchy of being, likely from Proclus's *Elements of Theology*.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. He was called "The Successor" (Diadochos) because he held the chair once occupied by Plato.</note>

## PROOF

Suppose, if it were possible, that there are two primary causes—or even if we were to grant that there are many. This leads to an impossibility. Indeed, it remains impossible even if there are many. For either each of these causes is that which results from both, but if that which results from both is a single thing, then we have one cause again and not two primary ones. If, however, they are distinct, then either one comes from the other—in which case both are not "first"—or they are both equal. But if they are equal, neither will be truly "first." For if one were "first" in a way that is not identical to the other, what would define that order? <note>Proclus is using a "reductio ad absurdum" argument: if you have two things claiming to be "first," there must be a third thing—a shared rule or nature—that makes them both "first," and that third thing would actually be the true "First."</note> That which is nothing other than what it is called, that is what is "first." Each of these, however, being different from the other, both "is" and "is not" what it is called at the same time. If these causes truly differ from one another, but do not differ in their capacity as the "primary cause," then that primary nature is a single shared experience. Thus, both would not be the first, but rather that single thing in which they both participate would be the first.

## COROLLARY

From these points, it is surely clear that the **primary Being** is only one, and there are not two primary beings, nor many. And the **primary Mind** is one alone, and not two primary minds. And the **primary Soul** is one. The same applies to every individual species: such as the **primary Beautiful**, the **primary Equal**, and so on for all things. In this same way, the **Form of the Animal** is one primary thing, as is the **Form of Man**; for the demonstration is the same in every case. <note>In Neoplatonic thought, every category of existence has a single, perfect "Monad" or source at the top of its hierarchy.</note>

## ON THE UNPARTICIPATED, THE PARTICIPANT, AND THE PARTICIPATED

### PROPOSITION 23

Every <term>unparticipated</term> <note>original: "imparticipabile"; This refers to a pure principle that remains transcendent and distinct, rather than being "divided up" among the things that depend on it.</note> principle produces from itself those things which <term>participate</term> in it; and all participating substances are traced back to unparticipated existences.

## PROOF

For the unparticipated, possessing the nature of unity—as something existing for itself and not belonging to another—is separate from those things that participate in it. <note>The text breaks off here; the proof would likely continue to explain how a source must be "unmixed" to be the origin of many shared instances.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that there can only be one ultimate source for any category of existence (Being, Mind, Soul) and introduces the relationship between transcendent "unparticipated" sources and the world of "participating" things.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, Primary Cause, Unparticipated, Participation, Metaphysics, Platonic Forms</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 23 regarding the nature of participation and the hierarchy of being in Neoplatonic metaphysics, specifically focusing on how causes relate to their effects.</meta>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 8

...[it] brings forth those things which are able to participate. For either it will remain sterile in itself and possess nothing honorable, or it will give something from itself. And that which receives, participates; and that which is given, exists by way of participation. But everything participating in something is secondary to that by which it is made a participant, which is present to all things alike and fills all things from itself. For that which is in one is not in others; and that which is present to all alike, so that it illuminates all, is not in one, but before all things. For it is either in all, or in one of all, or before all. But that which is in all, divided among all, will again require another to unite that which is divided, and no longer will all things participate in the same thing; rather, this [participant] will participate in one part, and that [participant] in another, if the "one" is divided. If, however, it is in only one of all, it will no longer belong to all, but to that one. If, therefore, it is both common to those able to participate and the same for all, it will be before all things, and this is <term>unparticipated</term> <note>Original Latin: "imparticipabile"; Greek: "amethekton." This refers to a Form or Principle that remains pure and transcendent, acting as a source without being "used up" or divided by the things that depend on it.</note>.

### PROPOSITION XXIV.

**Every participant is inferior to that which is participated, and the participated is inferior to the unparticipated.**

->**DEMONSTRATION.**<-

For the participant, being imperfect before participation but made perfect by participation, is altogether secondary to the <term>participated</term> <note>Original Latin: "participato"; Greek: "metechomenon." This is the quality or essence as it exists within a specific thing (like the beauty in a flower).</note>, insofar as it is a perfected participant. For insofar as it was imperfect, it is inferior to the participated which makes it perfect. 

Moreover, the participated, belonging to a specific thing and not to all—whereas the unparticipated belongs to all and not to a specific one—receives in turn a more diminished existence. For the latter <note>the unparticipated</note> is more closely related to the cause of all, while the former <note>the participated</note> is less related. 

Therefore, the unparticipated precedes the participated, and the participated precedes the <term>participants</term> <note>Original Latin: "participantibus"; Greek: "metechonta." The individual beings that receive a share of a higher property.</note>. And to put it briefly: The unparticipated is the "one before the many"; the participated is "in the many," being both one and not-one at the same time; and every participant is "not-one and one" at the same time.

<summary>This page establishes a three-tiered hierarchy of existence: the transcendent "Unparticipated" source, the "Participated" property that exists within things, and the "Participant" being itself. This structure explains how a single cause (like Beauty or Goodness) can affect many different things without losing its own unity.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Participation, Unparticipated, Participated, Participant, Hierarchy of Being, Metaphysics, The One and the Many</keywords>

8 PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato.</note>

->ON PERFECTION.<-

## PROPOSITION 25.

**Every perfect being proceeds into offspring which it is able to produce, imitating the one principle of all things.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For just as that <note>The "One" or the ultimate Source in Neoplatonism.</note>, because of its own goodness, is the uniform creator of all beings—for the **Good** itself and the **One** itself are the same, such that the "good-form" and the "one-form" are identical—so too those things that come after it hasten to generate other things because of their own perfection, without their own <term>essence</term> <note>The fundamental nature or "being" of a thing that remains unchanged even when it creates something else.</note> being diminished. This perfection is a certain portion of the Good itself; and the perfect, insofar as it is perfect, imitates the Good. 

Now, the Good was the creator of all things. Therefore, the perfect is naturally productive of those things which it is capable of producing. Indeed, the more perfect a thing is, the more things it is the cause of. For the more perfect a thing is, the more it participates in the Good. That which is closer to the Good is more akin to the <term>cause of all things</term> <note>The primary source from which all reality flows.</note>, and that primary cause is the cause of many things. 

However, the more imperfect a thing is, the fewer things it is the cause of. Being further removed from the producer of all, it has the power to generate fewer things. For to create, or to adorn, or to perfect, or to sustain, or to give life, or to act—and to do each of these things across many instances—is akin to the Source. But if a thing acts upon only a few, it is more "alien" to that source, etc.

### COROLLARY.

From these points it is indeed clear that **matter**, which is furthest from the principle of all things, is sterile and the cause of nothing. For if it were to generate anything, it would have something else coming after it, and it is clear that it would then not be the "furthest" thing. Rather, what it produced would be further than it, while matter itself would be closer to the source by virtue of its production and its imitation of the productive cause of all beings.

IC                                                                                             DE

<summary>Proclus argues that perfection is naturally creative; the more "perfect" a being is, the more it imitates the ultimate Source (the One/Good) by generating effects without losing its own essence. Consequently, matter is defined as the most imperfect level of reality because it is sterile and produces nothing further.</summary>

# THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 9

->ON THE PRODUCER<-

## PROPOSITION 26.

**Every productive cause of other things, while remaining itself within itself, produces those things that follow it and which come next in succession.** <note>This principle describes "emanation," a central idea in Neoplatonism where the source of existence flows outward like light from a candle without the candle being used up.</note>

### PROOF

For if the producer imitates the One <note>original: "vnum ipsum"; the ultimate, indivisible source of all reality in this philosophy.</note> itself—and the One produces the things that follow it without moving—then every producer also possesses its manner of producing in that same way. Indeed, the One itself produces immovably. For if it produced through motion, that motion would either be within the One or outside of it. If the One moved, it would no longer be truly "one," having changed from its unified state; furthermore, that motion would itself require a prior "one" to cause it. Thus, the One produces either in an infinite sequence of motions or, as is the case, it produces immovably.

And every producer will imitate the One and the universal productive cause. For from that which is "first," that which is "not first" <note>The "not first" refers to secondary or derived beings.</note> arises. In the same way, from that which is the producer of all things arises that which is the producer of only some things. 

Therefore, every producer produces those things that follow while it remains within itself. Consequently, while the producers remain in themselves without being diminished <note>original: "non imminutis"; meaning the source does not lose any of its power or substance by creating something else.</note>, the secondary things are produced by them. For if something is diminished in any way, it is impossible for it to remain exactly as it is.

## PROPOSITION.

<summary>This page argues that all creative causes produce their effects without being diminished or changed, imitating the "One" which creates all of reality while remaining perfectly still and complete.</summary>
<keywords>The One, Causality, Emanation, Production, Motion, Immobility, Neoplatonism, Proclus</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
->**PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR**<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. This text is from his *Elements of Theology*, a central work of Neoplatonic philosophy.</note>

...[multiplied by] two hundred, is the product itself. For this does not belong to generated things, nor to the generating causes. Nor is it a transition <note>original: "transitus"</note>, for the progression does not happen through matter. For it remains as it is. And the product is something other than the cause itself. Therefore, the generator remains unchangeable and undiminished, and by means of a secondary power <term>potentia: the inherent capacity of a higher being to produce lower effects</term> it multiplies itself, providing secondary substances from itself.

## PROPOSITION XXVIII.

**Every producer brings forth things similar to itself before it brings forth the dissimilar.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

Since the producer is necessarily superior to the product, they can never be simply identical or equal to one another in terms of power. But if they are not identical and equal, but rather different and unequal, they are either entirely separated from one another, or they are both united and separated. 

However, if they were entirely separated, they would not come together, and that which comes from the cause would in no way be <term>sympathetic: a Greek philosophical concept (sympathes) meaning a natural resonance or "feeling-together" between parts of the universe</term> to the cause itself. Therefore, one would not participate in the other <note>In Platonic thought, "participation" is the way a lower thing shares in the qualities of a higher reality</note>, since they would be entirely different. For that which is participated bestows a sharing upon the participant in that which it partakes. 

But it is necessary for the effect to be a participant in the cause, as it is from there that it derives its essence <note>original: "essentiam"</note>. If, on the other hand, the product is in some way separated and in some way united to the producer; if it experiences both equally, it is equally a participant and not a participant. And so it will both have its essence from the cause and not have it, in the same manner. 

If, however, it were more separated, the thing begotten would be more alien than proper to the begetter, and more discordant than harmonious, and more unsympathetic than sympathetic. 

If, therefore, the things that come from causes are kindred in their being and sympathetic to them, and depend on them by nature, and desire contact with them—desiring the Good and attaining what is Desirable through the cause—it is certainly clear that products are more united to their producers than they are separated from them. And those things which are more united are more similar than they are dissimilar to those with which they are most united. 

Therefore, every productive cause brings forth things that are similar to itself before it brings forth the dissimilar.

<meta>This page continues the logical sequence of Proclus's metaphysical system, moving from the general nature of causes to the specific mechanics of how effects relate to their causes through the triad of "remaining," "proceeding," and "reverting."</meta>

->THE ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 10<-

### PROPOSITION XXIX.

**All progression is accomplished through the likeness of the secondary things to the primary.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the producing cause <note>original: "producens"</note> generates similar things before it generates dissimilar things, then it is likeness that brings forth the produced things from the producers. For similar things are made similar through a state of likeness, and not through unlikeness. If, therefore, the <term>progression</term> <note>In Neoplatonism, "progression" or "procession" is the way higher principles flow down to create lower levels of reality without losing their own nature.</note> into a diminished state preserves the identity of that which is generated to that which generates it—and just as the cause exists primarily, it brings forth that which follows after it in a secondary way—it possesses its substance through likeness.

### PROPOSITION XXX.

**Everything produced immediately by something both remains in its producer and proceeds from it.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if every progression occurs while the primary things remain <note>original: "manentibus primis"</note>, and is accomplished through likeness—with similar things subsisting before dissimilar things—then the product also <term>remains</term> <note>The concept of "remaining" suggests that an effect never fully "leaves" its cause; it stays rooted in the power that created it.</note> in some measure in the producer. For that which proceeds entirely would have nothing in common with that which remains, but would be entirely separate. 

However, if it has something in common and unified with the producer, it will remain in that producer just as the producer was remaining in itself. On the other hand, if it were only to remain and not proceed, it would not differ from its cause, nor would it be a distinct effect while the cause remains. For if it is "another" thing, it is separated and distinct. But if it is distinct while the cause remains, then it has <term>proceeded</term> from the remaining cause so as to be separated from it. 

Insofar, therefore, as the product has something identical to the producer, it remains in it. Insofar as it is "another" thing, it proceeds from it. Since it exists as something "similar," it is in some measure both identical and "another" at the same time. It therefore remains and proceeds simultaneously, and neither state exists apart from the other.

### PROPOSITION XXXI.

**Everything proceeding from something according to its essence <term>reverts</term> <note>original: "convertitur" — the Greek "epistrophe." This is the third stage of existence where an effect "turns back" toward its source to achieve its own perfection.</note> toward that from which it proceeds.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

<summary>This page defines the relationship between causes and their effects, arguing that effects are created through likeness and exist in a state of simultaneously staying within their cause and moving away from it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the metaphysical "procession" (coming into being from a source) and "return" (the movement back toward that source) of all things. It specifically focuses on the relationship between causes and effects in the Neoplatonic system.</meta>

-># PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
->### PROOF<-

For if a thing were to proceed forth, but did not return <term>return: originally *conversio* (Greek *epistrophē*), the spiritual or metaphysical movement of an effect turning back toward its source to achieve fulfillment</term> to the cause of this procession, it would not desire its cause. For everything that desires is turned toward that which is desirable. But every being desires the Good, and the attainment of that Good is achieved by each thing through its nearest cause. Therefore, all things desire their own cause. For that by which "being" is given to each thing is the same as that by which "well-being" <note>original: *bene esse*</note> is given; and that toward which well-being is first directed is the primary object of desire; and that which is the primary object of desire is also the goal of the return.

### PROPOSITION 32.
Every return is effected through a likeness between the things returning and that toward which they return.

### PROOF.
For everything that returns to another hastens to join itself to it, and desires communion and a bond with it. Now, it is likeness that binds all things together, just as unlikeness separates and divides them. Return, therefore, is a kind of communion and contact. But all communion and all contact occur through likeness. Therefore, every return is effected through likeness.

### PROPOSITION 33.
Everything that proceeds from something and returns to it has a circular activity.

### PROOF.
For if it returns to that from which it proceeds, it joins the end to the beginning. The movement is one and continuous—one part moving away from the "abiding" source <note>original: *a manente*; Proclus uses a three-part cycle: remaining, proceeding, and returning</note>, the other part moving back toward the abiding source. From this, it is clear that all things proceed in a circle from causes back to causes. There are greater and smaller circles: some returns are made to the immediate causes situated directly above, while others are to higher causes, and even up to the Principle of all things. For from that Principle all things come, and to it all return.

### PROPOSITION 34.
Everything that returns according to its nature makes its return toward that from which it also had the procession of its own substance <term>substance: originally *substantiæ* (Greek *hypostasis*), the essential reality or underlying nature of a thing</term>.

### PROOF.

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 11

### PROOF.
For if something is reverted <note>original: "conuertitur." In this context, "reversion" (epistrophē) refers to the way an effect seeks fulfillment by turning back toward the source that created it.</note> according to its nature, it possesses an essential desire for that toward which it reverts, and it directs its entire being toward that to which it makes its essential reversion. Moreover, it is similar to that cause according to its essence, and thus it has a natural affinity with it <note>original: "sympathes." This refers to the concept of "cosmic sympathy," where parts of the universe are interconnected by their shared origins.</note>, as something related to that essence.

If this is true, then either their essence is the same, or one comes from the other, or both have obtained their similarity from some other third thing. But if their essence were the same, how could one be naturally reverted toward the other? If both came from a single source, then the natural reversion of each would be toward *that* source. Therefore, it remains that one must have its being from the other. If this is the case, then the procession <note>original: "progressio." This is the "outflowing" of reality from a higher source to a lower one.</note> is from that source toward which the natural reversion is directed.

### COROLLARY.
From these points it is clearly evident that the **Mind** <note>original: "mens," translating the Greek "Nous." In this system, Mind is the first level of existence below the absolute "One."</note> is the object of desire for all things; all things proceed from the Mind, and the entire world has its essence from the Mind. This is true even if the world is eternal; it does not fail to proceed from the Mind simply because it is eternal. For it is not the case that it fails to revert because it is always ordered. Rather, it both proceeds always, and is eternal according to its essence; it is reverted always, and is indissoluble according to its order.

## PROPOSITION XXXV.
**Every effect both remains in its cause, proceeds from it, and is reverted to it.**

### PROOF.
For if it only **remains**, it will not differ from its cause, existing as something indistinct from it. For procession occurs simultaneously with distinction.

If, however, it were only to **proceed**, it would be unconnected to the cause and have no affinity with it, sharing in the cause in no way whatsoever.

If, on the other hand, it were only to **revert**, how could that which does not receive its essence from the cause make an essential reversion toward something foreign to it?

If it **remains** and **proceeds**, but does not **revert**, how is there a natural appetite in each thing for "well-being" and for the Good, or an impulse toward that which generated it?

If, however, it **proceeds** and **reverts** but does not **remain**, how— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the argument that things return to what created them and introduces the famous Neoplatonic triad: remaining, proceeding, and reverting. It explains that all things in the universe depend on the "Mind" (Intellect) for their existence and order.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Cause and Effect, Procession, Reversion, Mind (Nous), Elements of Theology, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's systematic exposition of Neoplatonic metaphysics, specifically detailing the "triad" of remaining, procession, and reversion that governs how all reality flows from and returns to its source.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" (Diadochos) because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens centuries after Plato.</note>

how, since it stands apart from the cause, does it hasten to be joined to it? It would be unjoinable if it were so before the departure. For if it were joined, it would remain entirely according to that [cause]. If indeed it remains and **reverts** <note>original: "cōuertatur" (convertatur). In this philosophy, "reversion" is the internal desire of a thing to return to its divine origin.</note> but does not meet [the cause], how can it be reverted without being separated? For everything that reverts is assimilated into that to which it returns, from which it was divided according to its essence. It is necessary, however, either to **remain** alone, or to **revert** alone, or to **proceed** <note>original: "progredi." This refers to "procession," the way higher realities produce lower ones.</note> alone, or to bind the extremes together, or the middle between both extremes, or all of them together. It remains, therefore, that everything both **remains** in its cause, **proceeds** from it, and **reverts** to it.

### PROPOSITION XXXVI.
Of all things that are multiplied in <term>procession</term> <note>The outward movement or "flow" from a single cause into many effects.</note>, the first are more **perfect** than the second, and the second than those after them, and so on in like manner.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
FOR if the progressions of things produced distinguish them from their causes, then the second things are lower than the first. Those things that first proceeded are joined more closely to their causes, having been born directly from them. But the second things are further from the causes, and so on in order. Those that are nearer and more akin to the causes are more **perfect**. Indeed, causes are more perfect than the things they cause; therefore, those things further away are more imperfect, being already unlike their causes.

### PROPOSITION XXXVII.
Of all things that subsist according to <term>reversion</term> <note>The upward movement of an effect back toward its source to achieve its own completion.</note>, the first are more **imperfect** than the second, and the second than those following; and the last are the most perfect.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
FOR if reversions occur according to a circle—and the point from which the procession began is also the point where the reversion ends—and since procession begins from the most perfect, then reversion is toward the most **perfect**. And if that which the procession reaches is the last [and lowest] thing, then the first stage of reversion begins from this. But procession into the last is the most **imperfect**, and thus reversion begins from the most imperfect. Therefore, the first things in the order of reversion are the most imperfect, while the last are the most perfect.

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic cycle of existence: everything remains in its cause, proceeds out of it, and reverts back to it. It establishes that in the "downward" flow of creation, the first things are the best, while in the "upward" return to God, the final stage is the most perfect.</summary>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 12

### PROPOSITION XXXVIII.
**Every thing that proceeds from several causes reverts through as many stages as it progressed; and every reversion occurs through the same stages as the procession.** <note>original: "OMne proueniẽs... per quot progreditur, per totidem etiam conuertitur." This refers to the Neoplatonic idea of a "great chain of being" where the path back to the Divine source mirrors the path down into material existence.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.
Since both <term>procession</term> <note>The outward flow of existence from a higher cause to a lower effect.</note> and <term>reversion</term> <note>The "turning back" or spiritual return of an effect toward its cause to find its perfection.</note> occur through likeness, that which has proceeded immediately from a source also reverts immediately to it; for the likeness was immediate. However, that which requires a medium <note>An intermediate step or middle-man in the chain of existence.</note> in its procession also requires a medium in its reversion. It is necessary for both movements to occur toward the same point. Therefore, it will revert first to the medium, and then to that which is higher than the medium. Thus, for each thing, its **being** <note>original: "esse," referring to its existence.</note> is achieved through as many steps as its **well-being**; <note>original: "bene esse," referring to its spiritual perfection or completion.</note> and vice versa.

### PROPOSITION XXXIX.
**Every being reverts either essentially only, or vitally, or also cognitively.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For a thing possesses from its cause either its **being** alone, or **life** along with its being, or it has also received from there a **cognitive** power. Insofar as it only *is*, it performs an essential reversion. Insofar as it *lives*, it also performs a vital reversion. Insofar as it *knows*, it also performs a cognitive reversion. For as it has proceeded, so it reverts; and the measures of its reversion are defined by the measures of its procession. Therefore, the "appetite" <note>original: "appetitus." In this context, it means a natural tendency or gravitational pull toward a higher source.</note> or desire for these things is, for some, according to their **being** alone (an existing fitness for participating in their causes); for others, it is according to **life** (which is a movement toward higher things); and for others, it is according to **cognition** (a conscious sense of the goodness of their causes).

### PROPOSITION XL.
**All things that proceed from another cause are preceded by those things that are self-constituted and possess a self-subsisting essence.**
#### DEMON- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "DEMONSTRATIO."</note>

<summary>This page explains the Neoplatonic law of symmetry: the return of a soul or entity to its divine source must follow the exact same steps as its original creation. It also categorizes the types of return (essential, vital, or cognitive) based on the nature of the being.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Procession, Reversion, Well-being, Essence, Self-subsisting, Causality</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically concluding the demonstration for Proposition 40 and beginning Proposition 41. It focuses on the nature of "self-subsistent" entities—those that cause their own existence—within the Neoplatonic hierarchy.</meta>

->## PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
->### DEMONSTRATION<-

For if everything that is **self-sufficient**, whether according to its essence or according to its action, is more excellent than that which depends on another cause; and if that which produces itself is productive of its own being and thus **self-sufficient** for its essence, whereas that which is produced by another alone is not self-sufficient; then it follows that the self-sufficient is more closely related to **the Good**. <note>original: "ipsum bonum" — Proclus refers here to the ultimate principle of the universe, often called "The One," which is the source of all value and existence.</note> Now, things that are more closely related and more similar to their causes exist from that cause before those things that are dissimilar. Therefore, those things that are produced by themselves and are **self-subsistent** <term>self-subsistent: translates the Latin *per se subsistens*, referring to an entity that is the cause of its own existence, a key concept in Proclus's hierarchy of being</term> are more ancient than those that proceed into being from another cause alone. 

For either nothing is self-subsistent, or **the Good** itself is such, or the first things that subsist from the Good are self-subsistent. However, if nothing were self-subsistent, then in truth nothing would be self-sufficient—not even in the Good itself. For that ultimate principle is actually superior to self-sufficiency, being the One itself; it is the Good itself, but it does not "possess" the good <note>The One is so simple that it does not *have* qualities like goodness; it *is* the Good itself.</note>. Nor would self-sufficiency be found in those things that come after the Good, for all things would be in need of another—namely, the cause that exists before them alone. 

On the other hand, if the Good itself were self-subsistent—meaning it produced itself—it would not be "the One." For that which proceeds from the One is not the One itself. If it were self-subsistent, it would "proceed" from itself; thus, the One would be simultaneously "one" and "not-one." Therefore, it is necessary that the **self-subsistent** entity exists after the first principle <note>The One/The Good</note>. It is clear that it must exist before those things that proceed from another cause alone. For it is more primary than they are, and more closely related to the Good itself, as has been demonstrated.

### PROPOSITION 41

**Everything that exists in another is produced by another alone. But everything that exists in itself is self-subsistent.**

### DEMONSTRATION

For that which exists in another and requires a substrate <note>original: "subiecto" — a literal translation of the Greek *hypokeimenon*, meaning a foundation or underlying material that supports an attribute.</note> could never be the generator of itself. For that which is by nature intended to beget itself does not require a different "seat," since it is contained by itself and is preserved in itself, set apart from those things above it. 

Conversely, that which can remain and stand in itself is productive of itself, proceeding into itself and being capable of containing itself. Thus, it exists in itself as a cause exists in its effect—not as if it were in a physical place, nor as if it were in a substrate. For a place is something other than that which is in the place, and a substrate is different from that which is in the substrate. But this [self-subsistent thing]...

<summary>This page argues that self-subsistent entities (those that cause themselves) must exist as a middle ground between the ultimate One and dependent creatures. It establishes that existing "in oneself" is the defining characteristic of a self-caused being.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, self-subsistent, The Good, The One, causality, self-sufficiency, substrate, Elements of Theology</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of causes and their effects, specifically focusing on "self-subsisting" entities—beings that are their own cause. It transitions from Proposition 41 into the circular relationship between a thing's origin and its ultimate fulfillment.</meta>

-># THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 13<-

...to itself, the same. For that which is self-subsisting <term>self-subsisting: a technical term for a being that is its own cause and does not depend on a higher subject for its existence</term>, and exists as if in a cause, just as that which comes from a cause, exists in this way within itself.

### PROPOSITION 42.
**Everything that is self-subsisting is capable of reverting to itself.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it proceeds <note>original: 'progreditur'; in Neoplatonic thought, "progression" is the process by which a higher reality produces a lower one</note> from itself, it will also perform a reversion <note>original: 'conversionem'; the "return" or "reversion" is the process by which a created thing turns back toward its source to find its perfection</note> to itself. For from whatever source the progression of individual things begins, toward that source the coordinated reversion of the progression is also directed. 

If it were to proceed from itself alone, but did not, in its proceeding, turn back toward itself, it would never seek its own "good" <note>the ultimate goal or perfection of a thing</note>, nor that which it is able to provide for itself. Every cause is able to grant to that which comes from it—along with the essence <note>fundamental nature or being</note> that it gives—a sense of "well-being" <note>original: 'bene esse'</note> that is joined to that essence. Therefore, the self-subsisting thing grants this well-being to itself. 

This is the "good" proper to the self-subsisting being. Yet it will not seek this good unless it turns back toward itself; and by not seeking it, it will not attain it; and by not attaining it, it would be imperfect and not self-sufficient. But if self-sufficiency <note>the ability to lack nothing</note> belongs to anything, it must belong to the self-subsisting; therefore, it will attain its own good, and seek it, and revert to itself.

### PROPOSITION 43.
**Everything that reverts to itself is self-subsisting.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For if it is turned toward itself according to its nature, and is perfected in that return to itself, it must also have its essence <note>its very existence</note> from itself. For to whatever thing a natural reversion is directed, from that same thing an essential progression originates for each being. 

If, therefore, it provides "well-being" to itself, it will surely also provide "being" <note>original: 'esse'</note> to itself, and it will be the source of its own existence. Therefore, that which is able to turn toward itself is self-subsisting.

### PROPOSITION 44.
**Everything that reverts to itself according to its action is also reverted to itself according to its essence.**

### DEMON- <note>The text breaks off here at the start of the word 'DEMONSTRATIO'</note>

<summary>This page outlines the circular logic of self-caused beings, arguing that any entity that creates itself must also return to itself to achieve perfection and self-sufficiency.</summary>
<keywords>self-subsisting, progression, reversion, essence, well-being, self-sufficiency, Neoplatonism, Proclus</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of "self-subsistent" entities. It follows a discussion on how an entity’s action relates to its essence, moving into the arguments for why such beings have no beginning (unbegotten) and no end (incorruptible).</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI." Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the 'Successor' (Diadochos) because he headed the Platonic Academy in Athens centuries after Plato.</note>

## PROOF

For if an entity is able to return to itself through its **action**, but its **essence** does not return to itself, then the action would be superior to the essence. In this scenario, the action would be self-returning, while the essence would not be so at all. 

However, that which belongs to itself is superior to that which belongs only to another. Likewise, that which is capable of preserving itself is more perfect than that which is preserved only by another. If, therefore, an entity returns to itself according to the action that proceeds from its essence, it must also possess a self-returning essence. Thus, it does not merely act toward itself, but it belongs to itself, is contained by itself, and is perfected by itself.

### PROPOSITION 45

**Everything that is self-subsistent is unbegotten.**
<note>original: "ingenitum." In this context, it means something that was not brought into being by an external creator or process, but exists fundamentally.</note>

## PROOF

For if it were begotten, then by the very nature of being begotten, it would be imperfect by itself and in need of the perfection that comes from another. However, because it produces itself, it is perfect and self-sufficient. 

Every begotten thing is perfected by something else that provides existence to that which did not yet exist. Indeed, "generation" is a path from the imperfect to its opposite, the perfect. But if something produces itself, it is always perfect. It is always joined to its own cause—or rather, it is inherent within an essence that provides its own perfection.

### PROPOSITION 46

**Everything that is self-subsistent is incorruptible.**
<note>original: "incorruptibile." This refers to being indestructible or incapable of decaying or ceasing to exist.</note>

## PROOF

For if it were to be corrupted, it would abandon itself and be separated from itself. But this is impossible. Being a single unity, it is simultaneously the cause and the effect. 

Now, everything that is corrupted is corrupted because it departs from its own cause. For as long as a thing remains within that which contains and preserves it, it is kept safe; however, a <term>self-subsistent</term> <note>original: "per se subsistens." A technical term for a being that creates and maintains its own existence.</note> entity never leaves its cause because it does not leave itself. Its cause, you see, is itself. Therefore, everything that is self-subsistent is incorruptible.

### PROPOSITION—
<note>The text cuts off here at the start of the next heading.</note>

<summary>This page argues that any entity which is "self-subsistent" (creates its own existence) must be both "unbegotten" and "incorruptible." Proclus reasons that because these beings are their own cause, they can never be separated from their source of life and perfection.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, self-subsistent, unbegotten, incorruptible, essence vs action, causality, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of the "One" and self-subsisting entities, likely following Proposition 46. The text is part of a Neoplatonic treatise, specifically Proclus's *Elements of Theology*.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 14

### PROPOSITION 47.

Every <term>self-subsisting<note>Latin: *per se subsistens*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to an entity that contains the cause of its own existence within itself, rather than being produced by something else.</note></term> thing is indivisible and **simple**.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For if that which is self-subsisting were divisible, it would constitute itself as something divisible, and the whole would be turned back toward itself, and every part would be in every other part of itself: but this is impossible. Therefore, that which is self-subsisting is indivisible. Moreover, it is also **simple**. For if it were a <term>composite<note>Original: *compositum*. A thing made of multiple different parts or elements.</note></term>, there would be in it one part that is "worse" and another that is "better," and the better would arise from the worse, and the worse from the better, if indeed the whole proceeds from itself as a whole. Furthermore, it would not be self-sufficient, since it would be in need of its own elements from which it is composed. Therefore, everything that is self-subsisting is simple.

->ON THE ETERNAL<-

### PROPOSITION 48.

Everything that is not **eternal** is either a composite or subsists in another.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For such a thing is either dissolvable into the parts from which it is made (and is entirely composed of those things into which it eventually dissolves), or it is in need of a subject and, upon leaving that subject, passes away into non-being. If, however, it is **simple** and exists within itself, it will be indissoluble and incapable of being scattered.

### PROPOSITION 49.

Every self-subsisting thing is **eternal**.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For there are two ways in which it is necessary for something to be non-eternal: the way of composition, and the way of being in another. A self-subsisting thing, however, is neither composite (it is simple) nor is it in another, but rather in itself. Therefore, it is eternal.

FROM THE PROPO-

<summary>This page presents three logical proofs (Propositions 47-49) asserting that self-caused entities must be simple, indivisible, and eternal, as they do not rely on external subjects or internal parts for their existence.</summary>
<keywords>self-subsisting, simplicity, eternity, metaphysics, Neoplatonism, Proclus, indivisibility, composite</keywords>

<meta>This page contains Proposition 50 from Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," a foundational work of Neoplatonic philosophy. It continues Proclus's systematic deduction of the nature of reality, moving here into the distinction between eternal being and temporal existence.</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
### PROPOSITION 50

**EVERYTHING** that is measured by time, whether according to its **essence** <note>original: "essentiam"; the fundamental nature or "what-ness" of a thing</note> or according to its **action**, is a <term>becoming</term> <note>original: "generatio"; in this philosophical context, it refers to the process of "coming-to-be" rather than a fixed state of "being"</note>, insofar as it is measured by time.

### DEMONSTRATION

For if it is measured by time, it is proper for it to exist or to act according to time. Consequently, "was" and "will be" differ from one another. If "was" and "will be" were numerically the same, the thing would have been unaffected by the passage of time, which always possesses a distinct "before" and "after." If, therefore, "was" and "will be" are different, the thing is in a state of <term>becoming</term> <note>original: "in fieri"; the state of being in progress or under construction</note>. It never truly *exists* <note>in the sense of permanent, unchanging existence</note>, but proceeds simultaneously with the time by which it is measured, existing only in the act of being made. It is not fixed in the same state of being, but is always receiving a different and newer existence, so that at any moment, according to time, it is always becoming something else due to the progression of time. Therefore, it is not a "whole" all at once. Rather, it exists within the dispersion of temporal extension and is stretched out alongside it. This is, in truth, to have one's very existence rooted in non-being. For that which is becoming is not yet that which it is in the process of becoming. Therefore, that which exists in this manner is a <term>becoming</term>.

### PROPOSITION

<summary>Proclus argues that anything subject to time—whether in its nature or its behavior—cannot truly "be" in a permanent sense, but is always in a state of "becoming" or "generation." He demonstrates this by showing that temporal things are divided into past and future, meaning they are never a complete whole at any single moment.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Time, Essence, Becoming, Generation, Temporal Extension, Metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 15

...is, but as much as it can possibly be, it already possesses the whole undiminished, and without extension. That is to say, it possesses its action united to its essence <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "essence" is what a thing is, while "action" is what it does. In eternal beings, these are identical.</note>, and consisting in the same measure of perfection, as if compacted according to one and the same limit, immovably and without progression. For if it is eternal <note>original: "sempiternum"</note>, as the name itself denotes, it is "always being." But to be "at some time" is different from "always being"; in the latter, there ought not to be one part prior and another posterior. For if there were, there would be a process of becoming <note>original: "generatio"</note> and a state of not-yet-being. But where there is neither prior nor posterior, nor "was" and "will be," but only "to be," that which is exists all at once. And everything that it is, it is the same in its acting.

->COROLLARY.<-

From this it is truly manifest that eternity is the cause of things existing as wholes. For everything eternal, whether in its essence or its action, has its whole essence or action present to itself all at once.

### PROPOSITION 53.

Eternity pre-exists all eternal things, and Time pre-exists all temporal things.

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

If, indeed, in every case the "participants" are preceded by the "participated," and the "participated" are preceded by the "unparticipated" <note term="Participation">This is a core concept in the work of Proclus. An "unparticipated" principle is the pure, independent source (like Time itself). A "participated" principle is the version of that source that enters into a specific thing. A "participant" is the thing that receives it.</note>, it is clear that the eternal thing is one thing; the eternity within that eternal thing is another; and eternity itself, in and of itself, is yet another. The first exists as a participant; the second as something participated; and the third as the unparticipated source. 

In the same way, that which is in time is one thing, for it is a participant. The time that is within it is another, for it is participated. And the Time that exists before this is another, existing as the unparticipated source. Each of these unparticipated sources is the same everywhere and in all things; whereas the participated version exists only in those things by which it is shared. For there are many things both eternal and temporal, and in all of them, eternity exists according to participation. Time, in the former sense, is divided, while the unparticipated Time is indivisible. There is one Time before all these temporal things. The first is the "Eternity of eternities," while the latter is the "Time of times," the creators of all participated things.

### PROPOSITION 54.

Every eternity is the measure of eternal things, and all time is the measure of those things which are in time; and these are the only two measures of life and motion in beings.

E 2 PROOF-

<summary>This page discusses the metaphysical nature of Eternity and Time, arguing that they are indivisible wholes that exist as "unparticipated" sources prior to the things that exist within them.</summary>
<keywords>Eternity, Time, Participation, Essence vs Action, Proclus, Metaphysics, Neoplatonism, Unparticipated, Participated</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
<note>original: "Proclus Diadochus." Proclus (412–485 AD) was the last great head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. He was known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) to Plato.</note>

### PROOF.

For everything that measures, it measures either according to a part, or as a whole, adapted to the thing being measured. That which measures as a whole is **eternity**; that which measures by parts is **time**. Therefore, there are only two measures: one for eternal things, and the other for temporal things.

### PROPOSITION 56.

Everything that subsists according to time either exists for eternal time, or has its subsistence at some point in a specific part of time.

### PROOF.

If indeed all <term>progressions: the Neoplatonic concept of "procession" (proodos), where reality flows from a single source into many levels of existence</term> occur through similarity, then before reaching things that are completely dissimilar, those things that follow the first principles should subsist as more similar to them than dissimilar. However, it is impossible to join things that come to be only in a part of time directly to eternal things. For indeed, just as things that *become* differ from those that *subsist*, and things that exist *at some point* differ from those that *always* exist. 

The middle ground between these two groups are things that are somewhat similar to the former, and somewhat similar to the latter. Is it not true, then, that there is a gap between things that exist "at some point" and things that "always" are? The middle ground between these must therefore be either that which "always becomes" or that which "exists at some point." But it is impossible for something to be "at some point truly being." On the other hand, that which "is at some point not truly being" is the same as that which "becomes." <note>Proclus is distinguishing between "Being" (perfect, unchanging reality) and "Becoming" (the changing world we see).</note> Therefore, "existing at some point" cannot be the middle ground. It remains, then, that the middle ground between both is that which **always becomes**. Because it "becomes," it is joined to the lower, changing things; but because it does so "always," it imitates the eternal nature, etc.

### COROLLARY.

From these things it is clear that there is a double eternity. One is truly eternal, the other is eternal according to time. The former is a **standing eternity**, while the latter is an eternity **in the making**. The former possesses its being as a unity and as a whole all at once; the latter is poured out and unfolded according to temporal extension. The former is a whole within itself; the latter is made of parts, each of which is separated according to "before" and "after."
PROPO-

<summary>Proclus explains the Neoplatonic hierarchy of time and eternity, arguing that there must be a middle ground—"always becoming"—between the timeless eternal and the temporary moments of our world.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Metaphysics, Eternity, Time, Procession, Being vs Becoming, Proposition 56</keywords>
