<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," a foundational work of Neoplatonism. Following the discussion of causes and effects, this page introduces Propositions 57 and 58, which explore how higher causes exert more influence than immediate ones and have a wider reach.</meta>

->THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 16<-

### PROPOSITION 57.

Everything that is produced by secondary causes is also produced, even more so, by those causes that are prior and more fundamental, from which the secondary causes were themselves produced.

### DEMONSTRATION.

If a secondary cause <note>A "secondary cause" is an intermediary—like a tool in the hand of a craftsman or a planet reflecting the sun’s light.</note> derives its entire essence from the cause that exists before it, then its very power of producing must also originate from that prior source. This is because productive powers are essential to those things that produce; they fulfill and complete their very being. Now, if the secondary cause obtained its power to produce from a higher cause, it follows that the higher cause provided the basis for the existence of whatever the secondary cause creates. 

If this is true, then the things arising from the secondary cause are actually caused by that which stands before it. For that which makes the second thing a "cause" also makes the third thing a "result." If this is the case, the result is produced by the primary cause as well. Furthermore, it is clear that the result is *more* dependent on the primary cause. For if the primary cause gave the secondary one the ability to produce, it possessed that causal power first. Consequently, the secondary cause only generates because it received the power of generating from the source above it. If one thing is made productive through participation <note>In Neoplatonism, "participation" describes how a lower thing shares in the qualities of a higher thing without the higher thing losing anything.</note> while the other is productive by its own gift of that power, then that which bestows the power of generating upon its neighbor is the primary and greater cause.

### PROPOSITION 58.

Every cause acts both before its effect and continues to be productive of more things beyond it <note>This principle suggests that the higher a cause is in the hierarchy of the universe, the further its influence reaches.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Insofar as something is a cause, it is more perfect and more powerful than that which follows it. If this is true, it must be the cause of more things. It is the nature of a greater power to produce more; of an equal power, to produce equal things; and of a lesser power, to produce fewer things. Furthermore, a power that can achieve greater things can also achieve lesser things. However, a power that can only achieve lesser things will not necessarily be able to achieve greater ones. Therefore, if the cause is more powerful, it is productive of more effects. 

Indeed, whatever the effect itself can produce, the cause can produce even more effectively. For everything produced by secondary causes is produced even more by the prior and greater causes. These causes coexist...

<summary>This page presents two core Neoplatonic principles: that higher causes are more responsible for an effect than immediate causes, and that more powerful causes have a broader range of influence.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, causality, participation, primary cause, secondary cause, hierarchy of being, productive power</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus’s discussion on the nature of causes and their effects, specifically focusing on how higher causes (like the Good or Intellect) extend their influence further than lower causes (like the Soul).</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI". Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...stand forth, therefore, for her, everything that she is naturally born to produce. If indeed it even produces the effect itself first, it is clearly evident that it acts before it, according to its own productive action. Every cause, therefore, acts even before the effect, and with it, and produces other things after it.

->COROLLARY<-

From these things it is surely clear that of whatever things **Soul** is the cause, **Intellect** is also the cause; but not of whatever things Intellect is the cause, Soul is also the cause. <note>In the Neoplatonic hierarchy, Intellect (Nous) is a higher and more powerful reality than the Soul (Psykhe), thus its influence is broader.</note> But it also acts before the Soul, and what the Soul gives to secondary things, Intellect itself also gives, and to a greater degree; and even when the Soul is no longer acting, Intellect illuminates its gifts with things to which the Soul has not given itself. For even the inanimate, insofar as it has participated in <term>form</term><note>The "form" (eidos) is the essential nature or design of a thing that makes it what it is.</note>, participates in Intellect and the making of Intellect; and surely, also of whatever things Intellect is the cause, the **Good** is also the cause, but not conversely. For privations of forms come from there <note>original: "illinc", referring to the First Principle or the Good.</note>. For all things are from there. But Intellect is not the creator of <term>privation</term><note>Privation is the absence or lack of a quality that should naturally be present, such as darkness being the privation of light.</note>, since it is form.

->PROPOSITION 58<-
<note>The original uses "LIIX," an archaic Roman numeral for 58.</note>

**Everything produced by more causes is more composite than that which is produced by fewer.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if every cause gives something to that which proceeds from itself, more causes will make more gifts, while fewer causes will make fewer. Therefore, the participants also will be composed of more things or fewer things, according to what they have participated in; the former because of their progression from more causes, the latter because of their progression from fewer. Those from more are more composite, while those from fewer are simpler than those very things. Everything produced by more causes, therefore, is more composite, while that from fewer is simpler; for whatever participates in the latter <note>the lower/more specific cause</note> also participates in the former <note>the higher/more general cause</note>, but not vice versa.

->PROPOSITION 59<-

**Everything that is simple according to its essence is either superior to composite things or inferior to them.**

DEMON-

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstrations of Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the relationship between the simplicity of a cause and the extent of its power. It concludes the proof from page 116 regarding the hierarchy of beings.</meta>

->THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 117<-

->P R O O F.<-

For if the highest of beings are produced by fewer and simpler causes, while the middle beings are produced by a greater number, then the latter will be <term>composite</term> <note>In this context, "composite" means something made of multiple parts or influenced by many different levels of causes, making it less "pure" than the things above it.</note>. And as for the extremes: some are simpler in a superior way, while others are simpler in an inferior way. Since the highest beings are produced by fewer causes, it is clearly because they belong to the superior ranks; they begin their existence before the inferior things and extend further than them, reaching where the inferior things cannot go due to a lack of power. Because of this, the very last of beings is also the simplest, just like the first, because it arises from the first cause alone. However, one kind of simplicity is superior to all composition, while the other is inferior; and this same rule applies to all things.

->P R O P O S I T I O N. LX.<-
<note>LX is the Roman numeral for 60.</note>

**Every cause that affects a greater number of things is superior to that which has received power over fewer things, and it produces the very foundations of those things for which the latter cause produces the specific forms.**

->P R O O F.<-

For if one cause affects fewer things and another affects more, and the things affected by the one are parts of the things affected by the other, then the cause which produces the greater number will also produce the remainder. However, the cause that produces only the "fewer" will not produce all the things that the "greater" cause does. Therefore, the cause of the many is more powerful and encompasses more. For as product is related to product, so the producing causes are related to one another. That which is more powerful possesses a greater and more <term>universal</term> <note original: "vniuersaliorem">Universal here refers to a cause that has a broader reach across all levels of reality, rather than a specific or "particular" effect.</note> power. This makes it closer to the "Cause of All Things" (the Divine); and that which is closer to the Divine is inherently more "good," since the Divine is Goodness itself. Therefore, the cause of a greater number of things is essentially superior to that which produces fewer things.

->P R O P O S I T I O N. LXI.<-
<note>LXI is the Roman numeral for 61.</note>

**Every power is greater when it remains <term>indivisible</term> <note original: "impartibilis">Meaning the power is concentrated in a single point or unity rather than being spread out or fragmented.</note>, but becomes lesser when it is divided.**

->PROO-<-

<summary>This page argues that the most powerful causes in the universe are the most "universal" and "simple," reaching more things than lesser, more complex causes. It also introduces the principle that power is strongest when it is unified and indivisible.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, hierarchy of being, universal causes, simplicity vs composition, power, the Good, indivisibility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical propositions of Proclus (412–485 AD), a leading Neoplatonist. The text focuses on the "Elements of Theology," specifically discussing the relationship between the "One" (the ultimate source) and the "multitude" (the diversity of the created world).</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR 113<-

### PROOF.

For if it is divided, it departs into a multitude. If this happens, it becomes further removed from the One. <note>The "One" is the ultimate, indivisible source of all reality in Neoplatonic thought.</note> If this is so, it will be capable of less; since it departs from the One which contains it, and it becomes imperfect. Since indeed the good of each thing exists through its union.

### PROPOSITION LXII.

Every multitude existing closer to the One is lesser in quantity than those further away, but greater in power, etc.

### PROOF.

For that which is closer is more similar to the One. The One, however, was the constituent of all things without being multiplied itself. Therefore, that which is more similar to it exists as the cause of a greater number of things. For indeed, that thing will be more uniform and more indivisible than all other things if it is to be one. Just as, therefore, that which is less multiplied is more closely related to the One, so that which produces more things is more related to the "Cause of All." This, moreover, is more powerful.

### COROLLARY.

From these things it is surely clear that there are more bodily natures than there are souls; and there are more souls than there are minds. There are more minds than there are divine <term>units: originally "unitatibus" or Henads, the highest spiritual beings that bridge the gap between the Absolute One and the rest of existence</term>. And in all things the same reasoning applies.

### PROPOSITION LXIII.

Every <term>unparticipated principle: a "monad" or source that remains pure and independent while still allowing other things to derive their qualities from it</term> produces two orders of participated things: one among those things that participate only at certain times, and another among those that participate always and by their very nature.

### PROOF.

For that which is always participated is more similar to the unparticipated source than that which is only participated at certain times. Before, therefore, that which is participated only sometimes can exist, that which is always participable must exist. Indeed, because it is participated in by that which follows after it,

sum <note>This "sum" at the bottom of the page is a catchword, indicating the first word of the next page to ensure the book was bound correctly.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that as things move closer to the "One," they decrease in number but increase in power, establishing a hierarchy from divine units down to physical bodies. He also distinguishes between things that always share in a divine quality and those that only do so temporarily.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, Multitude, Henads, Divine Units, Participation, Metaphysics, Hierarchy of Being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 63 regarding the hierarchy of participation, moving into a corollary and then Proposition 64, which distinguishes between self-sufficient substances and those that exist only as "illuminations" within something else.</meta>

### THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 18

...[is] different, in that what is always [participated] is more akin and more similar to the <term>unparticipated</term> <note>original: imparticipabili; refers to a pure principle that remains "undistributed" and transcendent even as others derive from it</note>. Nor are those things which are only *sometimes* participated in the only ones that exist; for prior to these are the things which are *always* participated in, through which these [temporary things] are also gathered according to a well-ordered progression toward the unparticipated. Nor are those which are always participated in alone; for since they possess an inextinguishable power (if indeed they exist forever), they are productive of others—namely, those which are only sometimes participated in—and the <term>diminution</term> <note>original: diminutio; the "stepping down" of power and reality as it moves away from the source</note> continues down to these.

->COROLLARY<-

From these points it is surely clear that the unifications which shine forth from the One upon beings are of two kinds: some are always participated in, while others are participated in only sometimes. In the same way, intellectual participations are twofold, as are the animations of souls and other <term>forms</term> <note>Platonic "Forms" or "Ideas," the blueprints of reality</note> similarly. For Beauty, Likeness, Stability, and Identity are unparticipated; they are participated in by those things that always participate, and by those things that sometimes participate, which hold the second rank in the same order.

->PROPOSITION LXIV.<-

Every principal unity produces a double number: one of substances that are <term>perfect in themselves</term> <note>original: per se perfectarum substantiarum; entities that have their own independent existence</note>, and another of illuminations that have their existence in other things.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

If progression occurs through diminution, then from the altogether perfect causes, perfect things proceed; and through these intermediaries, imperfect things proceed in an orderly fashion. Therefore, some will be self-perfect substances, while others will be imperfect, and these latter become the "forms" of the participants <note>meaning they only exist as a quality *of* the thing they inhabit, like the color of an object</note>. For since they are imperfect, they require subjects for their existence. The others, however, make the participants belong to themselves. For because they are perfect, they fill the participants with themselves and establish those participants within themselves. They require nothing from inferior things for their own subsistence. Therefore, the self-perfect substances, because of their distinction into a multitude, are lessened from their principal unity, because of the self-perfection...

<summary>This page describes how divine qualities like Beauty and Identity flow from a pure source into things that are always eternal and then into things that are temporary. It establishes a distinction between "self-perfect" substances that exist independently and "illuminations" that require a subject to exist.</summary>

<keywords>participation, unparticipated, self-perfect substances, illuminations, diminution, Beauty, Likeness, Stability, Identity, Neoplatonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse of Proclus's *Elements of Theology*, specifically discussing the nature of existence, the relationship between causes and effects, and the hierarchy of divine unities.</meta>

-># PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI"</note> possess a perfect existence, they are to some extent assimilated <note>made similar</note> to that principle. But imperfect things—because they exist within others and are distant from that which <term>subsists by itself</term> <note>original: "per se substat"; refers to an entity that has its own independent reality</note>, and because they are imperfect and distant from that which perfects all things—follow a progression through similar things until they reach those which are altogether unlike. Thus, each of the primary units produces a double number <note>A reference to the two types of beings produced by any primary principle: those that are self-complete and those that are merely reflections.</note>.

### COROLLARY

From these points it is clearly evident that even the unities <note>The 'Henads' or divine units that represent the gods in Neoplatonic thought.</note>, which are perfect in themselves, have proceeded from the One. Regarding the illuminations <note>Radiations or emanations of power</note> of the unities and of the mind: some are indeed essences perfect in themselves, while others are certain intellectual perfections. Of souls, some belong to themselves, while others belong to animated things and exist only as reflections and images of souls. And so, not every unity is a God, but only the unity that is perfect in itself. Nor is every intellectual property an Intellect, but only the essential one. Nor is every illumination of a soul a soul, for there are also images of souls.

### PROPOSITION 65

Everything that exists in any way whatsoever is either in its cause primarily, or according to its own existence, or according to participation, as an image.

### DEMONSTRATION

For if a product is seen within its producer, it is pre-existent in the cause. This is because every cause contains the thing it causes within itself; that which exists in the effect secondarily exists in the cause primarily. Alternatively, the producer may be seen in the product. For the product, by <term>participating</term> <note>The philosophical concept of a lower thing "taking part" in the qualities of a higher source</note> in the producer, shows within itself secondarily what the producer is primarily. Finally, each thing may be considered according to its own order, neither in its cause nor in its effect. For the cause exists in a superior way, and the effect in an inferior way. But it must also be what it is. Therefore, each thing exists according to its own actual existence within its own rank.

### PROPOSITION 66

All beings, in relation to one another, are either wholes, or parts, or the same, or different.

### DEMONSTRATION <insert>truncated</insert>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the relationship between the One and the Many, specifically focusing on the Neoplatonic theory of "wholes" and "parts."</term></meta>

->THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 19<-

## PROOF.

For if some encompass <note>original Latin: *comprehendunt*; meaning to contain or include within a definition or boundary</note> others, and the rest are encompassed, or if they neither encompass nor are encompassed, they either share a common state as participants in the One, or they are distinct from one another. But if they encompass, they would be **wholes**. If they are encompassed, they are **parts**. If they participate in the One, they are the same in respect to that One. If they are only many, they are different from one another insofar as they are many.

# PROPOSITION 67.

**Every wholeness** <term>Totalitas: the condition of being a whole. Proclus argues that things can be "whole" in three different ways: as a perfect idea, as a physical object, or as a fragment that contains the essence of the whole.</term> **is either before the parts, or composed of the parts, or within a part.**

## PROOF.

For we either observe the form in the cause of each thing, and we call that a **whole-before-the-parts** <note>This refers to the "Idea" or "Form" of a thing that exists in the divine mind before any physical version is made.</note> which pre-exists in the cause; or we observe it in the parts that participate in it. 

And this latter case occurs in two ways: 
1. Either in all the parts together—which is the **whole-from-parts**, where the absence of any part diminishes the whole. 
2. Or in each individual part, such that the part becomes a whole through participation, which makes it a "part" that exists as a "whole" in a partial way.

The **whole-from-parts** exists according to its actual being; the **whole-before-the-parts** exists according to its cause; and the **whole-within-a-part** exists according to participation. For this last type is a whole according to its ultimate "diminution" <note>Diminution refers to the way a divine or perfect concept becomes less powerful or more limited as it moves down into the physical world.</note>, by which it imitates the whole-from-parts. This occurs not when it is just any random part, but when it is capable of being made like the whole—where even its own parts are wholes.

# PROPOSITION 68.

**Every whole-within-a-part is a part of the whole-composed-of-parts.**

## PROOF.

For if it is a part, it is a part of a certain whole. It is either a part of that very "whole-within-itself" (insofar as it is called a "whole-in-a-part"), but in that case, it would be a part of itself, and the part would be equal to the whole, making both identical; or it is a part of some other whole. And if it is a part of another, it is either only a part of that one—and thus would again not differ from the whole, being one...

->F 2<- <note>This is a "signature mark," a guide for the bookbinder to ensure the pages are folded and bound in the correct sequence.</note>

<summary>Proclus defines three types of "wholes": the causal (before parts), the composite (made of parts), and the participative (the whole as reflected in a single part). He begins to prove that these different levels of wholeness are logically nested within one another.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Wholeness, Parts, Participation, Causality, Metaphysics, Totalitas</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Proposition 68 regarding the nature of the "whole" and its relationship to its parts, leading into the hierarchical structure of universal causes.</meta>

->### BY PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

...existing, one existing part, or with another. For every whole, there are more parts than one, and that will be a whole made of several—namely, from those parts of which it consists. And thus the whole that is found within its own part—which is itself made of parts—will be a part. <note>Proclus is concluding a logical proof from the previous page showing that a "whole" cannot be identical to a single one of its parts.</note>

### PROPOSITION 69

**Every whole composed of parts participates in that totality which exists before the parts.**

### DEMONSTRATION

If a thing is composed of parts, it is a **resultant** whole. <note>original Latin: "passum," meaning a whole that has been 'affected' or brought into being by the union of components, rather than being a primary source.</note> For when the parts are made into one, the whole is "affected" by that union; it is a whole residing within parts that are not, themselves, the whole. Now, for every **participated** <term>Participated: A quality or form that is actually present in a specific subject, like 'wholeness' in a chair.</term> thing, there pre-exists an **unparticipated** <term>Unparticipated: A pure, transcendent form that exists independently of the things that take after it.</term> thing. Therefore, an unparticipated totality pre-exists the participated one. 

There is, therefore, a certain kind of totality that exists before that whole which is made of parts. This primary totality is not "affected" so as to become a whole; rather, it is **totality in itself**. It is the source of that other totality which is made of parts. Furthermore, a whole made of parts exists in many places and within many different things; in some it exists one way, and in others quite differently. However, there must be a single, self-existing unity of all these totalities. 

For none of these individual wholes are "pure," since they are in need of the parts that compose them (which are not the whole itself). Nor can any single one of them, if it comes into being, be the cause for all the others being wholes. That which is the cause for all other wholes must exist **before the parts**. For if this cause were also made of parts, it would merely be a "certain whole" and not "wholeness simply." This would then require another cause, and so on to infinity—or we must arrive at a First Whole, which is not a whole made of parts, but is Totality itself.

### PROPOSITION 70

**Every more universal principle shines into participants before the more specific principles do, and it remains in the participant after the specific ones have departed.**

### DEMONSTRATION

For indeed, a more universal principle begins its action in secondary things before the principle that follows it; it is present alongside that subsequent principle’s presence, and even when that subsequent principle is no longer acting, the more universal one remains... <note>Proclus is describing the hierarchy of causes. For example, "Being" is more universal than "Life." A stone has "Being" but not "Life," so "Being" acts even where "Life" does not reach.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that for every physical or composite whole, there must be a transcendent "Wholeness" that exists prior to any parts. He also begins to explain that more universal causes (like "Being") have a wider and more enduring reach than specific ones (like "Humanity").</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Totality, Wholes and Parts, Universal Principles, Participation, Hierarchy of Being</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 70 regarding the relationship between primary and secondary causes, specifically how a more universal cause (like "Being") persists even when a more specific cause (like "Reason") departs.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 20<-

while the [secondary] agent acts, that which is more truly a cause is still present and acting. This occurs not only in different subjects, but even in each of those things which participate at some time. <note>Proclus is explaining that higher causes—like 'Being'—extend their influence further than lower causes—like 'Life' or 'Reason'.</note> For example, it is necessary first to become a **being**, then an **animal**, and then a **human**. When the rational power <note>original: "rationali potentia"</note> leaves a man, he is no longer a human; yet he remains an **animal**, breathing and sensing. And again, if life leaves him, he remains a **being** <note>original: "ens"</note>; for even when he does not live, "to be" is present. It is the same in all cases. The reason is that that which is more truly a cause, being more effective, acts first upon the participant. For the same thing is acted upon by the more powerful, prior cause, and when the secondary cause acts in turn, that prior cause also co-operates. Because everything that the second cause produces is also co-generated by that which is more truly a cause; and when the second leaves, the first remains. For the communication <note>original: "communicatio," referring to the bestowal of a property or essence</note> of the more powerful cause, since it acts more strongly, is the last to leave the participant. Indeed, it has strengthened its own illumination <term>illumination|A Neoplatonic term for the way a higher principle radiates its essence into a lower one</term> through the communication of the secondary cause.

### PROPOSITION LXXI.

**O**ll things which, among the primary causes, hold a more universal and superior rank, become in their effects—according to the illuminations proceeding from them—the subjects <term>subject|In this context, the underlying foundation or "matter" upon which further qualities are built</term>, in a way, for the communications of more particular causes. Furthermore, those illuminations which come from superior causes receive advancements from secondary ones, while the former are stabilized in the latter. Thus, some participations precede others, and images <note>original: "simulachra," referring to the reflections or "shadows" of higher forms appearing in lower existence</note> are piled upon images from above, meeting in the same subject. The more universal causes act beforehand, while the particular ones, following the actions of the former, bestow their own communications upon the participants.

### DEMONSTRATION.

**N**ow if those things which are more truly causes act before the secondary causes due to their excellence of power, and are present to those things which have a more imperfect aptitude, illuminating them—and if those things that are lower and secondary in rank receive gifts from the higher ones—it is clear that the illuminations of the superiors preoccupy <note>In the sense of "taking possession of beforehand"</note> that which participates in both, and they strengthen the communications of the lower ones. These lower communications use those images which come from the superiors as though they were fulcrums and foundations, and they act upon the participant that has been prepared by them.

It demonstrates that the influence of a primary cause remains even after a secondary cause has ceased to act.</summary>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical sequence of Proclus' "Elements of Theology," moving from the nature of divine causes to the relationship between universal foundations (subjects) and the specific forms that inhabit them.</meta>

# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was a Greek Neoplatonist philosopher, known as the "Successor" (Diadochus) to Plato’s Academy. This text is from his *Elements of Theology*, a work organized like a geometry textbook with propositions and proofs.</note>

### PROPOSITION 72.

**All things that serve as a foundation for others proceed from more perfect and more universal causes.**
<note>original: "subiecti habent rõnem" – literally "have the nature of a subject." In this context, a "subject" is the underlying material or base (substrate) that supports higher qualities, like how a body supports a soul.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

The causes of a greater number of things are more powerful, more universal, and closer to the **One** than those that affect only a few things. 
<note>original: "vni" – referring to The One, the ultimate source of all existence in Neoplatonic thought.</note> 
Now, those causes that produce the things which serve as foundations for others are the causes of a greater number of things; this is because they produce the very *capacity* for existence before specific forms are even present. Therefore, these foundational causes are more universal and more perfect within the hierarchy of causes.

### COROLLARY.

From these points, it is clear why **matter**, having been produced directly from the **One**, is by its own nature devoid of **form**. 
<term>Form: In this philosophy, "form" is the blueprint or essence that gives matter its specific identity (e.g., the "form" of a chair makes a block of wood a chair).</term> 
Furthermore, the **body** by itself—even if it shares in existence—does not naturally share in the **soul**. For **matter**, existing as the foundation for *all* things, proceeded from the cause of *all* things. The **body**, however, which exists as the foundation for life (animation), originated from a cause more universal than the soul, even if it participates in existence in some other way.

### PROPOSITION 73.

**While every "whole" is a specific existing thing and participates in existence, not every existing thing is a "whole."**

### DEMONSTRATION.

Either **existence** and **wholeness** are the same thing, or one comes before the other. However, even a **part**, insofar as it is a part, is an existing thing (since a whole is made up of existing parts), yet a part is not a **whole** in its own right. Therefore, **existence** and **wholeness** are not the same; for if they were, a part would have to be considered a non-entity (something that does not exist). 

But if the part did not exist, the whole would not exist either. For every whole is a whole of parts—whether it exists as a concept before the parts or as a structure within them. If the part does not exist, it is impossible for the whole to exist. 

If, on the other hand, **wholeness** came *before* **existence**, then every single thing that exists would immediately be a **whole**. In that case, a part could not be a part, which is impossible. If a whole is truly a whole, it exists as the whole of a part; and the part must exist as a part of that whole. It remains, therefore, that every whole is an existing thing, but not every existing thing is a whole.

### CORE-
<note>The text cuts off mid-word ("CORRELARIVM"), which will be continued on the following page.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that the most basic foundations of reality (like matter) come from the highest possible causes, and he distinguishes the concept of "existence" from the concept of "wholeness."</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, universal causes, The One, matter and form, whole and part, existence vs wholeness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic metaphysical arguments regarding the hierarchy of existence, specifically focusing on the relationship between Being, Totality, and Species. It follows the previous discussion on how causes that reach more things are higher in the cosmic order.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 21

->COROLLARY<-

From these points it is clear that, first, <term>Being</term><note>Latin: *ens*. In this context, Being is the most fundamental category of existence that applies to everything.</note> exists beyond the level of <term>Totality</term>, since Being is present to a greater number of things. It even grants existence to parts, insofar as they are parts. Totality, however, is present to fewer things. As previously demonstrated, the cause of many things is superior, while the cause of fewer things is lower in the hierarchy.

## PROPOSITION 74.

**Every species is a kind of whole.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For a species consists of several elements, each of which completes the species. However, not every whole is a species. For example, a specific "something" or an individual is a whole (insofar as it is an individual), but it is not a species. Every whole consists of parts, but a <term>species</term><note>Latin: *species*. Here referring to a "Form" or "Type" that can be divided into further sub-types or individuals.</note> is specifically that which is divided into several individual forms. Therefore, "whole" and "species" are different things; the former belongs to many things, while the latter belongs to fewer. Consequently, the concept of the Whole ranks above the species of beings.

->COROLLARY<-

From this it is clear that the **Whole** holds a middle rank between **Being** and **Species**. It follows that Being exists before species, and while all species are beings, not every being is a species. This is why even among produced effects, <term>privations</term><note>Latin: *privationes*. This refers to the absence or lack of a quality, such as blindness being the privation of sight.</note> are in some sense "beings," yet they are not "species." Because of the unifying power of Being, even these lacks have received a certain faint image of existence.

## PROPOSITION 75.

**Everything that is called a "cause" in the proper sense is separate from its effect.**

->DEMONSTRATION<-

For if a cause existed within the effect itself, it would either be a part that completes the effect, or it would somehow depend on the effect for its own existence. In that case, the cause would be more imperfect than the effect itself. That which exists inside an effect is more of a "co-cause" than a true cause—it is either a part of the thing being made or an instrument used by the maker. For a part in the thing made,

-># PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

it is made, and is more imperfect than the whole, and is an instrument serving the process of generation, unable to determine for itself the measure of its own making. Therefore, every cause in the proper sense, if it is more perfect than its effect, also provides the measure for generation; it is exempt from being a mere instrument and from all the simple elements which are called <term>co-causes</term> <note>original: "concause"; these are secondary factors or materials that assist a primary cause but do not direct the outcome.</note>.

### PROPOSITION 76.
Everything that is produced by an **immobile cause** <note>A cause that does not change or move while it creates its effect.</note> has an **immutable existence**. But everything produced by a **mobile cause** is **mutable**.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
If the agent is entirely immobile, it produces what follows it not through motion, but by its very being. If this is so, that which comes from it is concurrent with the cause's own being. If this is the case, it produces for as long as it exists. It always exists; therefore, it always produces what follows it. 

And so, this effect is always being produced from that source, and always is. The "always" of the cause's action joins together the "always" of the effect's own progression. If, however, the cause is moved, then that which is made by it will be mutable in its essence; for that which has its being through motion must change its being when the motion changes. For if a product of motion remained immutable, it would be superior to its producing cause, but that is impossible. Therefore, it will not be immutable; thus it changes and will be moved in its essence, imitating the motion that produced it.

### PROPOSITION 77.
Everything that exists in **potentiality** <note>The capacity to become something else.</note> proceeds into **actuality** <note>The state of being fully realized or "in act."</note> through a being that is already in actuality. That which is in some respect potential comes from a being that is in some respect in act; that which is entirely potential comes from a being that is entirely in act.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For that which is potential is not by nature capable of bringing itself into act, since it is imperfect. For if that which is imperfect were to become the cause of its own perfection, and were to be a cause in act, then it would—as a result of being made by itself—be more imperfect than the result. Therefore... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the argument that an effect cannot be more perfect than its cause.</note>

<summary>This page explores how the nature of a cause dictates the nature of its effect, specifically arguing that unchanging causes create eternal results. It also introduces the principle that potential things require a pre-existing "actual" force to reach their full state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration of Proposition 77 regarding the relationship between potentiality and actuality, likely from a Latin translation of Proclus’ "Elements of Theology."</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 22

->...therefore, that which is in **potentiality**, insofar as it is potential to itself so that it may come into **actuality**, would be its own cause.<- For that which is imperfect would be the cause of the perfect; since everything that exists in potentiality, as such, is imperfect. But everything that exists in actuality <note>Latin: *actu*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to a thing being fully realized or 'in act'.</note>, as such, is perfect. If, then, that which is in potentiality is to become actual, it will receive its perfection from something else. And either that thing is itself potential (but in this way it would again be an imperfect thing producing a perfect thing), or it is actual. And either something else, or this very thing which is potential, becomes actual. But if something else already existing in actuality acts according to its own nature <note>Latin: *proprietatem*. Referring to the specific essence or "property" of the acting thing.</note>, it will make that which is potential in the other become actual. This [the potential thing] will not, therefore, become actual [of its own accord]; since it does not become so insofar as it is potential. It remains, then, that for something that is in potentiality to exist in actuality, it must be changed by that which exists in actuality.

### PROPOSITION 78.
**Every power is either perfect or imperfect.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For a power that brings about <term>actuality</term><note>The fulfillment or realization of a thing's nature.</note> is a **perfect power**. It makes other things perfect through its actions, and being the source of perfection for others, it is itself more perfect. But a power that needs another thing which already exists in actuality—insofar as it is merely something in <term>potentiality</term><note>The latent capacity to become or do something.</note>—is an **imperfect power**. It requires the perfection existing in another so that, by participating in that other, it may become perfect; for by itself, such a power is imperfect. Thus, the power of that which is in actuality is perfect, as it is fertile and productive of action. But the power of that which is potential is imperfect, receiving its perfection from the former.

### PROPOSITION 79.
**Everything that comes into being does so from a twofold power.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For the thing itself must be suitable and possess an **imperfect power** [the capacity to be changed]; and the agent, existing in actuality as that which the other is only potentially, must possess a **perfect power**. For every action proceeds from an inherent power. If the agent does not possess the power, how will it act and produce an effect in something else? But if...

<summary>This page concludes a proof that potential things require an external actual cause to become real, then defines "perfect" power (active/productive) versus "imperfect" power (passive/receptive), noting that all change requires both.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 79 and introduces Propositions 80 and 81 of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology." This text establishes the Neoplatonic distinction between the physical (body) and the metaphysical (incorporeal).</meta>

113
->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>The title "Diadochus" means the "Successor," referring to Proclus's role as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...what occurs does not have the power according to its suitability <note>Latin: *aptitudinem*; the natural fitness of a thing to receive a specific quality</note>, in what way will it occur? For the actor acts upon that which is able to be acted upon, but not upon just anything, nor upon that which is not naturally suited to be acted upon by it.

### PROPOSITION 80

**Every body is by nature intended to be acted upon <note>Latin: *pati*; in Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to being "passive" or the recipient of an action</note>. Every incorporeal thing, however, is intended to act. The former is ineffective by itself, while the latter is incapable of being acted upon <note>Latin: *impassibile*; meaning it cannot be changed or affected by external force</note>. Yet even an incorporeal thing is acted upon through its communion with a body, just as bodies are able to act through their participation in incorporeal things.**

### DEMONSTRATION

For a body, insofar as it is a body, is only divisible, and thus is everywhere capable of being acted upon, being divisible in every way and even unto infinity. However, an incorporeal thing, being simple, is incapable of being acted upon. For that which is indivisible cannot be divided, nor can that which is uncompounded be altered. Therefore, the acting force must be either nothing at all, or it must be incorporeal. Indeed, a body, as a body, does not act; it is only positioned to be divided and to be acted upon. Since every acting thing possesses an active power, a body will not act insofar as it is a body, but according to the power of acting which is within it. By itself, a body is ineffective and powerless. Therefore, it acts through the participation of power whenever it acts. But indeed, even incorporeal things participate in <term>passions|passiones</term> <note>Here, "passions" does not mean emotions, but the state of being "acted upon" or undergoing change.</note> when they are in bodies, being divided along with them and sharing in their divisible nature, even though they remain indivisible according to their own essence.

### PROPOSITION 81

**Everything that is participated in a separable manner <note>A "separable" participant is a higher principle (like a Soul) that remains distinct from the physical things that share in its nature.</note> is present through a certain inseparable power, which it imparts to the participant.**

### DEMONSTRATION

For if the higher thing itself exists separately from the participant and is not within it—as if possessing its own substance in itself—it then requires a certain <term>mediation|medietate</term> to connect the one to the other; a mediator that is more similar to the participated thing than the participant is itself. For if that higher thing is separable, how does the participant share in it? Not by the thing itself...

<summary>This page defines the relationship between the physical and the incorporeal, arguing that bodies are inherently passive and divisible while incorporeal entities are active and indivisible. It also begins explaining how higher, "separable" realities communicate their power to lower participants through intermediate forces.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, body vs. soul, passivity, active power, divisibility, participation, mediation, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus’s Elements of Theology, moving from the nature of participation to the concepts of self-knowledge and eternal power. The numbering follows the established sequence from previous pages.</meta>

THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 23

...itself, possessing that very thing and nothing other than itself. Therefore, the power and the **illumination** <note>original: "illustratio" — in Neoplatonic thought, this refers to the "light" or influence that flows from a higher source to a lower one.</note> proceeding from that source into the participant will join the two together. And this will be the means through which participation occurs; however, the <term>participated</term> <note>The higher source or form being shared.</note> is one thing, and the <term>participant</term> <note>The lower being receiving the influence.</note> is another.

### PROPOSITION 82.
Every <term>incorporeal being</term> <note>A non-physical entity, like a soul or a divine mind.</note> that is **self-turned** <note>original: "ad se conuersiuum" — the ability of a being to revert back to its own essence rather than just flowing outward.</note> and is shared by others, is shared in a separate [transcendent] way.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
If it were shared inseparably, its activity would not be separable from the participant, just as its <term>essence</term> <note>The fundamental "being" or nature of a thing.</note> would not be. If this were the case, it would not turn back toward itself. For once it has turned back, it will be distinct from the participant, existing as one thing while the participant is another. Therefore, if it is able to turn back toward itself, it must be shared in a separate way whenever it is shared by others.

### PROPOSITION 83.
Every being capable of **self-knowledge** is entirely turned back toward itself.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
It is manifest that a being turns toward itself in its activity when it knows itself. In this act, the knower and the known are one; the knowledge of oneself is directed toward oneself as the thing being known. As an action of the knower, it is directed toward itself because it is its own faculty of knowing. Furthermore, it has been demonstrated that if a being is self-turned in its action, it is also self-turned in its essence. Every being that is self-turned in its activity possesses an essence that returns to itself and exists within itself.

### PROPOSITION 84.
Everything that exists eternally possesses **infinite power**.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
If its substance is never-failing, then the power by which it exists—and through which it is what it is—must also be infinite. If its power of being were finite, it would eventually fail. If that power were to fail, the existence of the being which possesses that power would also <margin>G 2</margin> fail.

<summary>This page outlines the metaphysical rules for how non-physical beings interact with the world, arguing that self-aware beings must remain distinct from what they influence and that eternal existence requires infinite power.</summary>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. This text is from his *Elements of Theology*.</note>

...[does not] act, nor does it any longer exist as a being that always is. It is necessary, therefore, that the power of that which always is—the power that encompasses it according to its essence—be infinite.

### PROPOSITION 85

**Everything that is always in the process of becoming possesses an infinite power of becoming.**

#### DEMONSTRATION

For if it is always becoming, the power of generation <note>Original: *generatio*, the process of coming-to-be or being produced.</note> within it is unfailing. If this power were finite, it would eventually cease in the span of infinite time. If the power of becoming were to cease, then that which is becoming would also certainly cease—specifically, that which exists according to that power—and it would no longer be a thing always in the process of becoming. But it is assumed to be always becoming. Therefore, it possesses an infinite power of becoming.

### PROPOSITION 86

**Everything that truly exists is infinite, not according to multitude, nor according to magnitude, but according to power alone.**

#### DEMONSTRATION

Everything that is infinite is so either in terms of multitude <note>Quantity or number of parts.</note>, or in magnitude <note>Physical size or spatial extension.</note>, or in power. That which <term>truly exists</term> <note>Original: *enter ens*, a Latin phrasing of the Greek *ontōs on*, referring to the stable, eternal reality of the intellect rather than the changing physical world.</note> is indeed infinite, as it possesses an inextinguishable life, an unfailing existence, and an undiminished activity. 

Yet it is not infinite because of its magnitude, since that which truly exists is without magnitude, being self-subsisting. For everything that exists as a <term>self-subsisting</term> <note>A being that contains the cause of its own existence within itself.</note> being is <term>impartible</term> <note>Indivisible; having no parts.</note> and simple. Nor is it infinite because of multitude, for it is most uniform, being ordered very close to the One <note>In Proclus's system, "The One" is the ultimate, simplest source of all reality.</note> and being most akin to the One. 

Rather, it is infinite according to power. Wherefore, it is simultaneously impartible and infinite. And indeed, the more it is one and the more it is impartible, the more it is also infinite. For a power that is divided becomes weak and finite. Indeed, powers that are entirely divisible are entirely finite. Those that are last and furthest from the One are in some way finite because of their division, while those that are first are infinite because of their impartibility. For division scatters and dissolves the power of each thing, while impartibility, by binding and concentrating it, <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the thought that impartibility preserves power.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that anything eternally becoming must have infinite power to sustain itself, and that true, eternal Being is infinite in power—but not in physical size or number—because it is unified and indivisible.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Infinity, Power, Being, Impartibility, The One, Metaphysics, Elements of Theology</keywords>

->ELEMENTA THEOLOGICA. 24<-

...binding it, it contains it as unfailing and undiminished within itself. But infinity according to magnitude <note>original: "magnitudinem"; referring to physical size or extension</note> and according to multitude <note>original: "multitudinem"; referring to the number of parts</note> is a complete privation of indivisibility, and a falling away. For that which is closest to the indivisible is the finite, while the most distant is the infinite, departing in every way from the One. <note>The "One" is the ultimate source of all reality in this philosophy; distance from the One implies a loss of unity and power.</note> Therefore, power that is infinite is not found in an infinity of multitude or magnitude. Since indeed infinite power coexists with indivisibility. But the infinite in multitude or magnitude is furthest from the indivisible. If therefore a <term>truly existing being</term> <note>original Latin: "enter ens," a literal translation of the Greek "ontos on," referring to a being that has stable, true reality as opposed to a passing shadow.</note> were infinite in magnitude or multitude, it would not have infinite power; but it does have infinite power; therefore it is not infinite according to magnitude or multitude.

### PROPOSITION 87.

Every eternal thing is a being; but not every being is eternal.

### PROOF.

For in <term>generated things</term> <note>original: "generabilibus"; things in the physical world that are born, change, and die.</note> there is in a certain way a participation in being, insofar as these things are not that which in no way exists. If, however, the generated thing is not "not-being," then it is in some way a being; yet the eternal is in no way present in generated things—and especially not in those which do not participate in eternity even through the whole of time. But every eternal thing is always; for it participates in eternity, which grants "always-being" to those things by which it is participated. Being, therefore, is participated in by more things than eternity is. Therefore, being is above eternity. For those things which participate in eternity also participate in being. But those things which participate in being do not all participate in eternity.

### PROPOSITION 88.

Every truly existing being is either before eternity, or in eternity, or a participant in eternity.

### PROOF.

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus regarding the nature of being and its relationship to the foundational principles of Limit and the Unlimited. It follows the discussion on the nature of eternity.</meta>

-> # PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR <-

-> ### PROOF. <-
For it has been demonstrated that it <note>Referring to the "One" or the highest principle discussed in previous sections</note> exists before <term>eternity</term> <note>original: "sempiternitas"; in Neoplatonism, this refers to the realm of eternal being rather than just endless time</note>. But it is also within eternity. For eternity contains within itself the quality of "always" together with "being." And that which participates in eternity—for everything eternal is said to be eternal by a participation in both "always" and in "being"—possesses both of these according to that participation. But eternity itself possesses "always" primarily, and "being" by participation. <term>Being</term> <note>original: "ens"</note> itself, however, is primarily being.

## PROPOSITION LXXXIX [89].
Every **primary being** consists of **limit** and the **unlimited**.

### PROOF.
If being is <term>infinitely powerful</term> <note>original: "infinitipotens"</note>, it is clear that it is infinite, and in this respect, it subsists from the infinite. If, however, it is indivisible and uniform, it has in this respect participated in <term>limit</term> <note>original: "termino"; representing the Greek 'peras', the principle of order and definition</note>. For that which participates in the "One" is limited. But being is simultaneously indivisible and infinitely powerful. Therefore, every <term>essential being</term> <note>original: "enter ens"; a literal translation of the Greek 'ontos on', meaning truly or essentially existing being</note> consists of limit and the infinite.

## PROPOSITION XC [90].
For all things that consist of **limit** and the **unlimited**, there pre-exist in their own right a **first limit** and a **first infinity**.

### PROOF.
If those things which are beings in their own right <note>meaning they do not depend on something else for their existence</note> pre-exist as common and primary causes for all beings—and not just for some, but for all simply—then there must exist a **first limit** and a **first infinity** before that which is composed of both. For the limit within a "mixture" <note>A "mixture" refers to any reality that combines multiple principles, such as being itself</note> participates in the infinite, and the infinite participates in the limit. But the "first" of anything is nothing other than what it essentially is. Therefore, it is not proper for the first infinity to be "limit-formed," nor for the first limit to be "infinite-formed." Thus, these two exist primarily before the mixture.

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*, moving from the general nature of "the One" and "the Many" into the specific metaphysical relationship between Power, Limit, and Infinity.</meta>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 25

PROPOSITION XCI.

**Every power is either finite or infinite. But every finite power subsists from an infinite power, and infinite power subsists from the primal infinity.**

DEMONSTRATION.

Powers that exist only for a time <note>referring to transient or mortal things that do not exist eternally</note> are finite, having fallen away from the infinity of eternal being. However, the powers of eternal beings are infinite, as they never abandon their own existence.

PROPOSITION XCII.

**Every multitude of infinite powers depends upon a single primary infinity, which is not a "participated" power <note>meaning it is not a property residing within another thing</note>, nor does it exist within those things that possess power; rather, it exists by itself, not as the power of some participant, but as the cause of all beings.**

DEMONSTRATION.

If Being itself, as the first of things, possesses power, it is nonetheless not identical to Power itself, for it also possesses "Limit" <note>The Greek concepts of *peras* (limit) and *apeiria* (infinity) are the two fundamental principles beneath the One</note>. The primary Power is Infinity. For infinite powers are made infinite through the participation in Infinity. Therefore, Infinity itself must exist before all powers. Because of Infinity, Being is "infinitely-powerful," and all things participate in infinity. Yet, the First Principle <note>The One or the Good</note> is not Infinity, for it is the measure of all things, existing as the Good and the One. Nor is Being itself Infinity; for while Being is "infinite," it is not "Infinity" itself. Therefore, Infinity stands between the First Principle and Being, serving as the cause of all things possessing infinite power and the cause of all infinity found within beings.

PROPOSITION XCIII.

**Every infinite thing among beings is infinite neither to those things placed above it, nor to itself.**

DEMONSTRATION.

In whatever respect a thing is infinite, it is also "uncircumscribed" <note>original Latin: *incircunscriptile*, meaning it cannot be bounded or fully grasped</note> to that thing. However, every being is limited <note>defined or bounded</note> both in relation to itself and in relation to all things that come before it. It remains, therefore, that [it is only infinite to things below it]...

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical hierarchy of power, arguing that all finite powers derive from a singular, self-subsisting Infinity that sits between the Absolute One and the realm of Being.</summary>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Proclus's systematic exploration of Neoplatonic metaphysics, specifically focusing on the nature of the "Infinite" and its relationship to "Eternity" and "Power." It follows a discussion on how higher causes relate to their effects.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Diadochus" or "Successor" as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

...to those things which are inferior, it is infinite. This is because the infinity within those lower things is that over which the higher is expanded to such a degree that the higher remains imperceptible to them all. For however much those lower things are directed toward it, the higher possesses something entirely set apart from them. And even if all things enter into it, it nonetheless possesses something hidden from those things which follow, and something incomprehensible. And if it expands its powers, it nevertheless possesses something—by virtue of its union—that is insurmountable, enfolded, and surpassing their unfolding <note>original: "egressum," referring to the Neoplatonic concept of "procession" where reality flows out from a source</note>, containing and limiting itself. It is not infinite to itself, nor much less to those things above it, since it possesses only a portion of the infinity that is in them. For the powers of more universal things are "more infinite," since they are more universal and more closely ordered to the most primary infinity.

### PROPOSITION 94.

**Every eternity is a kind of infinity, but not every infinity is eternity.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or many infinite things do not possess infinity because they always "are." Examples include infinity according to multitude <note>an endless number of things</note>, infinity according to magnitude <note>physical size</note>, and the infinity of matter. This is also true of anything else of that nature, whether it is called infinite because it cannot be fully passed through <note>original: "impertransibile"</note> or because of the lack of a boundary <note>original: "interminationem"</note> in its essence. 

However, it is clear that eternity is a form of infinity. For that which never fails is infinite; and eternity is that which always possesses an unfailing substance. Therefore, the concept of "The Infinite" is prior to "Eternity." For that which constitutes a greater number of things and is more universal is more of a cause. Therefore, above perpetual existence <note>original: "sempiternitatem"</note> stands the First Infinity; and Infinity-in-itself exists before perpetual existence.

### PROPOSITION 95.

**Every power that is more unitary is more infinite than one that is multiplied.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

<summary>Proclus defines the hierarchy of infinity, arguing that while eternity is always infinite, not all infinite things (like matter or numbers) are eternal. He concludes that power increases as it becomes more unified and simple.</summary>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 26<-

### PROOF.
<note>original: "DEMONSTRATIO." This concludes the proof for Proposition 95, which argues that the closer a power is to the ultimate source, the more "unified" it is.</note>
For if the first infinity is closest to the **One** <note>The "One" is the ultimate, indivisible source of all reality in Neoplatonic philosophy.</note>, and belongs to that class of powers which is more akin to the **One**, then that which is further away from it is "more" infinite <note>Meaning it is more divided or fragmented, rather than having the pure, concentrated power of the source.</note>. For as it is multiplied, it loses its **uniformity**, in which it previously held superiority over others while remaining contained through its **indivisibility**. Indeed, in things that can be divided, powers that are concentrated are multiplied in their intensity. But when they are divided, they are weakened.

### PROPOSITION. XCVI.
**Every infinite power existing in a finite body is incorporeal.**

### PROOF.
For if it were corporeal, and this body is finite, then an infinite thing would exist within a finite thing. But if the body is finite, it is not a "power" simply by virtue of being a body. For if it were a power because it is a body, and the body is finite, the power would be finite; thus, an infinite power cannot exist according to the body's physical nature. Therefore, that power which exists as infinite within a finite body must be **incorporeal** <note>This argument suggests that while our bodies are limited in space and time, the "power" of the mind or soul is not bound by physical dimensions.</note>.

### PROPOSITION. XCVII.
**In every series, the primary cause communicates its own property to the entire series; and what that cause is primarily, the series itself is by degree of lessening.** <note>original: "per remissionem." This refers to the way a quality becomes "diluted" or "diminished" as it moves further from its original source.</note>

### PROOF.
For if it is the leader of the entire series, and all things in that same order are arranged toward that cause, it is clear that it bestows upon all of them the **single idea** <note>A "Form" or "Type" that gives the group its shared identity.</note> through which they are ordered under that same series. For either all these things participate in a likeness to that cause without a reason, or the same quality exists in all of them because of that cause. But it is impossible for this to be without a cause. For whatever is without a cause happens by **chance**. However, **chance** will never occur in those things where there is order, mutual connection, and where they always exist in the same way. Therefore, the entire series receives the property of its substance from that primary cause. But if it comes from that cause, it is clear that it occurs with a **lessening**, and a descent appropriate for those things in the second rank. For either [it exists] similarly in the ruler and in the subordinates...

<summary>This page presents Neoplatonic proofs arguing that concentrated power is stronger than divided power, that infinite power (like the soul) must be non-physical if it resides in a limited body, and that all members of a "series" inherit their characteristics from a primary source.</summary>

**Every separate cause is everywhere and nowhere at the same time.**

For by the communication of its power, it is everywhere. For this cause is the fulfillment of those things which are naturally fit to participate in it by themselves, and it is the leader of all secondary things, being present to all in the fruitful progressions of its <term>illuminations</term> <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "illumination" refers to the way a higher principle radiates its influence down to lower levels of reality without losing its own essence.</note>. THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 27

### PROPOSITION 99.

Every **unparticipated thing**, insofar as it is unparticipated, does not derive its existence from another cause. Rather, it is itself the principle and cause of all **participated things**; and thus every principle, in each series, is unbegotten. <note>The term "unparticipated" (original: imparticipabile) refers to a primary source that remains pure and "undivided" even as it creates. A "participated" thing is a version of that source that has been "handed down" or shared with lower levels of reality.</note>

->[Circular library stamp mark]<-

**DEMONSTRATION.**

For if a thing is unparticipated, it has been allotted the primary position in its own **series**, and it does not proceed from others; for if it did, it would no longer be the first. Furthermore, if it were not the first, it would receive this specific property—by which it is unparticipated—from something else. If it is indeed inferior to others and proceeds from them, it does not proceed from them insofar as it is unparticipated, but insofar as it is **participating**. For it participates in those things from which it descends, and the things it participates in are not primary. 

However, that which is unparticipated is primary. Therefore, it is not from a cause insofar as it is unparticipated. For if it were from a cause, it would be a **participant**, and not unparticipated. But insofar as it is unparticipated, it is the cause of participating things, and is not itself participating in others.

### PROPOSITION 100.

Every series of universals is referred back to an **unparticipated cause** and principle. Furthermore, all unparticipated things depend on the **One**, the principle of all things.

**DEMONSTRATION.**

For if every series undergoes the same experience, there is something presiding in each one that is the cause of its identity. For just as all beings come from the One, so too does every series come from a "one" <note>Here, "a one" refers to the specific head of a single chain of being, such as the source of all Intellects or all Souls.</note>. Now, all unparticipated unities are reduced back to the **One itself**, because they are all analogous to the One. Therefore, insofar as they themselves experience something identical—namely, their analogy to the One—their return to the One itself is achieved. 

And insofar as they all come from the One, none of them is the ultimate principle; rather, they are from that One as from a starting point. Yet, insofar as each is individually unparticipated, each is an individual principle. Thus, while being the principles of certain things, they depend upon the **Principle of all**. For the Principle of all is that which all things participate in. All things participate in the first alone; of the others, however—

H 2 <note>This is a "signature mark," a guide for the bookbinder to ensure the pages are folded and assembled in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Proclus establishes that every chain of reality is led by an "unparticipated" source that is not caused by anything else in its category, and that all these primary sources ultimately lead back to "The One."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from general principles of priority to the specific Neoplatonic hierarchy of "The Three Hypostases" or levels of reality: Being, Life, and Mind.</meta>

# **PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR**

...truly not all but some. For this reason, that which is simply first exists. Others, however, are first only in relation to a certain order, but are not "simply" <note>Proclus distinguishes between something that is "first" within a specific category and that which is "simply first" (absolute) in the entire hierarchy of existence.</note> first.

## PROPOSITION 101.

For all things that participate in <term>Mind</term><note>Latin: *mens*. In this context, it refers to the Greek *Nous*, the divine Intellect or the realm of pure thought.</note>, the leader is the unparticipated Mind; and for those that participate in Life, it is Life; and for those that participate in Being, it is Being. Among these themselves, Being is prior to Life, and Life is prior to Mind.

### PROOF.

Since in every order of beings the <term>unparticipated</term><note>Latin: *imparticipabilia*. A technical term in Neoplatonism referring to a "pure" source that exists independently of the things that "participate" in or receive its qualities.</note> precedes those that participate, it is necessary that before intellectual things there is Intellect, and before living things there is Life, and before beings there is Being. Since that which is the cause of more things precedes that which is the cause of fewer, Being will be the most primary among them. For Being is present to all things that possess Life and Intellect. For every living thing, and every thing that shares in intellection, is necessarily a being, but the reverse is not true; for not all beings live and understand <note>For example, a stone "is" (has Being), but it does not "live" or "think."</note>. 

The second is Life. For all things that possess Intellect also participate in Life, but not the reverse; for many things live but are left without experience of knowledge <note>Such as plants or simple animals.</note>. 

The third is Intellect. For every thing that knows in any way both lives and exists. If, therefore, Being is the cause of more things, Life is the cause of fewer, and Intellect of even fewer, then Being is the most primary; then Life, and then Intellect.

## PROPOSITION 102.

All things that are beings in any way exist from <term>Limit</term><note>Latin: *finis*. Referring to the principle of definition, form, and boundary.</note> and the <term>Infinite</term><note>Latin: *infinito*. Referring to the principle of power, growth, and lack of boundary.</note>, through the first Being. All living things are self-moving through the primary Life. All knowing things participate in knowledge through the primary Intellect.

### PROOF.

If that which is unparticipated in each series communicates its own property to all those things that fall under that same series, it is clearly evident that even the most primary Being communicates to all things both Limit and Infinity at once, being a mixture— <note>The text cuts off here, but Proclus is arguing that Being is the first "mixture" of the two highest principles: Limit and the Infinite.</note>

<summary>This page defines the hierarchical structure of reality, arguing that Being is more fundamental than Life, and Life is more fundamental than Intellect, because Being encompasses more things. It also introduces the idea that all existence is a combination of Limit and the Infinite.</summary>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 28

...existing <note>original: "existens"</note> from these first. And **Life** is the motion which is within itself. For Life is the first progression and motion from the stable substance of **Being**. And **Intellect** is cognition, for the summit of all cognition is in the Intellect, and the Intellect is the first cognitive thing.

->## PROPOSITION 103.<-

**All things are in all things, but in each in a manner appropriate to its nature.** <note>This famous Neoplatonic axiom suggests that the entire universe is interconnected; every level of reality contains the others, but expresses them according to its own capacity.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For indeed, in Being, there is both Life and Intellect; and in Life, there is Being and Intelligizing; and in Intellect, there is Being and Living. But all things exist in one place <term>intellectually</term> <note>in the manner of the Mind or Intellect</note>, in another <term>vitally</term> <note>in the manner of Life</note>, and in another <term>essentially</term> <note>in the manner of Being or Substance</note>.

For since each thing exists either according to **cause**, or according to **existence** <note>original: "existentiam," referring to its actualized state or essence</note>, or according to **participation**; in the first <note>Being</note>, the others exist according to cause. In the middle <note>Life</note>, the first exists through participation, and the third <note>Intellect</note> exists through cause. In the third <note>Intellect</note>, those which come before it exist through participation. 

Therefore, in Being, Life and Intellect pre-exist. But since each thing receives its character according to its actual existence—and not according to cause (for it is the cause of others) nor according to participation (for it receives from elsewhere that which it participates)—Being is there, and both Living and Intelligizing are present as **essential Life** and **essential Intellect**. 

And in Life, Being is present according to participation, while Intelligizing is present according to cause. Yet both also exist **vitally**. For it is according to this <note>Life</note> that existence is defined. 

Finally, in Intellect, both Life and Essence <note>Being</note> exist through participation, but both exist **intellectually**. For the Being of the Intellect is cognitive, and its Life is cognition.

->## PROPOSITION 104.<-

**Everything that is primarily eternal has both its essence and its activity eternal.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it has primarily obtained the property of eternity <note>original: "sempiternitatis"</note>, it has not obtained it in one respect while not in another. But in every respect...
