<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of eternity, time, and participation in divine qualities, following the previous discussion on the "everlasting" (sempiternum).</meta>

# PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Diadochus" or successor to Plato’s Academy in Athens. This text is from his "Elements of Theology," a foundational work of Neoplatonic philosophy.</note>

...that participates. For either participating according to its **activity**, it does not participate according to its **essence**. <note>In Neoplatonism, "essence" is what a thing is in its core nature, while "activity" is the way that nature expresses itself in the world.</note> But this is impossible, for the activity would then be more excellent than the essence. Or, if it participates according to its essence, it does not participate according to its activity. In that case, the "primarily everlasting" would be the same as the first thing that participates in time; and time would primarily measure the essence of certain things, while eternity—which is superior to all time—would measure the essence of nothing. This is because the primarily everlasting is not contained, in its essence, by eternity. Therefore, everything that is primarily everlasting possesses both an everlasting essence and an everlasting activity.

## PROPOSITION 105.

Everything immortal is eternal, but not everything eternal is immortal.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if something is immortal, it is that which participates in life forever. <note>original: "vitam semper participat"</note> But that which participates in life forever also participates in "being," and as it is always living, it always exists. Therefore, everything immortal is eternal. For "immortal" describes that which cannot receive death and is always living; whereas "eternal" describes that which cannot receive non-existence and is always a "being." <term>Being (Ens): a philosophical term for anything that exists, used here as the most basic level of reality.</term> 

However, if there are many "beings" that are either superior to or inferior to "life," and yet are not capable of receiving immortality, they can still be things that exist forever. Therefore, not every eternal thing is immortal. It is clear that many things exist forever without being immortal. For there are certain beings devoid of life that are nevertheless always existing and <term>incorruptible</term> <note>Meaning they cannot decay or be destroyed.</note>. For just as "being" relates to "life," so "eternal" relates to "immortal." Life that cannot be taken away is immortality itself, and being that cannot be diminished is eternity. But "being" is a broader category than "life"; therefore, "eternal" is a broader category than "immortal." <note>Proclus is creating a hierarchy: everything that lives must exist (be eternal), but some things might exist (like minerals or mathematical truths) without having "life."</note>

## PROPOSITION 106.

Between that which is entirely everlasting—both in its **essence** and its **activity**—and that which has its essence in **time**, there is a middle ground: that which is in one respect everlasting, but in another respect measured by time.

THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 29

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For that which has an **essence** <term>essence|In this context, the fundamental nature of a thing; what it is in itself as opposed to what it does.</term> encompassed by time is in every respect temporal. For long before this occurs, it has also been allotted a temporal activity. But that which is in every respect temporal is altogether dissimilar to that which is in every respect eternal. However, all **progressions** <note>original: "progreſsiones" — In Neoplatonic thought, this refers to the way reality flows out from the divine "One" into multiplicity.</note> occur through similar things. There is, therefore, something intermediate between these. Either, then, that which is eternal in essence but temporal in activity is the medium, or vice versa. But this <note>Referring to the idea of a temporal essence having an eternal activity.</note> is impossible; for the activity would then be more excellent than the essence. But this is impossible <note>In this system, a thing's fundamental nature must always be equal to or higher than its actions.</note>. It remains, therefore, that the former is the medium.

### PROPOSITION CVII.

**Everything that is in some respect eternal and in some respect temporal, is at once both being and generation.**

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

For every eternal thing is **being**, and that which is measured by time is **generation** <term>generation|Often translated as "becoming"; the realm of change, flux, and physical existence that begins and ends.</term>. Therefore, if the same thing participates in both time and eternity, it will be at once being and generation, though not according to the same respect <note>meaning: it is "being" in one part of its nature and "becoming" in another.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between universal and particular entities within a Neoplatonic hierarchy, specifically how lower orders "return" or connect to higher orders.</meta>

->### PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD), known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) to Plato’s Academy, was the last great philosopher of late antiquity. His "Elements of Theology" provides a systematic, geometric-style proof of Neoplatonic metaphysics.</note>

is dissimilar, just as the particular is to the universal, and as one order is to another. However, the universal is similar to those within the same <term>series</term><note>In Neoplatonism, a "series" or "chain" (seira) refers to a sequence of beings that descend from a single source, like a beam of light hitting different surfaces.</note> because of their shared property. It is also closely coordinated with the property placed above it because of their analogous existence. It is certainly clear that through these intermediaries, a "conversion" <note>original: "conuersio"; this refers to "epistrophe," the process by which a created thing turns back toward its divine source to find fulfillment.</note> toward that higher principle is naturally achieved, as like moves toward like. For a particular is similar to a particular, while that which belongs to the same series is its own property. But the universal of a superior series is dissimilar in both respects.

### PROPOSITION 109.

**Every particular <term>intellect</term>** <note>Proclus uses "Intellect" (Nous) to refer to a level of divine reality above the Soul, characterized by intuitive, non-discursive thought.</note> **participates in that primal unity which is above intellect, both through the universal [intellect] and through the particular unity coordinated with itself. And every particular soul participates in the universal intellect both through the universal soul and through its particular intellect. And the nature of every particular body participates in the universal soul both through universal nature and through its particular soul.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For every particular thing participates in the unity of the superior order either through its own universality or through the particular that exists in that higher order and is coordinated with it.

### PROPOSITION 110.

**Of all things ordered according to a single series, the first members—those joined to their own unity—are able to participate in those things established closest to them in the superior order, by means of analogy. However, the less perfect things, being remote from their own principle, are not naturally suited to enjoy those things.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

Because some things are kindred to those higher beings, having received a more excellent and divine nature within their own order; while others have progressed further [from the source], receiving a secondary and ministerial <note>The "ministerial" or "servant" role refers to lower entities that simply carry out the laws of nature rather than ruling over them.</note> role, rather than a primary and sovereign role within that series...

<summary>Proclus explains the hierarchical ladder of reality, showing how individual souls or intellects connect to the ultimate "One" through a chain of intermediate "universals." He argues that only the most "divine" members of a lower order can directly relate to the order above them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchical progression of beings from the "One" (the ultimate source), specifically focusing on how different levels of reality—Intellect, Soul, and Nature—are structured into "series."</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 30
...progression, of necessity, those things that are innate are joined to those belonging to the superior order, while others remain unjoined to it. For not all things possess an equal dignity, even if they originate from the same arrangement. Nor is there a single rationale <note>original: "ratio"; refers to the underlying principle or logic of their existence.</note>, but rather all things proceed from the **One** and toward the **One** from their own specific unity. Therefore, they do not all obtain the same power. Some are able to continuously receive the <term>participations</term> <note>Participation is the philosophical concept where a lower entity shares in the qualities or essence of a higher source without the higher source being diminished.</note> of the beings above them. Others, however, having become dissimilar through the furthest progressions from the First Principles, are deprived of such power.

### PROPOSITION 111.

In every <term>intellectual series</term> <note>An "intellectual series" (intellectualis ſeriei) refers to the chain of pure Minds or "Intellects" that exist below the level of the Gods but above the level of Souls.</note>, some are divine intellects that receive the participations of the gods, while others are intellects only. In every <term>series of souls</term> <note>animaſticæ ſeriei: the chain of beings that possess "Soul" or life-force.</note>, some are intellectual souls, bound to their own specific intellects, while others are souls only. And in every corporeal <note>Physical or bodily.</note> nature, some have souls placed over them from above, while others are natures only, entirely lacking the presence of souls.

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD), known as "The Successor" (Diadochus) to Plato, was a key philosopher in late antiquity who systematized Neoplatonism.</note>

...of the constitution <note>The Latin 'fionis' is a fragment of 'constitutionis,' referring to the structure of the universe.</note> of all things. Therefore, just as those things are in the first place, so too these obtain a kindred form to the nature of those others. And they appear to be such, through the property of their substance, as are those things which precede them.

### PROPOSITION 113.

**Every divine number has the form of the One.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

If indeed the divine number has for its cause the preceding **One** itself—just as the intellectual number has **Intellect** and the psychic <note>original: "animasticus"; relating to the soul (psyche).</note> number has **Soul**—and if every multitude is everywhere analogous to its cause: then it is certainly clear that the divine number is also **one-form** <term>one-form: also called "henadic"; having the character of unity or the One.</term>. This is because the One itself is God.

Indeed, this is so because the Good itself and the One itself are the same. For the Good itself and God are the same. For that above which there is nothing, and which all things desire, this is God; and he is that from which all things come, and toward which they tend. This, however, is the Good. If therefore there is a multitude of Gods, that multitude is one-form. And that this is so is clear: since every primary cause is the leader of its own multitude, and is similar and kindred to it.

### PROPOSITION 114.

**Every God is a self-perfect unity. And every self-perfect unity is a God.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if the number of unities is twofold—as was shown previously—and some are indeed self-perfect <term>self-perfect: something that is complete in itself and does not depend on a subject or "host" to exist.</term>, while others are "illuminations" <note>An 'illumination' in this context is a lower-level reflection or participation of a higher power that exists in a subject, rather than independently.</note> from them; and since the divine number is kindred and attached to the **One** itself and the **Good** itself, then the gods are self-perfect unities.

And conversely, if there is a self-perfect unity, it is a God. For just as a unity is most excellently kindred to the One itself, and as a self-perfect thing it is most kindred to the Good itself, and through both participates in the divine property, so also the self-perfect unity is a God.

<summary>Proclus argues that because God is synonymous with the "One" and the "Good," any "divine number" (group of gods) must mirror that unity and exist as independent, self-perfect entities called Henads.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, The One, The Good, Henads, divine multitude, self-perfect, theology, metaphysics</keywords>

ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 31

->**COROLLARY.**<-

If indeed there were a truth <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "truth" is often identified with the reality of Being.</note> that was not perfect in itself, or even a substance perfect in itself but not a unity, it would be classified into another rank due to the change in its defining property.

### PROPOSITION 115.

**Every God is super-essential, super-vital, and super-intellectual.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

If it is true that each God is a self-perfect unity, and if each of these three—essence, life, and intellect—is not a unity itself but rather something "unified" <note>original: "vnitum"; refers to something that receives unity from a higher source rather than being unity itself</note>, then it is clearly established that every God is above all the aforementioned: essence, life, and intellect. For if these three differ from one another, yet all things are present in all things—each existing as all—then a thing would no longer be just "one." 

Furthermore, if the First Principle is <term>super-essential</term> <note>Above the category of being or existence; from the Latin "superessentialis."</note>, and every God belongs to the first series (insofar as they are Gods), then each God will be super-essential. That the First is super-essential is clear: for "to be the One" is not the same as "to be Essence." Nor is it the same to be "unified" as to be the One itself. If they are not the same, then either both would be the First—meaning the First would not be the One alone, but something else besides the One—or it would be a participant in the One rather than the One itself. 

But if it were Essence, it would be in need of the One. It is impossible for that which is the Good and the First to be in need. Therefore, that First is the One alone, and thus it is super-essential. If indeed that which each thing is primarily bestows its own property upon its entire <term>series</term> <note>A chain of causes and effects proceeding from a single source.</note>, then the entire divine number is super-essential, since every principal cause produces things like itself before it produces things unlike itself. If, therefore, the first God is super-essential, then all the Gods are super-essential, for in this they shall be alike. If they were mere essences, they would be produced by a "First Essence" as if they were merely the "unities of essences."

### PROPOSITION 116.

**Every God is <term>participable</term>, except for the One.** <note>To be "participable" means a higher reality can be shared or "partaken of" by a lower reality without the higher reality being diminished.</note>

I 2 DEMONSTRATION— <note>The text cuts off here at the beginning of the next proof.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the Gods (Henads) transcend being, life, and intellect because they derive directly from the One, which is itself beyond all categories. It establishes that while the One is absolute and unparticipated, the subsequent Gods can be "participated in" by lower levels of reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs of Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the nature of the "One," the "Henads" (divine units), and their relationship to the rest of existence.</meta>

# **PROCLUS DIADOCHUS**
## **PROOF.**

**F**or it is clear that the first principle is <term>unparticipated</term> <note>original: "imparticipabilis." In Neoplatonism, an unparticipated principle is one that remains "pure" and independent, rather than being "divided" or shared out among the things that depend on it.</note>, lest by being participated—and for that reason belonging to something else—it should no longer be the cause of all things alike, both of those present and of those that truly exist. 

But let us now demonstrate that other <term>units</term> <note>original: "vnitates," referring to the Henads, the divine sparks or "gods" that proceed from the ultimate One.</note> are indeed participated, in this way: if there is another unparticipated unit after the first, how would it differ from the One? Either it exists in the same way as the first—but then how is one the "first" and the other "second"? Or it does not exist in the same way. In that case, the first is the One itself, while this second one is both "one" and "not one." 

But if this second is "not one," provided it is not a substance <note>The translator here uses "substance" for the Latin "substantia," meaning the underlying reality or essence of a thing.</note>, it will be "one" alone. However, if it is some substance other than the One, then that "one" will be participated by something that is "not one." Indeed, that which is perfect in itself is the One, by which it is joined. Therefore, this is again a God. But because it does not subsist as the "One itself," it subsists in the participation of the One. Every unit, therefore, which subsists after the One, is participated; and every God is participated.

## **PROPOSITION 117.**

**E**very God is a measure of beings.

## **PROOF.**

**F**or if every God has the form of the One, he defines and measures all the multitudes of beings. Since all multitudes are by their own nature indefinite, they are defined through the One. The One, however, which is both measuring and limiting, seeks to lead whatever it is present within into a limit—even that which is not naturally limited—according to its own power. For that thing also becomes uniform through participation. It thus departs from indeterminacy and infinity. And the more "one-like" a thing becomes, the less indeterminate and undefined it is. Therefore, every multitude of beings is measured by the divine units.

## **PROPOSITION 118.**

**E**verything, whatever it may be, that exists in the Gods, pre-exists in them according to their own character. And their character is "one-like" and <term>superessential</term> <note>original: "superessentialis." This means existing "above" or "beyond" being or essence itself; the gods inhabit a level of reality more fundamental than the things we can name or define.</note>. Therefore, all things exist in them in a uniform and superessential manner.

## **PROOF—**

<summary>Proclus argues that while the ultimate One is independent, all subsequent gods (units) are "participated" and act as the defining measures that bring order and limit to the infinite variety of existence.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, the One, Henads, participation, divine units, superessential, measure of beings</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the ontological status of the Gods as "Henads" or Unities.</meta>

-># THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 32<-

## PROOF
For everything exists in one of three ways: either by cause, by essence, or by participation. <note>In this philosophical system, a thing can exist as a "cause" (before it is physically manifested), as an "essence" (its actual nature), or by "participation" (receiving a quality from a higher source, like a window participating in the sun's light).</note> The divine number, being the first of all numbers, contains nothing within itself by way of participation. Instead, all things within the Gods exist either through essence or through cause. Furthermore, whatever the Gods have received as the causes of all things, they have received in a way that matches their own perfect union. For everything that acts as a primary cause for secondary things possesses that cause according to its own nature. Therefore, all things exist in the Gods in a unified way and "beyond-being" <term>super-essential: a state of existence that is higher and more fundamental than "being" or "substance" itself</term>.

## PROPOSITION 119.
Every God exists according to this beyond-being goodness; a God is good neither by "habit" <note>an acquired characteristic</note> nor by essence, etc.

## PROOF
For both "habits" and "essences" belong to a lower order that is secondary and far removed from the Gods. The Gods exist beyond-being. If the First Principle is the One and the Good itself—and insofar as it is the One, it is the Good, and insofar as it is the Good, it is the One—then the entire series of Gods is both "one-like" and "good-like." Where there is unity, there is goodness; where there is goodness, there is unity. Those who proceed from the First Principle and follow after it are likewise shaped by goodness and unity. Since the First Principle is the One and the Good itself, it follows that all Gods are Unities <term>Henads: the ultimate units of divine reality, from the Greek 'hen' meaning 'one'</term> and Goodnesses. Just as the "One" is beyond-being for the Gods, so too is the "Good" beyond-being for them; it is not something different from the One. A God is not one thing first and then "good" afterward; rather, a God is simply Goodness itself. Likewise, they are not some other thing first and then "one" afterward; they are simply Unity itself.

->### CONCERNING PROVIDENCE<-

## PROPOSITION 120.
Every God, by their very essence, provides for all things; the primary act of Providence resides within the Gods.

## PROOF—

<summary>This page argues that the Gods are identical with absolute Unity and Goodness, existing on a level above ordinary being, and that their very nature is to provide for all things through Providence.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Henads, Super-essential, Providence, Unity, The Good, Participation, Divine Number</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
# PROCLUS DIADOCHUS
## DEMONSTRATION

**F**or all other things that exist after the Gods exercise providence because of their communion with them. However, in the Gods, providence is innate. For if communicating "the good" to those being provided for is the specific characteristic of a provider, and if all the Gods are "goodnesses" <note>original: "bonitates." In Neoplatonic thought, the Gods are not just good, but are the essence of Goodness itself.</note>, then they will either communicate nothing of themselves—meaning no good would exist in secondary things <note>The "secondary things" refer to everything lower in the cosmic hierarchy, such as the physical world and human souls.</note> (for where would that which is called "good by participation" come from, if not from those who possess these properties in the first place?)—or, if they do communicate, they communicate good things, and in this way they provide for all. Therefore, providence exists first in the Gods. This is even indicated by the name itself; for "Providence" <note>original: "prouidentia." Proclus is referencing the Greek word "Pronoia."</note> is an action that is "before the mind" <note>original: "pro nu" (Greek: *pro nou*). Proclus uses a word-play to argue that Providence acts at a level higher than human or even divine Intellect.</note>, a pre-vision. By their very being, then, the Gods—and by being the essence of goodness—they provide for all things, filling everything with that goodness which exists prior to the mind.

### PROPOSITION 121

**E**very divine being possesses an essence that is goodness itself, a power that is unified, and a knowledge that is secret and incomprehensible to all secondary beings alike.

### DEMONSTRATION

**F**or if a divine being provides for all things, there is within it a power that contains the things for which it provides. Through this power—which is insuperable and indescribable—it has filled all things with itself and made all things subject to itself. For every primary cause and ruler of others dominates and contains them according to its nature, due to the abundance of its power. And truly, the most primary power in the Gods does not rule over some things while ignoring others, nor does it fail to rule all; rather, it commands the powers of all beings equally within itself. This is not an "essential" power <note>meaning a power belonging to the level of "Being" or "Essence."</note>, nor much less a "non-essential" one, but is a power innate to the existence of the Gods and is super-essential <term>super-essential: existing above the level of "Being" or "Essence"; a state of existence closer to the ultimate "One."</term>. 

Furthermore, the limits of all forms of knowledge pre-exist in a unified way within the Gods. Because of this divine knowledge, which is set apart from all things, all other types of knowledge have their basis. This divine knowledge is not "intellectual" <note>meaning it does not function through the "Intellect" or "Mind" (*Nous*), which Proclus considers a lower level of reality than the Gods themselves.</note>, nor much less is it like those types of knowledge that exist after the intellect. Rather, it exists according to the property... <note>The text on this page ends mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the Neoplatonic concept of Providence, arguing that the Gods provide for all things because their very nature is "Goodness" and they possess a "super-essential" power and knowledge that transcends the human intellect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the properties of the Gods (Henads) within Proclus's metaphysical system, specifically transitioning from the nature of divine knowledge and power to the mechanism of divine providence.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 33

... divine property, established above the mind <note>original: "supra mētem cōstitutam" — referring to the Neoplatonic idea that the Gods or 'Henads' exist even above the level of pure Intellect.</note>. If therefore cognition is divine, it is both secret and uniform. If indeed it is power, it is uncircumscribable by all, and encompasses all in the same way. If indeed it is goodness, it defines their essence. For even if all things are in them—cognition, power, and goodness—their essence is characterized by the best, and their subsistence <note>The way in which a thing fundamentally exists.</note> is according to the best; and this is goodness.

## PROPOSITION 122.

**Every divine being both exercises providence toward secondary things, and is separated from the things provided for; neither does providence diminish its unmixed and uniform excellence, nor does its separated union obscure its providence.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For while remaining in their uniform nature and in their essence, they fill all things with their power; and all things capable of **participating** <term>Participation: The way lower things receive a share of the qualities of higher things without the higher thing being diminished.</term> in them enjoy the goods which they can receive according to the measure of their own substance. Since the Gods shine forth with goods by their very being—or rather, by their very pre-existence—to all existing things. For since they are nothing other than "goodnesses," they bestow goods abundantly on all things by their very being; they do not make a distribution by following a process of reasoning, but rather because these [lower] things receive according to their merit, while those [divine] things bestow according to their own essence.

Therefore, they do not receive "relative regards" <note>original: "respectus" — Proclus argues the Gods don't have to "look down" or change their focus to help us; they help simply by existing.</note> toward the things they provide for; for by the very fact of being what they are, they make all things good, and everything that acts by its very being acts without a relative regard. For a "regard" is an addition to one's being, and is therefore contrary to nature. Nor, when they are separated, do they take away providence; for in that way they would take away their own essence (which is a wicked thing to say), the property of which is goodness. For if the gift did not descend into everything that can participate in it, then the greatest thing [the Divine] would not be "good-form," but merely "beneficent" <note>This distinction suggests that the Gods are not just "doing good" as an action, but are the very form of Goodness itself.</note>. Therefore, either no existing thing will have this goodness, because the Gods are prior to all beings; or else the things that are good by participation will have a greater good in them than the primary Goods themselves [which is impossible].

<margin>2</margin>

PRO-

<summary>This page explains that the Gods (the Divine) provide for all lower beings simply by existing, without needing to focus their attention downward or losing their own transcendent purity. It argues that divine providence is an effortless radiation of goodness that matches the capacity of the receiver.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding the nature of the divine, specifically focusing on how the "Gods" (divine unities) relate to the hierarchy of being and knowledge.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>original: "PROCLI DIADOCHI"; Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" or head of the Platonic Academy in Athens.</note>

### PROPOSITION 123.

**Every divine being, in its own nature, is ineffable and unknown to all secondary beings because of its super-essential unity.** However, it can be grasped and known through the things that participate in it. For this reason, only the First Principle is entirely unknown, as it is unparticipated.
<term>super-essential: a translation of the Greek *hyperousios*, meaning existing beyond the level of "being" or "essence." In Proclus’s thought, the divine is "above" existence itself.</term>

### PROOF.

For all knowledge through reason is of beings, and it grasps truth within the realm of beings. Indeed, it reaches intellectual concepts and exists within them. But the Gods are above all beings. Therefore, the divine cannot be grasped by opinion, nor by the wandering of reason, nor by pure intellection. 
<note>original: "intellectione"; refers to *nous*, the highest form of direct intuitive thought in Neoplatonism.</note>

For every being is either sensible (and thus an object of opinion), or purely being (and thus an object of the intellect), or something in between—being and becoming at once (and thus an object of reason). If, therefore, the Gods are super-essential and exist before beings, they are not reached by opinion, science, reasoning, or intellection. Instead, they are known through the distinct properties that belong to them, and this is by necessity. For the differences among participants are divided according to the properties of what is participated. Not everything participates in everything, nor is there a composition of things that are entirely unlike; nor can just anything participate in anything. Rather, what is related is joined to its counterpart and proceeds from it.

### PROPOSITION 124.

**Every God knows divisible things indivisibly, temporal things timelessly, things that are not necessary by necessity, and changeable things unchangeably.** In short, he knows all things in a way that is more excellent than the nature of the things themselves.
<note>This is a fundamental principle of Neoplatonic epistemology: knowledge is determined by the nature of the knower, not the object known.</note>

### PROOF.

For if everything that exists among the Gods is according to their own property, it is surely clear that they do not know according to the nature of the inferior things, but according to their own...
<margin>The divine knows the lower world through its own higher power.</margin>

<summary>Proclus argues that while the divine is "above being" and therefore inherently unknowable to the human mind, it can be studied through the ways lower things participate in it. He also asserts that the Gods know the physical, changing world in a divine, unchanging way.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, theology, super-essential, epistemology, participation, divine knowledge, being vs. becoming</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 124 regarding divine knowledge, followed by the complete text of Proposition 125 from Proclus’s Elements of Theology.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 34

...according to the nature of the inferior things, the knowledge of them existing in the Gods <note>In this Neoplatonic context, "Gods" refers to the Henads—the highest, most unified principles of reality.</note> will not be [according to those lower things], but according to their own transcendent excellence. Therefore, the knowledge of things that are multiple and subject to change <term>passible: capable of being acted upon, suffering, or changing</term> will be unified and unchanging <term>impassive: not subject to external influence or emotion</term>. Moreover, even if the object of knowledge is divisible, divine knowledge is indivisible; and if it is of divided parts, it is non-partite; and if of mutable things, it is immutable; and if of contingent things, it is necessary; and if of the indefinite, it is definite. For the divine does not receive knowledge from inferior things, such that the knowledge would be shaped by the nature of the object; rather, inferior things are defined by the definition of the Gods, and they change around the immutable, and they receive in a changing manner <note>original: "passibiliter"</note> that which is unchanging, and they receive temporally that which is without time. For it is possible for inferior things to be surpassed by those that are superior, but for the Gods to receive anything from inferior things is forbidden <note>original: "nephas"—suggesting something that is fundamentally against divine law or the nature of reality.</note>.

### PROPOSITION 125.

Every God, from whichever order he begins, proceeds through all subsequent things to reveal himself. He is always multiplying his communications and dividing them, yet preserving the specific character of his own substance.

### DEMONSTRATION.

For since progressions <note>In Neoplatonism, "progression" or "procession" is the way higher reality flows down into lower levels of existence without losing its source.</note> occur through a diminution <term>remission: a slackening or decrease in intensity as one moves further from the source</term>, primary things always multiply as they descend into secondary things. Those things that proceed according to their likeness to their producers receive their own appropriate ranking. Thus, the whole becomes in a way both the same and another; it proceeds while also remaining. Because of the diminution, it appears as something else; however, because of its continuity with the source, it does not depart from its original identity. Whatever character the first thing possesses, it establishes that same character in the secondary things, thus preserving the unbreakable communion of the series. 

It is therefore clear that each of the Gods is revealed in a manner appropriate to the ranks in which he makes that revelation; he proceeds from there even to the furthest limits through the generative power of the primary causes. He always multiplies the progression, which moves from the one into the many. Yet, throughout this progression—due to the similarity of the things produced to the identity of each series—he preserves the principal and primal-making cause.

K PRO- <note>This is a catchword, indicating the first word of the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains that divine knowledge is superior to its objects, remaining unified and unchanging even when knowing the complex and changing world. It also introduces the law of divine procession, where a God manifests through various levels of reality while maintaining his essential identity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical sequence of Proclus's *Elements of Theology*, focusing on the nature of the divine Henads (Unities) and their relationship to the One.</meta>

# **PROCLUS DIADOCHUS**

## **PROPOSITION 126**
**E**very God <note>original: "Omnis Deus". Proclus uses "God" to refer to the Henads, the supreme unities that proceed from the One.</note> is more universal the closer he is to the One, and more particular the further away he is.

### **DEMONSTRATION**
**F**or he who is the cause of more things is closer to the One, who produces all things; however, he who is the cause of fewer things is further away. He who is the cause of more things is more universal, and he who is the cause of fewer is more particular. Each of these is a unity <term>Unities (Henads): The individual, divine building blocks of reality that exist immediately after the absolute One.</term>, but one is greater in power, while the other is lesser in power. These more particular beings are generated from the more universal ones without the universal being divided (for they are unities) and without any alteration (for they are immobile), and without any dependency on the many (for they are unmixed). Instead, by their own nature, they produce secondary progressions through an abundance of power, which are diminished in comparison to the primary ones.

## **PROPOSITION 127**
**E**very divine being is primarily and supremely simple, and for this reason, is most self-sufficient.

### **DEMONSTRATION**
**T**hat a divine being is simple is clear from its status as a union <note>original: "ex unione". Proclus argues that to be divine is to be perfectly unified.</note>, for every divine thing is supremely "one." That which is of such a nature is excellently simple. Furthermore, that such a being is most self-sufficient can be learned if one considers that a composite thing is needy—not only of things outside of itself, but also of the very parts from which it is composed. However, that which is most simple, uniform, and "one" is identical to the Good; and by establishing itself in this Good, it is most self-sufficient. Every divine being is of this nature. Therefore, it neither needs other things (being goodness itself) nor does it need the things from which it is made, since it is uniform.

## **PROPOSITION 128**
**E**very God is participated in <note>In this philosophical system, "participation" describes how a lower level of reality receives the influence or qualities of a higher level.</note> immediately by those things which are near to him, but by those things which are distant, he is participated in through intermediaries, whether they be few or many.

<meta>This page continues the metaphysical hierarchy established by Proclus, focusing on how divinity descends from the "One" through intermediate stages—Intellect and Soul—to reach even the physical "Body."</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 35

## PROOF.

For those things which are <term>uniform</term> <note>original: "vniformia"; things that possess the quality of the One</note> through their kinship, because they are so, can themselves participate in the divine <term>unities</term> <note>original: "vnitates"; referring to the "Henads," the first emanations from the One</note>. But those things which, through their lessening and their extension into a <term>multitude</term> <note>original: "multitudinem"; the state of being many or divided</note>, have need of others that are more unified, participate in those things which are unities in themselves, rather than being merely "unified." For between unity-in-itself and a divided multitude, there exists a "unified multitude." This is capable of coalescing into unity through a process of union, yet it remains akin to the divided multitude because of its many parts.

## PROPOSITION 129.

Every divine body is divine through a divine soul. Every divine soul, however, is divine through a divine intellect. But every divine intellect is divine through participation in a divine unity; and the unity itself is God by its own nature. Thus, the intellect is the most divine thing, the soul is divine, and the body is god-like in form.

## PROOF.

For if the whole number of divine things exists above the intellect, and if participations are accomplished through things that are akin and similar, then: 

First, the impartible essence <note>the Intellect</note> will participate in the <term>super-essential</term> <note>original: "superessentiales"; beyond the level of Being or Essence</note> unities. 

Second, that which is joined to generation <note>the Soul</note> will participate. 

Third, generation itself <note>the physical Body</note> will participate, each through the level immediately placed above it. 

And indeed, the property of the Gods proceeds even to the last things among the participants, through those intermediaries which are akin to them. For Unity first gives to the intellect its own distinguished power among divine things, and also makes that intellect such as it is itself, according to a uniform multitude. But through the intellect, it is also present to the soul, drawing it to the intellect and setting it on fire <note>original: "coinflammans"; a metaphor for spiritual or intellectual illumination</note>. 

If that intellect is of a type that can be participated in, it also bestows its property even to the body through the soul—provided that the body is a participant of a soul. And thus the body becomes not only animated and intellectual, but also divine; receiving life and motion from the soul, but an indissoluble permanence from the intellect. But union...

->## PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

...the divine by its participated unity. For everything communicates its own essence to those things that follow it.

### **PROPOSITION 130.**

**In every divine order, the primary members depend more on those causes immediately ranked above them than the secondary members do on their own subordinates. Conversely, the secondary members depend more on those immediately above them than those that come after them depend on them.**

->**DEMONSTRATION**<-

For the more uniform and universal a thing is, the greater the pre-eminence it receives over those things that follow it; and the more diminished it is in power, the more closely it is joined with what follows. <note>Proclus is explaining a "cascading" effect: higher beings are more powerful and "purer," while lower beings are more diluted and mixed with the things they create.</note> Higher things are indeed more united to their own causes, while lower things are less so. For it is a mark of greater power to be more separated from inferior things and more united to superior ones. Conversely, to recede from the superior and to "sympathize" with the inferior is a diminution of power. This indeed happens to the secondary members in every order, but not to the primary ones.

### **PROPOSITION 131.**

**Every God begins his own activity from himself.**

->**DEMONSTRATION**<-

For a divine being first displays within himself the characteristic of his presence toward secondary things, because he also communicates himself to others according to his **super-fullness** <term>superplenitudinem: a Neoplatonic concept where a being is so overflowing with perfection that it naturally shares its essence without losing anything itself</term>. For neither deficiency nor mere "fullness" is characteristic of the Gods. For anything deficient is imperfect; and it is impossible for something to make another thing perfect when it is not perfect itself. That which is merely "full" is only self-sufficient, but not yet ready to share or communicate. Therefore, that which fills other things and extends its gifts into others must be "super-full." If, then, the divine fills all things with good by its own means, everything within it is super-full. If this is so, it establishes first within itself the characteristic...

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 36

### PROPOSITION 132.
All the orders of the Gods are bound together by a middle term. <term>middle term</term> <note>Latin: *medietati*. In Proclus's philosophy, reality is a continuous chain; "middle terms" act as bridges or intermediaries to ensure there are no abrupt jumps between the highest and lowest levels of divinity.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For all the progressions of beings are made to be very similar to one another; and even more truly, the orders of the Gods possess an unbreakable continuity, as they exist in a uniform way and are defined according to the one primary cause of them all. Therefore, the descents <note>Latin: *remissiones*. Literally "slackening" or "easing," referring to how divine power gradually diminishes as it flows further from the source.</note> occur in a unified way, solely through the resemblance of secondary things to the primary ones found within all beings. Indeed, the essence of the Gods is situated in union far more than the essence of ordinary beings is. 

Thus, all divine classes are bound to their own middle terms. The primary things do not proceed immediately into entirely different progressions, but rather move through classes common to both—those from which they proceed and for which they are the immediate causes. For these middle terms bring the extremes together into a single union; they are naturally suited to those things placed below them, while they depend closely on those above them, thus preserving the well-ordered generation of divine things.

### PROPOSITION 133.
Every God is a beneficial unity, or a unifying goodness. And they possess this essence insofar as each is a God. But the very first principle is simply Goodness itself, and simply Unity itself. However, of those who come after the First, each is a *certain* goodness and a *certain* unity.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For the divine property has distinguished the unities and goodnesses of the Gods. Thus, each God, according to some specific character <term>character</term> <note>Latin: *idioma*. The specific "flavor" or individualizing trait that makes one god different from another (e.g., a "perfecting" god versus a "guarding" god).</note> of goodness, brings good to all things—such as by perfecting, or containing, or guarding them. For each of these acts is a *certain* good, but not *the whole* of good. Indeed, the First principle established itself as the uniform cause. Therefore, that First is Goodness itself, acting as the foundation of all goodness. For not all the Gods—

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

The essences of the gods are simultaneously made equal to the One <note>original: "uni"</note>, so great is the superiority that the One has obtained over the multitude of gods.

### PROPOSITION 134.

**Every divine <term>intellect|In Neoplatonism, "Intellect" (Nous) refers to a higher level of reality characterized by pure, non-discursive thought</term> understands as an intellect, but exercises <term>providence|The divine care and ordering of the lower world</term> as a God.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For knowing existing things <note>original: "entia"</note> and reaching perfection in acts of understanding is characteristic of the intellect. However, it belongs to a God to exercise providence and to fill all things with goodness. This sharing and filling occurs through a union of those who provide with those who come before them. The intellect, by imitating this, reaches the same state as the <term>intelligible|Objects of pure thought, rather than physical senses</term> realms. 

Therefore, in the act of providing, it acts as a God, since providence exists even before the action of the intellect. For this reason, it communicates itself to all things as a God, but it is not present to all things as a mind. For where the intellectual property does not reach, the divinity still extends. Indeed, even those things that do not have the power of understanding still desire to be provided for and to participate in some good. This is because not everything seeks the intellect—even among those things capable of participating in it—but all things seek the Good and hasten to acquire it, etc.

### PROPOSITION 135.

**Every divine unity is participated in immediately by some single being, and everything that is <term>deified|Elevated to a god-like state through union with the divine</term> is directed toward a single divine unity. There are as many participating classes of beings as there are participating unities <note>In Proclus's system, "unities" (henads) are the primary manifestations of the One</note>.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For two or more unities are not shared by a single being. For if the properties within those unities were swapped, how would the nature naturally born <note>original: "connatum"</note> with each being not also be changed? This is because the connection occurs through similarity. Nor is a single unity shared by many beings in a divided way; for many things are disconnected from unity, both as beings are to that which exists before all beings, and as many—

### ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 37

-> ### PROPOSITION 136 <-

**Every God**, existing as more universal and ordered closer to the First <note>The "First" refers to the First Principle or the Absolute One, the source of all existence</note>, is participated in by a more universal genus of beings. Conversely, a more particular God, further removed from the First, is participated in by a more particular genus of beings. As being is related to being, so is unity related to divine unity.

-> **DEMONSTRATION** <-

**If** there are as many beings as there are unities, and conversely, if one is participated in by one, it is quite clear that the order of beings arises according to the order of unities, modeled after that which exists before it. Thus, more universal beings coalesce with more universal unities, while more particular beings align with more particular unities. If this were not so, then dissimilar things would be joined to dissimilar things, and there would be no distribution according to rank <note>original: *dignitatem*; refers to the "worth" or "merit" of a thing's position in the cosmic hierarchy</note>; but such things are impossible. Indeed, in all other things, the One and its own proper measure shine forth and descend from there. Therefore, there will be an even greater order of participation among these, arranging similar things according to the power of their similarity.

-> ### PROPOSITION 137 <-

**Every unity**, together with the One, establishes the being that participates in it as a being.

-> **DEMONSTRATION** <-

**For** just as the One is the constitutive cause of all things, so it is also the cause of the participated unities <note>These "participated unities" are often called "Henads" in Neoplatonic philosophy—the divine sparks or gods that bridge the gap between the Absolute One and the world of beings</note> and of the beings that are drawn toward those unities. That which depends on each thing—namely, the unity which shines forth within it—produces it. To "be" in a simple sense is the work of the One. But to be "inborn" <note>original: *connatum*; literally "born with," meaning a nature that is intrinsic or natural to the being</note> is the work of the unity, which...

<meta>This page continues Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the hierarchy of reality. It transitions from a discussion on the nature of "super-essential" divinity into specific propositions regarding "Being" as the primary participant in the Divine.</meta>

# **PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR**

to which it is connatural, is therefore itself that which through itself defines Being <note>original: "ens"</note> as a participant of it, and demonstrates its super-essential <term>super-essential: that which exists beyond or above the category of "Being" or "Essence," usually referring to the One or the Gods</term> property within it essentially. For from that which is primary, it proceeds everywhere secondarily to be that which it is. If, therefore, there is any property of the super-essential Divinity, it belongs also to the Being that participates in it essentially.

### **PROPOSITION 138**

**O**f all things that participate in the divine property and are deified <note>original: "Deificatorum"; referring to things elevated to a divine state through participation</note>, the very first and highest is Being itself.

### **DEMONSTRATION**

**F**or if Being is above Intellect and above Life, as has been demonstrated <note>Proclus argues that "to be" is more fundamental than "to think" (Intellect) or "to live" (Life)</note>—for if Being is the cause of more things after the One, it must be the highest. For it is more uniform than these others, and for that reason altogether more venerable. Nothing else exists before it except the One. For before a unified plurality <note>original: "uniformem multitudinem"</note>, what else could there be but the One? Moreover, a unified plurality is Being itself, as it exists from the Limit and the Infinite, and is altogether the super-essential Being before essence. Since even in the divine illuminations, the second is reached only by the One, which extends beyond Being. But Being follows immediately after the One. For that which is Being in potentiality, though not yet Being in actuality, is "one" according to its own nature; and that which follows this is already Being in actuality. And thus among the principles of Being, that which is immediately above Being is "non-being," as it is more excellent than Being, and is the One.

### **PROPOSITION 139**

**A**ll things that participate in the divine unities <note>original: "unitates"; also known as Henads, the highest bridge between the One and the rest of existence</note>, beginning from Being, end in corporeal nature.

### **DEMONSTRATION**

**F**or the first of the participants is Being, and the last is the Body. Indeed, we even speak of "divine bodies." For the highest of all genera are dedicated to the Gods: the highest of bodies, of souls, and of minds, so that in every order there are things analogous to the Gods, which contain and preserve those of the second rank. In this way, every number <note>here meaning a "series" or "order" of beings</note> is a whole, existing as a "whole in the part," having all things within itself—and before all else, the divine property. It exists, therefore, even corporal-
                                                                                             **ly,** <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "ter," completing "corporaliter" (corporeally) on the following page.</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that Being is the highest principle that can participate in the Divine, sitting just below the One. He then establishes a chain of divinity that extends from Being all the way down to physical bodies.</summary>

ELEMENTA THEOLOGICA. 38

...ly, and animistically <note>original: *animastice*; pertaining to the level of the Soul</note>, and intellectually <note>original: *mentaliter*; pertaining to the level of the Mind or Intellect</note> the divine race, and it is clear that all these things are divine through participation. For the primarily divine consists in unities <note>original: *unitatibus*; these are the "Henads," the ultimate, irreducible units of the divine in Proclus's system</note>. Therefore, those things participating in divine unities begin indeed from being, but end at incorporeal nature.

### PROPOSITION 140.

**O**mne divine powers, beginning from above and proceeding through their own proper intermediaries, reach even unto the last things and the regions around the earth.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**N**or does anything prohibit them or forbid their presence in all things. For they lack nothing—neither locations nor distances—because of their unsurpassable excellence over all things and their unmixed presence everywhere. Nor is that which is fit to participate in them prohibited from that participation; rather, as soon as anything becomes ready for participation, the powers are present. They are not "arriving" at that moment, nor were they absent before, but they are always in the same state. 

If, therefore, anything among those things around the earth should be fit to participate, the powers are present to it and fill all things with themselves. They are more present to the higher things, but present to the middle things according to their order, and to the last things, finally. From above, therefore, they extend themselves to the last things; thus, even in these lowest things there are images of the first things, and all things "sympathize" with all others <note>original: *compatiuntur*; referring to the concept of cosmic sympathy where all parts of the universe are interconnected</note>. In the first things, the second pre-exist; in the second things, the first are apparent. For everything exists in three ways: either by cause, by existence, or by participation.

### PROPOSITION 141.

**O**mne providence of the Gods is either distinct from those things for which it provides, or else it is coordinated with them.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

**F**or one kind of providence, according to its existence and the property of its order, is entirely spread above the things it illuminates; the other, being of the same... <margin>of the same</margin>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-
<note>Latin: *Proclus Diadochus*. Proclus (412–485 AD) was the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens; "Diadochus" means "The Successor" to Plato.</note>

...of the same order of reason, they provide for those subjects within the same arrangement: and they imitate the provident action of the separate Gods, and these very secondary beings desire to fill [their subjects] with whatever goods they are able.

### PROPOSITION 142.

**All Gods are present to all things in the same way, but not all things are present to the Gods in the same way.** Instead, each individual thing participates in their presence according to its own rank and power—some in a uniform way, others in a manifold way; some eternally, others according to time; and some without physical bodies, others bodily.
<term>Participation: In Neoplatonic philosophy, "participation" describes how lower, material things receive life, form, or light from higher, divine sources without the source being diminished.</term>

#### DEMONSTRATION.

It is necessary that a difference in the <term>participation</term> of the same things arises either from the participant or from that which is participated. However, every divine being always maintains the same order, and is detached from all relative connections and is **unmixed**.
<note>The term "unmixed" (Latin: *incommixtum*) suggests that the divine remains pure and unaffected by the lower things it influences.</note>
Therefore, it remains that the change arises from the participant; the Gods do not exist in these things in the same way, but these things relate to the Gods differently at different times, and different things relate to them in different ways. Thus, while the Gods are present to all things in the same way, all things are not present to them in the same way. Rather, as each thing is able, so it is present; and as it exists, so it enjoys them. Indeed, the participation of these things is according to the measure of the divine presence they can hold.

### PROPOSITION 143.

**All inferior things depart from the presence of the Gods, even if the participant is suitable.** Everything alien to the divine light withdraws, yet all things are suddenly illuminated by the Gods.

#### DEMONSTRATION.

For divine things always encompass more, and are more powerful than those things which proceed from them; however, the unfitness of the participants becomes the cause of the absence of divine light. For the participant's own infirmity weakens even that light. When that light is weakened, some other kind of dominion seems to take over—not because of a change in divine power, but because of the impotence of the participant—so that the participant seems to depart from the illumination of the divine form.

PRO-

<summary>This page explains that while the Gods offer their presence equally to all of creation, individual things receive that presence differently based on their own capacity, and any perceived absence of the divine is due to the weakness of the receiver, not a withdrawal by the Gods.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, divine presence, participation, illumination, divine light, hierarchy of being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic exploration of Neoplatonic theology, specifically focusing on the relationship between the "Gods" (the highest unities or Henads) and the rest of creation. It builds on previous propositions regarding how divine power "cascades" down through different levels of reality.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 39<-

### PROPOSITION 144.
**All beings, and all the organized ranks of beings, have proceeded only as far as the divine arrangements extend.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
For since the Gods produced all beings in association with themselves, it is impossible for anything to exist, or to receive its measure and order, outside of the Gods. For all things are perfected, ordered, and measured by the power of the Gods. Therefore, even before the lowest <term>genera</term> <note>biological or logical categories; here meaning "types" or "classes" of existence</note> of beings, the Gods exist to arrange them, granting them life, form, and perfection, and turning them back toward the Good itself. This is true for the middle ranks and the highest ranks alike; all things are bound to and rooted in the Gods, and it is for this reason they are preserved. If anything were to depart from the Gods and become isolated, it would slip entirely into non-being and perish, being utterly deprived of those things which held it together.

### PROPOSITION 145.
**The specific quality of every divine rank permeates fruitfully through all things, giving itself to all lower classes of being.**

### DEMONSTRATION.
If beings proceed as far as the divine orders extend, then in every single class of being, the specific quality of the divine powers shines down from above. Each thing carries a quality from its own immediate cause, according to how that cause received its own essence.

For example: if there is a **Purifying** Divinity, then purification exists in souls, in animals, in plants, and even in stones. Likewise, if there is a **Protective** Divinity, or a **Converting** <note>Turning things back toward their source</note> one, or a **Perfecting** or **Life-giving** one, the same pattern holds. A stone participates in the purifying power only in a physical way. A plant, however, participates more clearly according to its life-force. An animal possesses this quality through its form of motion; a rational soul possesses it through reason; and the intellect possesses it intellectually. But the Gods possess it in a <term>supra-essential</term> <note>original: "superessentialiter"; meaning a state of being that is beyond the definition of "substance" or "existence" as we understand it</note> and unified way. The entire series possesses the same power.

->L 2<-

<summary>This page argues that all existence is rooted in the divine, asserting that nothing can exist outside the influence of the Gods. It describes how divine attributes like "purification" or "protection" filter down through every level of reality, from the highest intellects to common stones.</summary>

Everything, however, is full of the Gods <note>This is a famous phrase originally attributed to the first Greek philosopher, Thales.</note>, and whatever each individual thing possesses according to its own nature, it possesses from that source.

### PROPOSITION 146
**The ends of all divine progressions are made like unto their own beginnings, maintaining a circle without beginning and without end through their return toward their origins.**

#### DEMONSTRATION
For even if every single thing that proceeds forth returns to the specific principle from which it originated, then surely much more so must the universal orders, having proceeded from their summit, return back to that summit. This return of the end to the beginning makes the whole into a single, finished unit, inclined toward itself, demonstrating a uniformity within its many parts through this shared inward leaning.

### PROPOSITION 147
**The summits of all divine orders are made like unto the ends of the orders positioned above them.**

#### DEMONSTRATION
If there must be a continuous progression of the divine, and if each individual order is to be bound together by its own intermediaries, it is necessary that the summits <term>summits: the highest, most unified point of a specific rank of being</term> of the second orders be connected to the boundaries of the first. This joining occurs through "similitude" or likeness. Therefore, there will be a likeness between the beginnings of a lower order and the ends of the order placed above it.

### PROPOSITION 148
**Every divine order is united to itself in a threefold manner: from the summit within it, from its middle, and from its end.**

#### DEMONSTRATION
For that summit, possessing the most singular power (so to speak), transmits union to the entirety of that order, and unites the whole...

<meta>Continuing from page 39, which likely discussed the three stages of divine existence: remaining in the cause, proceeding from it, and returning to it.</meta>

->ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 40<-

...abiding in itself from above. The <term>middle</term> <note>In this metaphysical system, the 'middle' acts as a bridge or mediator between the highest cause and the lowest effect.</note>, however, reaching to each of the extremes, connects the whole around itself. It conveys the gifts of the first things and extends the powers of the last things, instilling communion and mutual connection in all. For in this way, one coordination arises from both the fulfillers and the fulfilled, as if they were all inclining together toward a certain center in the middle. That which ends, in turn, is converted back toward the beginning; by leading back the powers that have <term>proceeded</term> <note>Procession (procedens) refers to the way reality flows outward from the divine source.</note>, it provides similarity and a shared inclination to the whole order. Thus, every order is at once a single unity, because of the unifying power of the first things, because of the containment found in the middle, and because of the <term>conversion</term> <note>Conversion (conversio) is the Neoplatonic term for the way things "turn back" to contemplate and reunite with their source.</note> of the end of the progressions back to the beginning.

### PROPOSITION 149.

**Every multitude of divine unities is limited in number.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if a thing is close to the One, it does not exist as infinite; for the infinite is not akin to the One, but alien to it. If multitude as such recedes from the One, it is clear that an infinite multitude is entirely abandoned by it. For that reason, it would also be powerless and ineffective. Therefore, the multitude of gods is not infinite. It is therefore uniform and limited—and indeed more limited than any other multitude. For it is more akin to the One than any other multitude. 

If, then, the multitude is a principle, it ought to be less of a multitude the closer it is to the primary principle, and more of a multitude the farther away it is. For that which is closer is more similar. But since the One itself is the first principle, the multitude touching it is "less" of a multitude than that which is farther away. The infinite, however, is not "less" of a multitude, but is the greatest multitude possible.

### PROPOSITION 150.

**Every proceeding thing in the divine orders is not naturally capable of receiving all the powers of its producer; nor, in general, are secondary things capable of all the powers of those that exist before them. Rather, the prior things have certain powers that are withheld from the inferior things and are incomprehensible to those that follow after them.**

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>Continuing from Proposition 150, which discusses how divine properties are distributed among the gods according to their rank.</meta>

->PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR 113<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was known as the "Successor" (Diadochus) because he headed the Academy in Athens, centuries after Plato. This text is from his *Elements of Theology*.</note>

->PROOF.<-

For if the properties of the gods differ, those of the lower gods pre-exist in the higher ones, while those of the higher gods are more universal <term>universaliores: having a broader scope of influence</term> and do not exist in the lower ones. But superior beings sometimes bestow properties upon those they produce, and other times they possess them beforehand in an eminent way within themselves. For it has been demonstrated that those closer to the "One" <note>The "One" is the ultimate, indivisible source of all reality in Neoplatonic thought.</note> are more universal, while those further away are more particular.

Now, if the more universal beings possess powers that encompass those of the more particular, then those of the second and more particular rank will not encompass the power of the superior ones. There is, therefore, something in the higher beings that is incomprehensible and "uncircumscribed" <note>original: "incircumscriptibile"—meaning it cannot be limited or fully grasped by a lower mind</note> to those in the lower ranks. For every divine being is truly infinite; it has been shown to be its own being, not merely existing before itself, but far more so before all things that come after it. 

The infinity in those higher beings is according to their power. However, the infinite is incomprehensible to those for whom it is infinite. Therefore, the lower beings do not participate in all the powers which the superior beings possessed beforehand within themselves. For those powers were incomprehensible to the second-rank beings, just as, indeed, the second-rank beings are to them. Thus, the lower beings do not possess all the qualities of the higher ones because they are more particular; nor do they possess the qualities they *do* share in the same manner as the higher ones, because of that infinity which makes the higher beings transcend the lower.

->PROPOSITION 151.<-

Everything paternal <term>paternum: the "fatherly" or creative principle</term> among the Gods is primordial and pre-existing in the order of the Good, extending through all divine arrangements.

->PROOF.<-

For the paternal principle produces the very essences, the entire powers, and the substances of the secondary beings according to a single, ineffable <note>Too sacred or vast to be expressed in words.</note> excellence. This is why it is called "paternal," as it displays the unified and "good-form" <term>boniformem: having the nature or shape of the Good</term> power of the One, acting as the procreative cause of secondary things. 

In every order of the Gods, the paternal genus acts as the leader, producing all things from itself and organizing them. This is because it is arranged in an analogy to the Good itself. And among these "Fathers," some are indeed more universal, while others are more particular, just as the orders of the Gods themselves are distinguished by more universal and more particular qualities.

<summary>This page argues that higher gods possess infinite, universal powers that lower gods cannot fully grasp, and introduces the 'Paternal' principle as the creative source modeled after the ultimate Good.</summary>

### PROPOSITION 152.
**E**very generative power of the Gods proceeds according to the infinity of divine power; it multiplies itself, permeates through all things, and excellently demonstrates an unceasing quality in the progression of secondary beings.

#### DEMONSTRATION.
**F**or to multiply those things which proceed, and to lead them forth into new growth from their hidden enfolding within their causes—to what else could this belong but to the infinite power of the Gods? Through this power, all divine things are filled with fertile blessings. Therefore, the generative nature produces other things from itself through its "over-full" power. <note>original: "superplenam potentiam." This refers to the idea that the Divine does not lose anything by creating, but overflows because it is more than full.</note> It is characteristic of the generative Deity to have mastery over power, multiplying the powers of those things it brings forth, making them fruitful, and stirring them up to generate and produce other things. For if individual things first communicate the specific property they possess to others, then surely every fruitful thing imparts a fruitful progression to those that come after it. It represents that primal infinity of all things from which every generative power has proceeded, pre-eminently pouring forth the perpetual progressions of the divine.

This refers to the Henads or Gods who exist above the level of Being or Mind.</note>, so too are there different perfections. Some perfections belong to the Gods themselves according to their essence, while others belong to the beings that come second after them. The former are perfect in themselves and are the primary makers, because the Good is present in them primarily; the latter, however, possess perfection through participation. <term>Participation: the way a lower thing shares in the qualities of a higher thing without the higher thing being diminished.</term> Therefore, the perfection of the Gods is one thing, and the perfection of those things which are deified <note>Refers to souls or intellects that have been elevated to a god-like state.</note> is another. <meta>This page continues Proclus's *Elements of Theology*, focusing on the specific functions of divine orders—perfecting, protecting, and life-giving—and how they relate to the hierarchy of being.</meta>

-># OF PROCLUS THE SUCCESSOR<-

In the Gods, the **perfect** exists primarily; it is the cause of perfection not only for those who are deified <note>original: "deificatis"; those lower beings raised to a divine state</note>, but also for the Gods themselves. For if, insofar as it is perfect, everything turns back toward its own principle, then the perfecting class of the Gods is the cause of every divine **reversion** <term>reversion: the Neoplatonic concept of 'epistrophe', where an effect seeks to return to and unite with its source</term>.

->### PROPOSITION 154<-

**Everything that is protective among the Gods preserves each thing in its proper order, kept uniformly separate from secondary things and established in primary things.**

->### PROOF<-

For if protection immutably preserves the measure of each order and contains everything preserved in its own perfection, it bestows on all things a superiority over lesser things, and establishes each one permanently in itself without mixing; being the cause of unblemished purity for the things protected, it grafts them into the higher things. For everything that is immediately close to the first things is perfect, remains in itself, and extends above lesser things.

->### PROPOSITION 155<-

**Every life-giving thing among the divine classes is a generative cause, but not every fertile order is life-giving.**

->### PROOF<-

For the "generative" is more universal than the "life-giving" and is closer to the First Principle. **Generation** shows the cause that produces beings into a multitude. **Life-giving**, however, represents the Deity who bestows all life. Therefore, if the former multiplies the substances of beings, while the latter establishes the progressions of life—just as Being relates to Life—so the generative order will relate to the life-giving series. Thus, it will be more universal, the cause of more things, and for that reason closer to the Principle <note>Proclus argues that 'Being' is a broader category than 'Life', because all things that live have being, but not all things that have being are alive.</note>.

->### PROPOSITION 156<-

**Every cause of purity is contained within the protective order. However, the reverse is not true; the preserving order is not the same as the purifying class.**

->### PROOF<-

For purity bestows on all Gods a lack of mixing with lesser things, and keeps that which is purified within the providence of fertile things.
