<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine attributes and the hierarchy of causes, specifically focusing on the protective, purifying, and paternal functions within the Neoplatonic system of Proclus.</meta>

# ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 42

Preservation <note>original: *Custodia*; refers to the divine power that keeps things in their proper place</note> also achieves this: it contains all things within itself and firmly establishes them upon those things which are superior to them. Therefore, the power of preservation is more universal than that of purification. For it is the specific property of preservation to maintain the order of each thing, both in relation to itself and in relation to those things which come before it and after it. Purification <note>original: *puritatis*; the act of cleansing or separating</note>, however, is the act of separating the superior from the inferior. These powers exist first in the Gods. For it is necessary that one cause precede all others. That which exists in all things, and indeed in the whole, are the uniform measures of all good things, which are received beforehand from the Gods; there is no good in secondary things that does not already exist in the Gods by way of a cause. From where else would it come, and what cause would it have? In the Gods, therefore, purity is a primary good, as is preservation, and every quality of that kind.

[A faint circular stamp is visible on the right side of the page]

**PROPOSITION 157.**

Every paternal cause is a giver of being to all things and establishes the essences of beings. But every Demiurge <term>Demiurge: From the Greek 'demiourgos', meaning a craftsman or artisan; here referring to the divine power that shapes and organizes the world</term> precedes the formation of composite things, their order, and the division of them according to number; this power belongs to the same rank as the paternal, but within more specific classes of beings.

**DEMONSTRATION.**

Both belong to the rank of "Limit" <note>In this philosophy, 'Limit' and 'Infinity' are the two primary principles below the One</note> itself, because essence, number, and form are all characterized by limit. Thus, these are joined to one another. But while the Demiurge leads the process of creation into multiplicity, the Father <note>the paternal cause</note> provides the uniform progressions of beings. One is form-giving <note>original: *formificum*</note>, while the other is substance-giving <note>original: *substantificum*</note>. Therefore, just as form and being differ from each other, so the paternal role differs from the demiurgic. Form is a specific thing, whereas being is more universal; thus, paternity is more universal and causal, ranking above the genus of the Demiurge, just as being is more universal than form.

**PROPOSITION 158.**

Every elevating cause <note>original: *causa anagogica*; a power that 'leads upward' from the material to the divine</note> among the Gods differs from both the purifying cause and the classes of causes that convert things back to their source.

**DEMONSTRATION.**

It is clear that this must also exist primarily in the Gods, since the causes of all universal goods pre-exist there. But the elevating cause pre-exists before the purifying cause, because the latter separates a thing from its inferiors, whereas the former joins it to its
                                        M      supe- <note>The text breaks here; 'supe-' likely completes as 'superioribus' or 'superiors' on the next page</note>

For from the first of beings, all secondary beings have acquired their essence.

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration of the hierarchy of divine principles, specifically focusing on the relationship between Being, the Intellect, and the divine unities (Henads). It follows the previous discussion of how the "One" interacts with the "Many."</meta>

### ELEMENTS OF THEOLOGY. 43

#### PROOF.

Since "Being-as-being" <term>enter ens: a phrase meaning "truly being" or "essential existence," referring to the highest level of reality that can be defined as "existence."</term> is the first of those things that participate in the divine union, as has been shown, it also fills the Intellect from itself. For the Intellect is a being; and as something filled by Being, it is indeed divine and **intelligible** <term>intelligible: that which can be grasped by the mind; the object of pure thought.</term>. As something deified, it is divine; but as something that fills the Intellect and is shared (participated) by it, it is intelligible. 

And while the Intellect is a being because of that which is "First Being," First Being itself is separate from the Intellect, because the Intellect follows after Being in the order of reality. **Unparticipated things** <note>original: imparticipabilia. These are primary principles that exist independently and are not "shared out" among lower things, maintaining their purity.</note> exist before those that are shared. Thus, Being, which is paired with the Intellect, also exists beforehand in its own right as an unparticipated being. For the intelligible exists not as something equal to the Intellect, but as something that completes the Intellect from a higher position, since it also grants existence to the Intellect and fills it with essential being. <margin>Circular stamp mark</margin>

#### PROPOSITION. CLXII.

**Every multitude of unities** <note>These "unities" or "Henads" are the highest divine powers, existing even above the level of Being.</note> **that illuminates "Being-as-being" is secret and intelligible. Secret insofar as it is joined to the One, but intelligible insofar as it is shared by Being.**

#### PROOF.

For all Gods are named after the things that depend upon them, since it is possible to know their different essences through these dependent things, even if the Gods themselves are unknown to us. For every divine thing is inexpressible and unknown in itself, as it is born from the one inexpressible source. However, through the various ways that lower things participate in them, it is possible to understand their specific characteristics. 

Indeed, those Gods who illuminate "Being-as-being" are called **intelligible**. Because "Being-as-being" is intelligible, divine, and unparticipated, it exists before the Intellect. This reality would not depend on the very first Gods unless they also possessed a primary essence and the power to bring other Gods to perfection. Indeed, as the things that participate in the divine relate to one another, so too do the essences of the divine things being participated in.

#### PROPOSITION. CLXIII.

**Every multitude of unities participated in by the unparticipated Mind is intellectual.** <term>intellectualis: Pertaining to the realm of the Intellect or pure, active thought, which is one step below the "Intelligible" level.</term>

M 2 **PROOF—**

<summary>This page describes the hierarchy of divine emanations, explaining how "Being" exists above the "Intellect" and how the highest divine unities (Henads) are known through the things they sustain. It establishes that the "Intelligible" level of reality precedes and completes the "Intellectual" level.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Neoplatonism, Henads, Being-as-being, Intellect, Intelligible, Participation, Divine Hierarchy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Proclus's "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the hierarchy of divine "Unities" (Henads) and how they relate to the soul and the physical world.</meta>

->### THE PROOF OF PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Diadochus" or "Successor" as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. This text is from his "Elements of Theology."</note>

For just as the intellect relates to that which truly exists <note>original: "ente ens" — a philosophical term for "beingly being" or absolute reality</note>, so the unities themselves relate to the intelligible unities. Therefore, those unities which illuminate the divine and unparticipated <term>unparticipated: a source that remains pure and does not descend into or mix with the things it creates</term> intellect are themselves intellectual; however, they are not intellectual in the sense that they exist within the intellect, but rather as pre-existing causes of the intellect and the progenitors of the intellect.

### PROPOSITION 164.
Every multitude of unities participated in by the unparticipated soul is super-mundane <term>super-mundane: existing "above the world" or beyond the physical cosmos</term>.

### PROOF.
Since the unparticipated soul is the primary thing above the world, and the Gods who are participated in by it are super-mundane, they maintain the same relationship toward intellectual and intelligible beings as the soul does toward the intellect, and as the intellect does toward that which truly exists. Therefore, just as every soul is directed toward the intellect, and the intellect is turned toward the intelligible, so surely do the super-mundane Gods excel the intellectual beings, just as these [intellectuals] excel the intelligibles.

### PROPOSITION 165.
Every multitude of unities participated in by any sensible body is mundane <term>mundane: belonging to the world/cosmos (from the Latin 'mundus')</term>.

### PROOF.
For [the divinity] illuminates the parts of the world through the mediation of the intellect and the soul; for intellect is not present in any mundane body without a soul. Nor is the Deity joined immediately <note>meaning: without an intermediary</note> even to the soul, for participations occur through things that are similar. Even the intellect itself—according to its own intelligible and highest part—is a participant in unity. Therefore, mundane unities are those that complete the whole world and act as the "deifiers" <note>original: "deificatrices" — powers that impart a divine quality</note> of visible bodies. For each of these [bodies] is also divine, though not by virtue of the soul (for the soul is not the primary God), nor even by virtue of the intellect (for the intellect is not identical to the One). Rather, the body is indeed animated and self-moving by virtue of the soul. But always in the 
                                                                                                same <note>This word "eodem" is a catchword, repeated from the start of the next page to ensure the binder placed them in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Proclus explains the hierarchy of "Unities" or "Henads" (divine ones), distinguishing between those that exist above the physical world and those that inhabit and "deify" the physical cosmos.</summary>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the hierarchy of the Intellect (Nous) and its relationship to souls and self-knowledge.</meta>

# THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 44

...proceeding in the same manner and in the most excellent order, it is carried through the intellect; the divine, however, is carried through union, and if it possesses providential power, it is for this reason such as it is.

## PROPOSITION 166.

**Every intellect is either unparticipated or participated. And if it is participated, it is participated either by supramundane souls or by mundane souls.** <term>Unparticipated (imparticipabilis)</term> <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, an "unparticipated" entity is a pure source that remains distinct and separate, while a "participated" entity is the version of that principle that actually enters into and organizes a lower level of reality, such as a soul.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For an unparticipated intellect presides over every multitude of intellects, having the most primary existence. Of those that are participated, some illuminate the <term>supramundane</term> <note>Existing above or beyond the physical universe.</note> and unparticipated soul, while others illuminate the <term>mundane</term> <note>Belonging to the physical, material world.</note> soul. For a mundane multitude does not arise immediately from the unparticipated source, since progressions in reality occur through similar things. That which is separated from the world is more similar to the unparticipated than that which is divided throughout the world. Nor does the supramundane multitude exist alone, but there are also mundane intellects. Indeed, there is a mundane multitude of Gods, and the world itself is simultaneously animated and endowed with mind; and the participation of mundane souls in the supramundane gods occurs through the intermediate mundane intellects.

## PROPOSITION 167.

**Every intellect understands itself. But the most primary intellect understands only itself, and being one in number, in this it is both the thinking subject and the object of thought. Each succeeding intellect understands simultaneously itself and those things which are before it; and the "intelligible" for such an intellect is partly that which it is, and partly that from which it originates.** <term>Intelligible (intelligibile)</term> <note>The 'intelligible' refers to the object of the intellect's thought—the pure forms or truths it contemplates.</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For every intellect understands either itself, or that which is above it, or that which is after it. But if it understands that which is after it, it will be turned toward that which is inferior; yet since it is an intellect, it would not even then truly know that toward which it has turned. For it would not be within itself, but outside itself; it would only possess a "type" <note>original: "typum"; here meaning a mere impression or reflection.</note> of the object within itself, which was made in it by that object. For it knows what it possesses and what it experiences, not what it does not possess and from which it does not experience. But if it understands that which is above itself through the knowledge of itself, it understands itself and that thing simultaneously...

<summary>This page describes the hierarchy of the Intellect, distinguishing between those that remain transcendent (unparticipated) and those that interact with the world (mundane), while asserting that all intellects possess self-knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Proclus, Intellect, Unparticipated, Supramundane, Mundane, Self-knowledge, Intelligible, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Proposition 167 from Proclus’s *Elements of Theology*, focusing on how a divine Mind (Intellect) achieves self-knowledge by knowing its own cause.</meta>

-># PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-

...he will know; but if he knows only that <note>Meaning his cause or origin</note>, he will be ignorant of himself, even though he is an intellect. In every case, however, by knowing that which exists before him, he also knows that it is a cause, and what things it is the cause of. For if he is ignorant of these, he will also be ignorant of that which he produces by his very being—what things he produces and the nature of those things. But that which someone produces, and what things he is the cause of, he knows by the fact that he knows what is before him, and thus he will know himself as having been produced from that source. In every case, therefore, by knowing what is before him, he will also know himself. 

If, therefore, there is a certain <term>intelligible intellect</term> <note>original: "intellectus intelligibilis"; a level of reality that is both a thinking subject and an object of thought</note>, that intellect, in knowing himself, also knows the "intelligible," being himself an intelligible thing. But each of those who come after that first stage understands the intelligible that is within himself and, at the same time, that which is prior to himself. Therefore, there is an "intelligible" within the intellect, and an "intellect" within the intelligible. But one is identical to the intelligible itself, while the other is identical to that which is within himself; however, he is not identical to that which is before him. For the "simply intelligible" <note>The pure object of thought at the highest level</note> is one thing, and the "intelligible within the one who understands" is another.

### PROPOSITION 168.

**Every intellect in activity knows that which it understands, and also knows that it is understanding; it is not the case that the capacity to understand belongs to one part, and the capacity to know that it is understanding belongs to another.**

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it is an intellect in activity and understands itself—not being something different from the object it understands—it knows itself and gazes upon itself. But in beholding the "one who is understanding," and in knowing that "one who is beholding," it knows that it is an intellect in activity. Knowing this, it knows that it is performing the act of understanding, and not only what it is understanding. Therefore, it knows both things at once: the <term>intelligible</term> <note>The object of its thought</note> and the fact that it is understanding that object, and it is understood by itself as the "one who understands," etc.

### PROPOSITION 169.

**Every intellect has its essence, its power, and its activity in <term>eternity</term>.** <note>original: "sempiternitate"; Proclus uses this to mean a state outside of linear time</note>

### DEMONSTRATION.

For if it understands itself, and the intellect is the same as the intelligible object, then the act of understanding (intellection) is the same as the intellect and the intelligible object. For since the act of understanding is the medium between the "one understanding" and the "thing understood," and these things are identical...

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic theory of self-knowledge, arguing that a divine Intellect knows itself by knowing its origin and that its essence, power, and activity exist simultaneously in eternity.</summary>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "Intellect" (the divine Mind) and its relationship to time and eternity, specifically concluding Proposition 169 and beginning Proposition 170.</meta>

**THEOLOGICAL ELEMENTS. 45**

...if these remain, then understanding will certainly be the same for both. But since the essence of this is eternal (for it is all at once), its understanding is likewise eternal, provided that it is the same as its essence. For if the Mind <note>original: *intellectus*; in Neoplatonist philosophy, this refers to the 'Nous', the second principle of reality which contains the archetypes of all things</note> is immobile, it certainly will not be measured by time, neither according to its being nor according to its action. Since these things are the same, its power is also eternal, etc.

**PROPOSITION 170.**

**E**very Mind understands all things at once. But the unparticipated <term>unparticipated: a term for a primary reality that remains pure and distinct, rather than being shared or "participated in" by lower levels of existence</term> Mind understands all things simply. Conversely, each of those Minds which follow after it understands all things according to their specific individual nature.

**DEMONSTRATION.**

**I**f every Mind has established its essence in eternity, and its action is simultaneous with its essence, it will understand all things at once. Indeed, to understand things individually, or at different times—one after another—is not characteristic of eternity. For that which happens in sequence belongs entirely to time. Priority and posteriority belong to things that happen successively, and not to the whole all at once. If, therefore, all Minds understood in the same way, they would not differ from one another. For if they understand all things in the same way, they are all the same, since they *are* what they understand. But if they were all identical, one would not be "unparticipated" while another is not. For those whose understandings are the same, their essences are also the same. Indeed, the understanding of each is the same as the being of each, and each thing is both: its understanding and its being. 

It remains, therefore, either that each Mind does not understand all things in the same way—but rather understands one thing, or many things, but not all—or that it understands all things according to its individual nature. But to say that a Mind does not understand all things would be to make it ignorant of some existing things. Furthermore, if it does not understand all at once but moves from one thought to another, even though it is supposed to be immobile, it would be like a soul <note>In this hierarchy, the Soul is "lower" than the Mind because the Soul moves through thoughts over time, whereas the Mind is supposed to grasp truth instantly</note>, and it would be inferior to that which understands all things in motion. If it understood only one thing because of its permanence, it would be limited. Therefore, it understands all things according to its individual nature. For it understands either all, or one, or all things specifically; the understanding of all things always exists in all Minds, yet all things are defined by one thing out of the whole. Thus, one thing becomes predominant in the understanding and in the objects understood, while all things are understood together through that one, with that one thing shaping all others to itself.
**PRO-**

<summary>This page argues that the divine Mind (Intellect) exists outside of time and therefore understands all truths simultaneously rather than one after another. It explains that while every individual Mind contains all knowledge, each one is distinguished by a specific primary focus that colors its perspective.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect, Nous, Eternity, Essence, Unparticipated, Proposition 170, Simultaneity, Neoplatonism, Proclus</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical progression of Proclus’s "Elements of Theology," specifically focusing on the nature of the Intellect (Nous) and its relationship to time, division, and causality.</meta>

-># PROCLUS DIADOCHUS<-
<note>Proclus (412–485 AD) was the "Successor" (Diadochus) as the head of the Platonic Academy in Athens. This text is from his *Elements of Theology*, a systematic defense of Neoplatonic metaphysics.</note>

### PROPOSITION 171.
**Every <term>intellect</term> is an indivisible essence.**
<note>In Neoplatonism, "Intellect" (Nous) refers to a divine level of reality that is higher than the human soul; it is the realm of pure, eternal thought and the Forms.</note>

#### DEMONSTRATION.
If it is without magnitude, incorporeal, and immobile, it is indivisible. For anything that is in any way divisible is divisible either by magnitude, or by <term>multitude</term> <note>A technical term for a collection of distinct parts or beings.</note>, or by actions that are spread out in time. But the Intellect is eternal in every respect, and exists beyond bodies, and the multitude within it is unified. Therefore, it is indivisible. The fact that the Intellect is indeed incorporeal is shown by its **conversion toward itself**.
<note>original: "conuersio eius ad se ipsum". This refers to self-awareness or self-reflection; Proclus argues that only an immaterial thing can fully "turn back" and know itself.</note>
For no body can turn back toward itself. Furthermore, that it is eternal is shown by the identity of its action with its essence; for this was demonstrated previously. That its multitude is unified is shown by the continuity of the intellectual multitude with the **divine unities**;
<note>original: "vnitates diuinas" or Henads. These are the highest principles in Proclus's system, sitting just below "The One."</note>
for these unities are the first multitude, and the Intellect comes after them. If, therefore, every intellect is also a multitude, it is nonetheless a unified multitude. For that which is compact and closer to the One precedes that which is divided.

### PROPOSITION 172.
**Every intellect is the immediate progenitor of things that are eternal and immutable according to their essence.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.
Everything produced by an immobile cause is itself immutable in its essence. But the Intellect is immobile—existing eternally, remaining in eternity, and producing what it produces by its very being. If it always is, and is always in the same way, then it always produces, and always in the same way. Therefore, it is not a cause of things that exist at one time but not at another; rather, it is a cause of things that exist forever.

### PROPOSITION 173.
**Every intellect is, in an intellectual manner, both the things that are before it and the things that are after it.**

#### DEMONSTRATION.
For it contains those things that are after it according to their **cause**, while it contains those things that are before it through **participation**.
<note>original: "per participationem". This is a key Platonic concept where a lower reality shares in the nature of a higher reality without being identical to it.</note>
The Intellect itself is the same [as its own contents], having been allotted an intellectual...
<note>The text cuts off here mid-sentence: "intellectualem sortitus est..."</note>

<summary>Proclus argues that the Intellect is an indivisible, eternal, and incorporeal entity that creates eternal things simply by existing. He establishes that the Intellect contains all reality—both its causes and its effects—within its own unified nature.</summary>

<keywords>Proclus, Intellect, Indivisibility, Incorporeal, Self-conversion, Henads, Causality, Participation, Neoplatonism, Eternity</keywords>
